### The "Human GPS": the skull's three axes
- **id:** human-gps-axes
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse maps the head and trunk as a three-axis "Human GPS": the sphenoid bone as the clock-setting switchboard, the notochord line from skull base to sacrum as the drain, and the heart's twist as the spin, all tuned by light, 40 Hz vibration and a faint timing signal from space.
- **protocols:** tuning-forks, mastic-gum
- **key points:**
  - Kruse describes a "Human GPS" built into the skeleton, with three axes: the sphenoid clock, the notochord drain line and the heart's twist.
  - In his model the sphenoid acts as a "symmetry selector," choosing light-based physics under sunlight and heavy, deuterium-loaded physics under man-made light and fields.
  - He says the human aqueduct's twist spins spinal fluid into a "super-fractionator" that flings heavy hydrogen out, which he links to becoming human.
  - All three axes, he argues, keep time to a faint hydrogen signal from space that the power grid and 5G now drown out.
  - His advice follows the axes: sunrise light, 40 Hz vibration, chewing, DHA and cholesterol from food, staying upright and grounding.

Kruse describes a navigation system built into the human skeleton, which he calls the "Human GPS." In his model it has three axes. The X-axis is the circadian compass housed in the sphenoid bone and the brain's master clock. The Y-axis is a vertical line running from the skull base down the spine, carrying fluid past the fourth ventricle and into the vagus nerve's drain. The Z-axis is the twisting vortex of the heart. He presents this map as the way people must understand their bodies to cope with the present shift in Earth's magnetic field.

#### Why he calls it a "Lagrangian"

In physics a Lagrangian is a single expression that sets energy of motion against stored energy; the whole Standard Model is written this way. Kruse stresses that the same equation yields different physics depending on which symmetry governs it, and that nature chooses its symmetries first and the forces follow.

He places the body's own "Lagrangian" where the sphenoid meets the notochord line at the clivus, the sloping bone of the skull base. This junction, in his account, converts sunlight into currents made by pressure, bending and heat in bone, into motion, and into self-sustaining waves, forms of power a magnetic field can steer.

The sphenoid, he says, acts as a "symmetry selector." Under sunlight and 40 Hz vibration it picks the light-based symmetry, and the pump that sorts heavy hydrogen keeps working. Under nnEMF (his term for man-made wireless and electrical fields) and artificial light it reads the environment as a heavy, high-gravity state he likens to the confinement of quarks, and deuterium (heavy hydrogen) piles up in the spinal fluid. Much of modern ill health, in his view, is the visible result of that environmental choice of symmetry.

#### The X-axis: the sphenoid switchboard

Kruse calls the sphenoid, the bat-shaped bone behind the eyes, the keystone that anchors the head's sensors. It cradles the pituitary gland and carries the optic and trigeminal nerves, which makes it, in his model, the hardware for both the brain's 40 Hz rhythm and the 380 nm violet signal of sunrise. Light reaching the bone charges its piezoelectric framework (bone that makes current when stressed) and so gives the pituitary its timing. Ultraviolet and near-infrared light set the clock by day, and the fading light of sunset trips the melatonin switch.

The arteries around the sphenoid form a "thermal manifold" that warms and thins blood water toward his structured "160" state (the high charge-holding state of cell water he contrasts with ordinary "78" water) before it enters the brain.

The thalamus, the relay and pacing center just above, completes this axis. Kruse wants it loaded with cholesterol and DHA, the long omega-3 fat, which in his model turn it into an induction coil that wraps the fluid chambers in a magnetic sheath, so the swirl of spinal fluid is steadied by Earth's field instead of turning turbulent.

Blue light and nnEMF jam the X-axis, and so does a weakening field once the swirl of spinal fluid through the cerebral aqueduct, the narrow channel between the third and fourth ventricles, loses its speed. A sphenoid knocked out of alignment, he adds, kinks the cranial nerves that pass through its openings, the vagus among them, so the brain can no longer sense how much deuterium it carries. Metabolism then stutters and the body's frame begins to sag.

#### The Y-axis: the notochord line

The notochord is the rod that defines the embryo's head-to-tail axis. Kruse traces how the clivus forms around its upper end, with the notochord signalling the surrounding tissue to lay down the cartilage of the skull base. Its leftovers in adults are the jelly-like cores of the spinal discs. He says these cores are the most water-rich tissue in the body, built to hold deuterium-depleted water as a liquid crystal at his 160 value. That makes the column a vertical waveguide along which protons can tunnel from brain to sacrum.

A 40 Hz tone, he says, sets the whole system ringing: the sphenoid, the temples and the disc cores. The vibration loosens deuterium and raises what he calls the thalamus's "antenna gain" for the Schumann resonance, Earth's natural electromagnetic hum.

Posture is part of the design. In apes, Kruse says, the aqueduct runs fairly straight. Walking upright and a shrinking braincase gave the human aqueduct a particular pitch and twist, so fluid passing through it spins as a double helix that he calls a "super-fractionator," flinging heavy hydrogen out of the stream. Upright posture is the gravity assist that lets this nozzle reach the speed needed for the Venturi effect (fluid speeding up as a channel narrows). In his account this is what lets human mitochondria run the ATP motor near 9,000 revolutions per minute where a chimpanzee's cannot, and he says it made us human. Short of DHA and cholesterol, or with a sphenoid left still by too little chewing, a person drops back into a heavier "primate" isotopic state and grows disconnected from other people.
The floor of the fourth ventricle and the inner ear are, in his model, the body's gravity sensors. Deuterium makes the fluid of the inner ear's balance canals heavier and thicker, so it trails behind each turn of the head, and he links this to unsteadiness and clumsiness.

The Y-axis also has a shortcut. Citing recent anatomy work, Kruse says heavy isotopes can drain from near the trigeminal nerve's main relay straight into nearby veins, and that vibration opens this drain. He calls the trigeminal and vagal routes a "dual exhaust" that works as a buddy system.

#### The Z-axis and the timing belt

The third axis is the heart. Kruse describes the heart's gravity well and spiral flow, together with the vortex in the fourth ventricle, as the spinners that throw deuterium out into the blood and spinal fluid, keeping the mitochondrial interior stocked with light hydrogen.
All three axes, he says, keep time to a faint signal from space. He reads a reported "21 Hz/cm" fingerprint as probably a harmonic of the 21-centimeter line, the radio signature of neutral hydrogen across the galaxy, and calls it the system's "timing belt" and "master metronome." As the solar system leaves the galactic hydrogen cloud that once surrounded it, this background signal fades, and the 60 Hz of the power grid and 5G drown it out like a whisper next to a jet engine, which is why he thinks nnEMF does so much harm now.

#### In practice

Kruse's advice follows the axes. Get sunrise and daytime ultraviolet and near-infrared light on the eyes to set the X-axis, and keep blue light and wireless fields out of the evening. Ring the sphenoid and spine with 40 Hz, through his tuning-fork routine or a hummed low "M," and keep the sphenoid moving by chewing, as in his mastic-gum practice. Keep enough DHA and cholesterol from food to load the thalamus, stay upright and on your feet, and ground so the vertical axis has somewhere to discharge.

- **sources:** [370317](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370317/), [370318](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370318/), [370590](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370590/), [370589](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370589/), [369873](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369873/), [369874](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369874/), [369875](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369875/), [369904](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369904/), [370219](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370219/), [370201](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370201/), [368766](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368766/), [368759](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368759/)

### The brain's exhaust valves: circumventricular organs, the ventricle cyclone and the bird's pecten
- **id:** brain-exhaust-valves
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse says the brain vents heavy water by day through spots that lack a blood–brain barrier, sorts it in fluid chambers shaped like a cyclone separator, and points to the bird eye's pecten as the same design taken to an extreme.
- **protocols:** vagus-nerve-stimulation
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says the brain vents heavy water by day through circumventricular organs, the few spots with no blood–brain barrier.
  - In his model the ventricles are shaped like a cyclone separator, spinning heavy fractions out of the fluid with gravity's help.
  - He sees the bird eye's pecten as the same design taken to an extreme: antenna, local hormone gland and deuterium exhaust.
  - He argues birds show that light and heavy-hydrogen removal, not insulin and leptin, sit at the core of metabolism.
  - His practical lessons are talking, singing and rhythmic breathing, daylight in the eyes, staying upright and moving, and salt-water grounding.

Kruse argues the brain does not wait for sleep to get rid of heavy water. In his model it vents deuterium (heavy hydrogen) through built-in relief valves during the day and sorts it in fluid chambers whose shape does the work. Birds, he says, show the same design in its most extreme form, in a structure inside the eye.

#### Relief valves in the barrier

The blood–brain barrier walls the brain off from the blood. Kruse sees it as a shield that keeps isotopic interference away from the brain's iron-based (Fe²⁺) core. The circumventricular organs, or CVOs, are the few places where that barrier is naturally missing, among them the subfornical organ, the area postrema and the median eminence. He calls them the brain's "hydraulic masterstroke," the relief valves of the shield — and also open doors: high-atomic-mass material that should never reach the hypothalamus or thalamus can enter here, and he warns that wireless earbuds and phones next to the head make that path worse. Clustered around the third and fourth ventricles, the CVOs let the brain dump the heavy water it makes while Complex IV, the last enzyme of the energy chain, is working flat out by day (he speaks of it "redlining" at 355%), without waiting for the glymphatic clean-out of sleep. The faint light the brain gives off during the day, its ultra-weak photon emission, is in his account the feedback that lets the conscious brain judge how much to vent. The inner ear adds another open path: a melanin sheet in the cochlea, tied to the endolymphatic sac, sits without a blood–brain barrier, so pulsed RF there, he says, can uncouple the head's GPS antenna.

He lists the sensors he thinks read the isotopic "heaviness" of the blood. They include tanycytes, the gatekeeper cells lining the third ventricle; heat- and sodium-sensing channels (TRPV1 and Nax) in the subfornical organ and its neighbor the OVLT; the tightly packed cells lining small vessels, which feel how thick the blood is and how strongly its particles repel one another; and glucose-sensing neurons that call for glucagon. When the load rises, he says, the body raises its metabolic "RPM": birds sing or migrate, and people talk or fall into rhythmic breathing.

These organs are ancient. Kruse notes that every vertebrate has CVOs, with the pineal and the hormone-releasing back lobe of the pituitary the most conserved. In birds the pineal still senses light directly, while the mammalian one only secretes. Birds also have extra CVOs that mammals lack, including a lateral septal organ and a lumbosacral organ in the lower spine that helps with balance.

#### Shape is the separator

Kruse believes the ventricles, the brain's fluid chambers, are shaped to act as a cyclone separator, the kind of device industry uses to spin heavy particles out of a moving stream. For that reason he says their shape is everything. A chamber that is too narrow cannot form a vortex, so deuterium settles out in still, layered flow and pressure climbs. One that is too wide swells like a backed-up sewer. The shape, in his model, mirrors the magnetic map the brain's mitochondria live in.

Gravity holds the separator steady. Without it, he says, the fourth ventricle can no longer keep heavy and light fractions apart, and fluid backs up into the sheath around the optic nerve and presses on the eye, which he gives as the cause of the eye changes astronauts develop in orbit. He assigns the exits of that chamber by isotope: the midline foramen of Magendie for light hydrogen and the lateral foramina of Luschka for deuterium, so a weak vortex dumps heavy water onto the floor's chemoreceptor zone and brings on nausea — his sign that the head's centrifuge has stalled.

#### The bird's pecten

The pecten oculi is a pleated, comb-like structure that projects into the bird eye, packed with blood vessels and pigment. Kruse says theropod dinosaurs and birds solved a puzzle for him, an idea he says came to him while standing before Michelangelo's David in Florence. The pecten has 14 to 15 folds (he cites the Baladi duck) capped by a bridge of melanin, and in his model it works as a topological antenna that traps biophotons and harvests both sunlight and magnetic flux. That, he argues, is how migrating birds can fly for long stretches without food or sleep. Sleep, to him, is mainly housekeeping that clears thermal noise, and the dielectric shield the pecten builds makes much of that unnecessary.

It also stands in for a gland. Birds lack the intermediate lobe of the pituitary, the part that makes alpha-MSH, the melanocortin hormone that in mammals drives pigment and energy use. Kruse says the melanocyte-rich, vascular pecten takes over that job locally, so together with the hypothalamus it lets the bird set its metabolism straight from the light entering its eye, without relying on leptin from fat or a slow central signal. It keeps the temperature and acidity of the vitreous, the gel filling the eye, steady so its water stays coherent. He calls it a "coherence pump" that lets birds "dissipate mass directly into time."

Its third job is exhaust. The bird's inner retina is thick, has no blood vessels and runs short of oxygen. The pecten pumps glucose in and lactate out, and in Kruse's model it is also the main exit for the deuterium that the retina's fast sugar-burning makes, sending it through the blood to the liver. As the eye flicks during saccades the pecten undulates, and he calls this a mechanical "Maxwell's demon" that stirs the vitreous toward the drain. Birds can therefore shed deuterium through their eyes and even shrink their guts on long flights; he links this to the Arctic tern, which he wrote about in his 2013 book, and to the godwit. Seabirds, he adds, ground their oxidative "exhaust" in salt water, which spares them the charge build-up he blames for false oxygen starvation and muscle fatigue.

#### Built for sugar

Kruse says birds are built to ignore insulin by design. He describes them as having lost GLUT4, the insulin-driven sugar gate, and the omentin gene, which he dates to oxygen drops around 320 million years ago. They have a liver glucagon receptor that is always switched on (reported in May 2025), five to ten times more of the glucagon-making alpha cells, and blood sugar that can run up to 700 mg/dL to feed the oxygen-starved retina through the pecten. Amacrine cells in the retina even make glucagon locally. To Kruse this shows that insulin and leptin are not the core of the story; light and the removal of heavy hydrogen are.

#### In practice

Kruse's lessons for people follow from the design. Keep the daytime vents working by talking, singing, humming and breathing in rhythm, his vagus-nerve-stimulation practice. Let real daylight into the eyes, since in his model light entering the eye is what steers metabolism. Stay upright and moving so gravity helps the fluid chambers spin, and use shoreline salt water to ground.

- **sources:** [369419](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369419/), [369420](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369420/), [369466](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369466/), [369467](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369467/), [370187](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370187/), [370188](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370188/), [369742](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369742/), [369482](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369482/), [369483](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369483/)

## Light

### Sunrise: dawn UV-A and the master clock
- **id:** sunrise-why
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse says dawn light — UV-A near 380 nm plus red and near-infrared, taken in through bare eyes and skin — is the master signal that sets the body's clocks and readies its energy machinery for the day.
- **protocols:** sunrise-horizon, morning-bowel-movement
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says first light at sunrise, UV-A near 380 nm plus red and near-infrared, is the master signal that sets every body clock.
  - In his model neuropsin in eyes and skin reads that UV-A, sets the daily NAD⁺ rhythm and tells mTOR which mode to run.
  - He says sunrise renews heme and melanin and drives the hormone cascade from POMC to alpha-MSH and ACTH.
  - He argues the body reads daylight as an ordered sentence, and mitochondrial energy output is gated by the clock that sunrise opens.
  - His routine is 30–45 minutes outside at sunrise, nothing over the eyes, skin uncovered and feet grounded.

Kruse argues that the most important light of the day is the first light at sunrise. In his model its UV-A (the gentle ultraviolet just beyond visible violet, which he pins near 380 nanometres), together with the red and near-infrared that dominate the low sun, is the master signal that tells every clock in the body what time it is. Because he treats mitochondria, the cell's power plants, as light-driven devices, missing sunrise means starting the day with the body's wiring out of step.

#### How light reaches the clock

Kruse says most of light's effect on the body has nothing to do with seeing. Citing the ophthalmologist Fritz Hollwich's 1948 work on the "energetic portion" of the visual pathway, along with Hufeland, Paracelsus and Franz Halberg's early circadian research, he describes an "optico-magnetic" route: light entering the eyes and skin steers the hypothalamus and pituitary, the brain's hormone control centre. The receivers are not the cells used for vision but other light-sensitive materials — neuropsin, melanopsin, melanin, cholesterol, water, heme and collagen.

Speed is part of his case. When a photon strikes retinal, the light-catching molecule inside an opsin, it changes shape in about 200 femtoseconds (millionths of a billionth of a second), which he reckons is some ten million times quicker than a nerve impulse. Flavins answer blue light and the energy chain's cytochromes answer red and near-infrared just as directly, so in his telling light has already altered the body's chemistry before nerves, hormones or metabolism have had time to respond.

Neuropsin (also called OPN5) is the one he puts first. It is a UV-A sensor in the eye and skin — in cornea and skin he treats it as the non-visual filter for the PER2 clock path — and in his account 380 nm light striking it, paired with near-infrared so melanin can form, sets the daily rhythm of NAD⁺, the molecule cells use to carry electrons and to run repair enzymes such as SIRT1. He also calls NAD⁺ a fluorophore tuned near 340 nm UV-A: that front-end light, with midday UV-B braking between Complexes III and IV, clears electron pile-ups before they become a Marcus crash. He calls 380 nm the "mTOR switch" because it tells mTOR, the cell's build-or-clean-up regulator, which mode to be in.

UV-A has a second target in the skin. Acting on tryptophan, an amino acid, it makes a compound called FICZ that switches on the aryl hydrocarbon receptor (AhR), which Kruse treats as another light antenna. Blue light and wireless radiation, he says, disorder the water around these molecules and blur that signal.

Kruse describes neuropsin as a "dual-axis transistor": it reads sunlight on one axis and Earth's magnetic field on the other. When the two agree — his "Zeeman handshake" — oxygen stays in its stable, safe form (triplet oxygen) and metabolism flows toward repair. When the field is disturbed or the light is artificial, he says the handshake fails and the same chemistry tips toward histamine, inflammation and singlet oxygen, oxygen's reactive and damaging form.

#### What sunrise renews

In his model, sunrise UV-A "renovates" heme (the iron-holding core of hemoglobin and of the energy chain's cytochromes) and melanin. The mild stress of UV-A switches on POMC, a hormone precursor the body cuts into alpha-MSH, the signal that makes melanin; mitochondria make melatonin at the same time to buffer that stress. Because the first step of heme production happens inside mitochondria and depends on UV, he ties how well the body builds its blood pigment to full-spectrum sun. The same light, he adds, gives hair its colour.

Kruse calls hormones "light ferry boats": carriers of magnetic and photonic information, not just chemical messengers. Watching the sunrise while grounded, he says, switches on the MITF–AMPAR pathway (the circuit that keeps a person pigmented) and builds an "internal tan" of biophotons, the faint light cells give off. That light helps cut POMC into ACTH, the pituitary hormone that drives the adrenal glands, and alpha-MSH. ACTH then carries the signal to the adrenal cortex. With enough sun, he argues, the message arrives so cleanly that the adrenals need to make less bulk cortisol.

Skin, he adds, can do much of this even with the eyes in darkness. Skin and brain grow from the same embryonic layer, the neuroectoderm, and share the POMC and MITF wiring, so he calls skin an "unfolded" brain and a body-sized solar panel. On his photorepair slide, UV-A reaching neuropsin in the skin refills NAD⁺ and resets the core clock genes, while 600–1000 nm infrared switches on AMPK, the cell's fuel gauge, and orders the water inside cells. Hormones released from sunlit skin then tell the brain the sun is up, which he says lets an over-alert brain stop straining to "listen harder."

Kruse also says the body reads sunlight as a sentence, in order. The blue pre-dawn hour is the preface that anchors the clock, the amber sunrise is the opening line, and dusk is the closing chapter that cues night-time melatonin and repair. A lone orange LED switched on at a random hour carries none of that meaning. Each sunrise, in his telling, reboots the whole system and reminds it that the sun is the one reference with no alternative; he puts the lifetime need at roughly 25,000 sunrises, about seventy years of mornings. He points to Egypt and the Maya at Tikal as cultures that organised their days around this sequence.

The first horizon light, he says, also triggers the cortisol awakening response (the natural morning rise in cortisol) and the glucagon gene network. Together these start the pancreas's roughly two-litre daily bicarbonate flush, which in his model sweeps deuterium (heavy hydrogen) out through the gut. Morning sun also lets the longevity gene FOXO3 apply its brakes on cell division, which he says protects the genome.

Mitochondria keep time too. Kruse cites Glen Jeffery's finding that ATP output runs about 40% higher in the morning, the energy-chain complexes peak in the afternoon, and cells lean on glycolysis (splitting sugar without oxygen) at night. To him this shows the energy machinery is gated by the clock, and sunrise is when the gate opens. The same morning light, he says, calms adrenaline, re-tightens collagen through nitric oxide and powers the liver's output of albumin, the blood protein that holds water inside vessels; people who block or skip morning light end up, in his account, with flattened cortisol rhythms and leaky vessels.

#### In practice

His sunrise-horizon routine is to be outside as the sun clears the horizon, using the first 60–90 minutes and staying 30–45 minutes. Nothing should sit between your eyes and the sun: no sunglasses, contacts, glasses or window glass. Uncover as much skin as you reasonably can and stand barefoot on surf, wet sand, dewy grass or basalt. He takes a well-formed stool (Bristol type 4) as the sign that the morning bicarbonate flush is working.

- **sources:** [374374](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374374/), [374377](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374377/), [383767](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383767/), [384649](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384649/), [383345](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383345/), [382109](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382109/), [377098](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377098/), [385066](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385066/), [375711](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375711/), [373674](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373674/), [373170](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373170/), [368923](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368923/)

### Midday UV-B and vitamin D
- **id:** solar-noon-vitamin-d
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** In Kruse's model, UV-B and the vitamin D it makes in skin are part of a coded light-and-magnetic-field system with a built-in safety cutoff that pills bypass, and the local field, latitude and season limit how much vitamin D sunlight can make.
- **protocols:** sun-exposure, pills-he-avoids
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says midday UV-B and skin-made vitamin D act as a safety brake on the mitochondrial energy chain, not a nutrient to top up.
  - In his model sun-made vitamin D has a built-in cutoff, and the local magnetic field decides how much D3 the skin can make.
  - He says latitude, season, skin cholesterol and deuterium load all limit what sunlight can produce.
  - He argues a pill delivers "the ink without the book": the blood marker rises while the light-driven steps never happen.
  - His routine is torso and back in solar-noon sun, no sunscreen or eyewear, building up gradually, grounded, and avoiding oral D3.

Kruse rejects the idea that vitamin D is just a nutrient to top up. In his view UV-B, the shorter and stronger ultraviolet of the midday sun, and the vitamin D it makes in skin form a self-regulating light system tied to the body clock and to the local magnetic field. That is why he wants vitamin D made in the skin, not swallowed.

#### UV-B as a safety brake

Kruse treats UV-B and UV-A as a pair of brakes on the mitochondrial energy chain. Vitamin D's receptor, which he places on the inner mitochondrial membrane, slows electron flow between Complexes III and IV, while UV-A releases nitric oxide, which briefly slows Complex IV. Together, he says, they keep the chain out of "Marcus inversion" — his term for electron flow that stalls when it is pushed too hard — whenever Earth's field is unstable. The slowing is the point, he says: sunlight paces energy production on purpose, so the aim is a steady, ordered chain, not a motor making as much ATP as it can.

That is why he calls UV-B "term life insurance" for anyone who is not "pinned" (living grounded and sunlit under a stable local magnetic field), and why he says it should not be feared. Citing Slominski, Holick and Reiter (2026), he treats vitamin D and the circadian clock as one coupled loop. Mitochondria, he adds, make melatonin in daytime using UV, so vitamin D and melatonin are twin products of the sun rather than day-and-night opposites.

He illustrates the brake with the elephant, whose unarmoured skin makes plenty of vitamin D; its receptor works like a clutch that keeps a large brain from overheating under the midday sun.

#### The built-in cutoff

UV-B turns a cholesterol precursor in the skin into vitamin D, but Kruse notes that only about a quarter of the yield becomes vitamin D3; the rest becomes lumisterol and tachysterol. He calls this a self-limiting "thermostat" that makes it impossible to overdose on sun-made vitamin D. In his account the UV-B hit creates a radical pair, two fragments each holding one unpaired electron. If their spins point opposite ways they recombine into D3; if they point the same way they break down into the cutoff products. The local magnetic field tips that balance.

Because the field chooses the path, he says the magnetism where you live caps how much vitamin D you can make. That is his explanation for levels that stay flat in California despite plenty of sun, and for a reader's stalled level in Florida, which sits close to the South Atlantic Anomaly, a large weak patch in Earth's field where, he says, tissue cannot complete the light-driven repair that lets levels climb.

The form of skin cholesterol matters too. In summer, sulfur-rich cholesterol carried in HDL is the "antenna" tuned to UV-B. Years of blue light and nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring) can freeze skin cholesterol in its electron-poor LDL form, his "Mismatch Freeze," so even naked equatorial sun finds nothing to work with. A cell loaded with deuterium (heavy hydrogen), he adds, lacks the energy to make or activate vitamin D at all, and the liver and kidneys can only turn it into its active form when they are themselves low in deuterium. In his reading, that is how light on the skin reaches the isotope balance of organs that never see daylight.

Latitude and season set limits as well. Above about 37°, Kruse says, skin makes little vitamin D for two to eight months of the year, and in winter the low sun stops it whatever a person's ancestry. Darker skin needs more UV to make the same amount, which he says is why the stronger UV of altitude helps melanin-rich people. In a mid-latitude summer, regular time outdoors supplies enough UV-B.

#### More sun is not always better

Strong UV without grounding and infrared can itself stall the energy chain, so Kruse reads a sunburn as proof the person was not pinned enough, and calls burning on purpose foolish. Citing Fosbury's maps of US deaths, he notes that death rates peak one to three weeks after the winter solstice and bottom out after the summer solstice; he blames low light combined with an unstable field.

#### Why the sun feels good

Strong UV on skin cells, in Kruse's account, switches on POMC, a hormone precursor, right in the skin. POMC is cut into alpha-MSH, the tanning signal, and beta-endorphin, the body's own opioid, which reaches the brain's reward centres and releases dopamine. Sun is meant to feel rewarding, he says, and a life without it lowers beta-endorphin, bringing flatter mood and more pain.

He ties thinking to the same arc. The brain's sharpest window, in his view, mirrors solar noon. People with a steady lifelong history of daytime light, he says, think faster and age more slowly mentally, while light at night lowers dopamine, melatonin and GABA, the brain's main calming messenger.

#### Sunlight as a coded signal

Kruse describes the sun as a pulsed, filtered, coded power supply rather than raw energy. When oxygen rose in Earth's early atmosphere, the ozone layer that formed began carving a pattern into sunlight. The dark Fraunhofer lines in the solar spectrum act, in his telling, like a barcode, and ozone casts a "shadow" across the yellow-orange. He calls this pattern a "photonic grammar" and says it is what lets the vitamin D receptor work as a metabolic brake. Melanin, in his model, is the software that reads the code.

#### Why pills fall short

An oral pill delivers pure D3 with none of the cutoff products and none of the light-run steps; in his phrase, it gives the body "the ink without the book." Kruse cites an observational study of more than 5,000 older adults that linked routine supplementation in people with normal levels to slightly faster slipping in thinking skills. In his reading the extra vitamin D acts as an "atomic impurity" in an ungrounded brain under artificial light.

He reads the flat results of two large supplement trials, VITAL and ViDA, the same way: a pill cannot repair a shortfall that is physical rather than chemical. Sun on skin, he says, drives a current and lifts the cell's water toward its structured state (his "160"). A pill raises 25-hydroxyvitamin D, the blood marker doctors test, while delivering no photons and none of the infrared that thins the blood, so the lab value looks fine while the body stays locked up; he likens it to photoshopping a bank balance. He treats a low blood level as a dashboard gauge reporting high deuterium and weak magnetic drive, which a pill only paints over.

#### In practice

His sun-exposure routine: near solar noon, when your shadow is shorter than you, expose your torso and back, with no sunscreen, sunglasses, contacts or glasses. Build up gradually and move to shade at the first warmth on the skin. Give UV-B priority if you are not pinned and around the winter solstice, and stay grounded while you do it. Oral vitamin D3, especially when levels are already normal, is on his list of pills to avoid.

- **sources:** [385033](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385033/), [383426](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383426/), [383708](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383708/), [381795](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381795/), [382564](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382564/), [382570](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382570/), [376501](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/376501/), [380239](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380239/), [373578](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373578/), [370216](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370216/), [373131](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373131/), [388036](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/388036/)

### The four-phase sun day and the "solar callus"
- **id:** solar-callus
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse's account of how a person, especially one from a northern maternal lineage, builds tolerance to strong sun by stepping through the day's wavelengths from sunrise to sunset, and why place and altitude change the pace.
- **protocols:** four-phase-sun-day, sunset-viewing
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says a "solar callus," skin that tans rather than burns, is built gradually through daily sun rather than inherited.
  - In his model northern maternal lineages have "leaky" mitochondria that need slow arrival and grounding to re-couple in the tropics.
  - He says the day's light should be taken in order: red and infrared at sunrise, UV-A mid-morning, brief UV-B at noon, infrared at sunset.
  - He reads early-season skin reactions as deuterium stored over winter, so build-up must be slow and burning avoided.
  - He says sunscreen, sunglasses and contacts block the melanin signal, while altitude and place change how fast the callus forms.

Kruse uses "solar callus" for skin and tissue that have adapted to strong sun, so they tan rather than burn. He argues the callus is built gradually, not inherited, and that how it is built matters most for someone whose ancestors evolved far from the equator and who moves to the tropics, as he did when he moved to El Salvador.

#### Lineage and pace

He explains this through haplogroups, the maternal lineages traced through mitochondrial DNA. His own, H, is northern European, and he describes its mitochondria as naturally "leaky": built to shed heat rather than to couple tightly to energy production. Plunged suddenly into tropical UV, he says, such mitochondria lose their coherence. An H-lineage person who flies straight from northern Europe to San Salvador is, in his view, in a very different position from one who arrives slowly.

Mitochondrial DNA does not change, and a northern lineage does not become a tropical one. But Kruse says epigenetic flexibility plus local grounding — bare feet on basalt or wet black volcanic sand, which he says carry a strong horizontal magnetic field — lets the person "re-couple." The mitochondria become better insulated, leak less, and keep the blood and spinal-fluid flows that clear deuterium (heavy hydrogen) working. He calls this a re-coupled relocation. At high latitudes, by contrast, he notes that no one can hold a tan all twelve months, so a year-round natural callus is out of reach there.

#### Place and altitude

Where you stand also changes how fast the callus forms. A reader in Australia noticed he tanned far faster at sea level than at home, about 700 metres up. Kruse's explanation is that at altitude the magnetic field is weaker and noisier, closer to the radiation belts, so MITF, the master switch of pigment cells, stays in a repair-and-survive mode, spending its effort quenching damage instead of laying down pigment. At sea level the thicker atmosphere and stronger field give the melanin-making pathway the steady conditions it needs to switch into building mode, and the tan comes quickly. A tan, he adds, reports only on the skin, not on the state inside the body.

#### Four phases of light

He built his own callus by treating each day as a training program through the solar spectrum, in order. Sunrise red and near-infrared expand structured water in tissue (water ordered into a charge-holding gel layer) and prepare cells to handle the UV that comes later. Mid-morning UV-A on bare eyes signals the brain, through the POMC hormone pathway, to lay down melanin, which he treats as the skin's spin filter. A brief midday UV-B dose makes vitamin D and sulfur-rich cholesterol and, together with the new melanin, completes the callus. The last daylight hour's red and infrared then repair the day's oxidative damage and prepare the body to make melatonin. In his model the infrared at both ends prepares and repairs the tissue around the UV-heavy middle.

He finds support for those infrared bookends in Chossat's 1843 starvation experiments, as retold by Herbert Shelton. Animals close to death from hunger revived with outside heat alone, and died if the heat was taken away before their food was digested. Kruse reads this as infrared re-expanding structured water and restarting the ATP motor without food, which for him makes food a battery extender rather than the main power supply.

#### Why the build-up must be slow

Kruse uses the same model to explain why the first strong sun of spring can bring an itchy, burning rash that fades as summer goes on. Over a dark, sedentary winter, he says, deuterium collects in skin that stays covered, such as the chest and arms. When intense UV strikes skin loaded with heavy water and metals, deuterium cannot vibrate or tunnel fast enough to shed the energy, so it piles up as a local heat spike, and the rash is the immune system clearing what the UV has just "cooked." As summer UV clears the surface and the body's fluid flows speed up, the skin "hardens."

Someone whose tissues are loaded while their blood is clean, he adds, will feel up and down in the first weeks of long sun days, so an erratic early response is expected. For very sensitive people he stages the light: infrared first, and UV-A only once the melanin shield and the insulating sheaths around nerves have been rebuilt, since UV-A switched on too early overwhelms the system. His gates for moving on are a flashlight shone on the teeth, which he reads for clarity, and a crisp pupil reflex.

#### Redness and filters

Redness, Kruse says, means the membrane's electrical containment has broken down, so his rule is never to burn. Sunscreen blocks the UV-B and UV-A needed for vitamin D and melanin, and in his telling also blocks the magnetic job of skin melanin and cholesterol so oxygen tips toward its reactive form. Sunglasses make the brain read cloud or darkness, so the melanin signal never starts and the person burns quickly at midday; contacts interfere in the same way. He names DHA from wild seafood as the fat that turns sunlight into current in tissue, and says saffron speeds the callus: its carotenoids crocin and crocetin, its aroma compound safranal and the flavonol kaempferol.

#### Skin colour as a readout

In Kruse's view pale skin is not a sign of health but an atrophied organ, made pale by sunscreen, sunglasses, indoor life or drugs. Near-infrared sun draws blood to the skin and boosts red-cell production, and strong UV keeps blood oxygen in its stable form, turning blood deep red. Sun-flushed skin is, for him, a visible readout of redox health that other species also use to choose mates (he cites gelada baboons and birds). He retells accounts, which he credits to Lavoisier, of ship surgeons near the equator mistaking bright-scarlet venous blood for arterial blood.

#### In practice

His four-phase sun day: at sunrise, 30–45 minutes of red and near-infrared on bare eyes and as much skin as possible, feet on basalt or wet black sand; mid-morning (about 8:30–11:00), 15–25 minutes of UV-A on eyes and skin while moving, with no eyewear; at midday, when your shadow is shorter than you, 3–10 minutes of UV-B on torso and back while grounded, starting at 3 minutes and adding a minute every few days as you tan, and moving to shade at the first warmth. In the last daylight hour, spend 20–30 minutes outside with bare skin and no sunglasses to wind down (his sunset-viewing practice). Keep artificial night light and out-of-season food out of the picture, and if you are relocating to the tropics, arrive gradually.

- **sources:** [382093](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382093/), [382091](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382091/), [382089](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382089/), [380737](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380737/), [376133](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/376133/), [372473](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372473/), [368745](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368745/), [370158](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370158/), [369100](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369100/), [369530](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369530/)

### Red and near-infrared: the sun first
- **id:** red-nir-light
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse values red and near-infrared light highly — it rebuilds cell water, restarts the energy chain and lowers blood sugar — but says it cannot fix an unpinned body on its own, and that the sun is its only complete source.
- **protocols:** red-nir-light
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says red and near-infrared, about 43% of sunlight, rebuild cell water, free Complex IV and spin ATP synthase on light hydrogen.
  - In his model it works best at dawn, needs a full NAD⁺ pool, and pairs with UV-A in a push-pull over nitric oxide.
  - He says modern LED-lit life leaves people short of near-infrared, an "electronic winter" at the level of the cell.
  - He argues near-infrared "lubricates rather than drives": without a stable magnetic field and UV, it can deepen the jam.
  - His guidance puts the sun first, treats panels as secondary, and avoids pairing methylene blue with red light unless grounded.

Red and near-infrared light (NIR, invisible light just past red, roughly 600–1000 nm) hold a central place in Kruse's model. About 43% of sunlight falls in this band, he says, and it reaches deep into the body to power mitochondria. But he insists it is only one part of what sunlight provides, and that it only works fully in a body that is "pinned": grounded and sunlit under a stable local magnetic field.

#### What red and infrared do

Building on Peter Mitchell's account of how mitochondria make energy, Kruse calls red light the sun's "command channel" for light hydrogen (ordinary hydrogen, as opposed to heavy deuterium). Red light, he says, penetrates the abdomen to the liver's mitochondria and spins ATP synthase, the rotary motor that makes the cell's energy currency, at full efficiency on light hydrogen. The liver then needs less food fuel and keeps deuterium out; he calls this the "43% red light engine."

He puts the energy barrier for smooth electron and proton flow down the energy chain at about 0.66 electron-volts. He places photons of that energy near 1878 nm, past the usual near-infrared band, and traces them to the negative hydrogen ion in the sun's atmosphere, which he says pours out a continuous infrared stream there. In his view the stream only becomes biologically useful once it has passed through Earth's magnetic shield and reached the body's water.

That energy, he says, accelerates the hand-off of electrons from NADH, the loaded form of the carrier NAD⁺, into the energy chain, turning it back into NAD⁺. When the chain stalls, NAD⁺ falls. The size of the NAD⁺ pool also decides what red light does: when it is low, 670 nm light has nowhere to land and ends up as heat, a "nanoscopic sunburn," while a large pool lets the same pulse become the membrane's electric field.

NIR's best-known job in his model is at Complex IV, the last step of the energy chain, where oxygen is turned into water. Nitric oxide can sit on Complex IV's copper–iron centre and block it. Morning NIR breaks that bond, his "photonic purge," so oxygen can enter. Without it, he says, electrons pile up, oxygen flips into its reactive singlet form, and the cell can no longer make its own deuterium-depleted water. Until Complex IV is running and the cell's water has changed, he says, no supplement works.

He pairs this with UV-A in a push-pull. UV-A frees the nitric oxide stored in skin and blood, relaxing vessel walls and making them slick; he notes the body's nitric oxide falls by about 65% between the twenties and the sixties. But that same nitric oxide competes with oxygen at Complex IV, so near-infrared is needed to lift it off again, restoring ATP and the metabolic water that keeps elastin, the springy protein of artery walls, hydrated. Without enough infrared, he says, elastin dries and calcium binds to its fibres.

Order matters. Kruse says red and near-infrared first rebuild exclusion-zone water, the ordered, charge-holding layer water forms beside surfaces, and only once that layer is restored can UV drive the orderly spin changes the energy chain depends on. That is why he puts red and infrared at both ends of the sun day.

Timing matters as well. Citing Glen Jeffery's work, Kruse says 670 nm light lifted mitochondrial output only early in the day and did nothing in the afternoon, when cells are already at full load. He treats mitochondria as clocks that need a dawn reset pulse, so red light from a device late in the day is lost as heat. In nature, infrared peaks relative to UV at dawn and dusk, when he says it thins the water in blood and spinal fluid; window glass filters it, so people indoors miss those transitions.

Red light also acts on blood sugar. Citing work with 670 nm light, he says blood glucose fell by 27% with no insulin, while blue light pushes glucose up as the system stalls to avoid overheating. Lowering blood sugar, in his view, means fixing the light and clearing deuterium.

#### The near-infrared shortfall

Kruse describes a modern lack of 600–1000 nm light. An ancestor sitting by a wood fire, in the sun and grounded to a strong local field, was bathed in it. Modern people live under LEDs that emit almost none, and the phase-out of incandescent bulbs removed the last indoor source. He calls the result an "electronic winter": having traded the sun's water-thinning infrared for cold blue light, people are chilled at the level of the cell whatever the thermostat says.

#### Why NIR alone is not enough

Yet Kruse says NIR "lubricates rather than drives." It thins the water around ATP synthase and speeds Complex IV, but it does nothing to align electron spins, the job his model gives to the magnetic field. If Complexes I–III upstream are already in Marcus inversion from a weak field or nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring), spinning ATP synthase harder only pulls more electrons through the crash; he likens it to sending more cars onto a NASCAR pile-up. Red or near-infrared on its own is, in his words, a half truth, like "pressing the gas pedal with no brakes": UV-A releases nitric oxide that briefly slows Complex IV, and UV-B via the vitamin D receptor steadies the hand-off between Complexes III and IV. He adds that neuropsin, the body's UV-A sensor, needs UV-A and near-infrared together to keep the clock running.

Devices have further limits. Most panels emit a few narrow bands that stop at 850 or 940 nm, well short of the 1878 nm band; their light is far more uniform than sunlight's broad, scrambled mix; and even "flicker-free" drivers add electrical noise that the cell must spend energy sorting. A costly panel, he says, can leave someone merely better lit while their deuterium load stays put.

For people who are unpinned he goes further. When oxygen is in its singlet state and NAD⁺ is loaded with deuterium, he says metabolism becomes blind to light, so magnetism matters more than light, a catch-22 he calls rare in human history. Such a person can lack near-infrared while standing naked in the sun. The precondition for using light is what he calls a "paramagnetic lock": membranes, cholesterol rafts and structured water acting together as an ordered antenna that keeps oxygen in its stable form.

He also warns against a popular combination. Methylene blue shuttles electrons past Complexes I and III, and 670 or 830 nm light excites Complex IV; in an ungrounded person living under wireless and blue light, he likens the pairing to a turbocharger on a short-circuiting transformer.

#### In practice

His red-nir-light guidance puts the sun first: get red and infrared from sunrise and daylight together with UV-A and UV-B, and if you have hours of sun, a panel is redundant. Don't rely on single-wavelength devices, which he says can harm; where he does use panels, they are 660 and 850 nm, and never as a substitute for the UV brakes. He prefers grounding plus full solar light to panels, and says not to combine methylene blue with red light unless grounded and away from wireless and blue light.

- **sources:** [381022](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381022/), [385031](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385031/), [382566](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382566/), [382570](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382570/), [381027](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381027/), [383821](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383821/), [368748](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368748/), [374624](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374624/), [371039](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371039/), [371743](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371743/), [370865](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370865/), [388032](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/388032/)

### Melanin: the body's solar semiconductor
- **id:** melanin-semiconductor
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse treats melanin as a light-driven semiconductor, spin filter and metal and deuterium trap, wired to the brain through a two-way skin–brain loop, that daily UV-A, a stable magnetic field and well-hydrated cells must renew.
- **protocols:** sun-exposure, hair-care
- **key points:**
  - Kruse calls melanin a biological semiconductor that splits water with light, feeding cells a current that does not depend on food.
  - In his model it filters electron spin in skin and arteries, traps metals and deuterium, absorbs radiation, and can migrate when voltage and sun allow.
  - He says a two-way skin–brain loop through MITF and AMPA receptors lets sunlit skin tell the brain the sun is up.
  - He argues sunglasses, sunscreen, hats, tattoos, hair dye and lotions block or smother the signals melanin needs to charge.
  - His main lever is regular unfiltered sun, sunrise UV-A, grounding, and at most tallow or coconut oil on the skin.

For Kruse, melanin is far more than skin pigment. Found in skin, eyes, brain, the nerves around the heart, fat and fascia, it is in his model a "biological quantum semiconductor," a material that turns light into electrical current, and a vault for metals. Keeping it renewed with daily sunlight is central to his approach.

#### What melanin does

Kruse compares melanin to chlorophyll. It absorbs a broad range of light, especially UV and visible, and uses it to split water into hydrogen, oxygen and free electrons. That continual splitting feeds a "wireless" current into cells that does not depend on food. Working with structured water (water ordered into a charge-holding layer), he says, melanin also sorts light hydrogen from deuterium, its heavy form.

That sorting has a cost. Splitting deuterium-heavy water takes eight to ten times more energy, so an overloaded melanin stalls, bleaches or browns. He calls melanin a sacrificial sink: it soaks up deuterium so the heavy hydrogen never reaches the mitochondrial motor, and it wears out doing so. Its one-handed (chiral) structure also lets it pass on only electrons with the right spin and soak up almost any frequency of electromagnetic noise before it reaches mitochondria.

Melanin is also a trap, a buffer and a radiation sink. It grabs free iron and copper, which in his account stops iron-driven radical chemistry and singlet-oxygen formation at the source. It buffers protons, magnesium and calcium like a capacitor. He reads high heavy-metal levels as a sign that a person's internal melanin is failing, which makes them a sun and grounding problem. He stresses that melanin absorbs huge amounts of electromagnetic energy, which is why he wants people outdoors in strong sun, "like a rotisserie chicken," especially in the tropics, to keep that shield charged.

Neural-crest melanin also lines arteries on an elastin and fibrillin scaffold, where he says it keeps deuterium in the blood rather than the cell. When that filter fails — too little sun or grounding, too much blue light — calcium settles in vessel walls and teeth yellow. Eumelanin versus pheomelanin is largely sulfur chemistry set by triplet oxygen; under a weak field or the South Atlantic Anomaly, people can lose the ability to melanate even in equatorial sun. Adult neural-crest cells still migrate along collagen and elastin, so melanin can move — explorers lighten in the Arctic and darken at the equator, and an ungrounded plane flight can shed a tan in hours. Interior mitochondria "tan" when membrane voltage is high enough for cells' own UV glow to drive POMC.

Holding metals is how melanin turns information into chemistry. When it absorbs a biophoton (the faint light cells give off) or a particular radio frequency, Kruse says, it changes shape, squeezing or loosening the metal it holds and so reshaping the enzyme attached to it: light becomes metabolism with no gene in between. When a reader worried about low copper, his answer was that melanin controls the metals, and sun and grounding restore the controller.

#### The interior tan

In the brain, Kruse calls neuromelanin bound to iron in the substantia nigra a solid-state battery that holds local fields steady so oxygen keeps its stable spin; nnEMF, blue light or dehydration deform it. Melanin in dark places like this and the inner ear harvests the faint light mitochondria give off. A skin tan shows only the outer shield; this "interior tan" matters more, and a deep skin tan can hide a "browned" interior.

#### Renewal, and how melanin "rusts"

Melanin must be renewed. Without 380 nm UV-A reaching neuropsin and without strong pinning (grounded and sunlit under a stable local field), the POMC pathway that yields alpha-MSH shuts down. The ageing polymer soaks up toxins and deuterium, its iron "rusts," and debris settles as lipofuscin, his "rusted melanin." Melanin can only shed UV safely when wet with the deuterium-depleted water Complex IV makes.

With UV-A and well-oxygenated blood, tyrosine becomes melanin; without them the pathway slides back toward tyrosine and phenylalanine, and stored melanin breaks down into catecholamines used as emergency "dopants." He reads low vitamin D and greying hair as the same circuit failing — and color can return when pinning returns, so pigment loss is not a one-way clock.

#### The skin–brain loop

Kruse's newest piece is what he calls the transdermal MITF–AMPAR loop. He points to research showing that human pigment cells carry working AMPA receptors, the fast receivers for glutamate, the brain's main "go" signal, and that blocking them sharply lowers MITF. He reads this as proof that the glutamate system running the brain also sets the charge of the melanin "battery," in skin and inside the head alike.

The loop also explains, in his view, what happens when light goes missing. The faint light mitochondria give off under load is meant to strike melanin, with AMPA receptors acting as its antennae. When that signal fails, through light starvation or wireless noise, the brain adds AMPA receptors to "listen harder" and slides into a noisy, over-excited state with foggy thinking. Sun on skin reverses this: hormones released through POMC tell the brain the sun is up, and receptor numbers can fall.
#### What blocks the signal

Blocking daylight, in his model, cuts the eye-to-skin signal that builds melanin. Sunglasses make the brain read darkness, so pigment is never ordered and the person burns at midday; contacts, sunscreen, face-shading hats and daytime blue blockers do the same. He treats sunscreen as a tarp over the skin's solar panel: it blocks melanin and cholesterol, tipping oxygen toward reactive singlet. Skin and brain share the neuroectoderm, so the tarp underfeeds the brain — worse at high latitude indoors. Tattoos, in the same spin story, ruin the chirality of that solar-semiconductor surface.

Hair, for Kruse, belongs to the same antenna: a melanin-filled light guide, not dead keratin. Dyeing or bleaching, he says, "shaves the antenna."

#### Melanin and the magnetic field

Melanin, like oxygen, is paramagnetic, so in Kruse's model making it inside the body depends on Earth's field; when the field weakens, internal melanin fails first. He tells people to live where they can make melanin, naming places with an erratic compass, such as Hawaii and New Zealand, as hard on it.

#### Skin water and lotions

Kruse treats dry skin as a light-clock failure, not a shortage of drinking water. Light at night disrupts the POMC signal, and the sweat glands stop building a film of structured, deuterium-depleted water on the skin. Skin, he says, is hydrated from the inside, by mitochondria making water. Lotions make matters worse: petroleum and silicone bases collapse skin water from its structured state (his "160") to ordinary water (his "78"), hidden UV filters breed singlet oxygen under LEDs, and any synthetic coat smothers the oxidising film the skin makes at its surface.

#### In practice

His sun-exposure routine is the main lever: regular unfiltered sun, with sunrise UV-A for renewal, and no sunscreen, sunglasses, contacts or blue blockers in daylight. For dry skin he uses morning sun, cold and grounding rather than lotion; after a shower he allows at most tallow or coconut oil, and never petroleum, silicone or UV-filter products. He sums the daily posture as "make like the Sphinx and eat like a great white shark": ground at sunrise, face the sun, and keep the plate on clean seafood rather than packaged fillers.

### Blue light, LEDs and screens
- **id:** blue-light-hygiene
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse blames blue-dominant indoor light and screens, stripped of red and infrared, for damage to the body clock, eyes and metabolism, and calls screen life "reality subtraction."
- **protocols:** blue-light-hygiene, screen-light-hygiene
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says LEDs and screens deliver blue light stripped of red and infrared, and in his view disturb the body more than food does.
  - In his model indoor blue light without magnetic "pinning" raises blood sugar and insulin and pushes deuterium into tissue.
  - He says wrong-way light makes singlet oxygen that damages the clock's physical hardware, starting with the eye.
  - He wants blue gone after sunset but never blocked in daylight, where blockers act on the clock "like a drug."
  - He calls screen life "reality subtraction" and says to swap in incandescent bulbs and keep screens away from workouts and bedrooms.

Kruse argues that modern indoor light is one of the biggest forces pulling the body out of order — in his view a bigger one than food. LEDs and screens deliver a spike of blue light with almost none of the red and infrared that come with natural light, and people take it in indoors, away from both the sun and the ground.

#### How lighting changed

He dates a turning point to efficient blue LEDs, whose inventors (Akasaki, Amano and Nakamura) won the 2014 physics Nobel. Built to emit only visible light, LEDs strip out the red and infrared that sunlight, firelight and incandescent bulbs supply — the longer wavelengths that, in Kruse's model, penetrate the body and power mitochondria. Phasing out incandescent bulbs removed the last indoor source of infrared and left a blue spike he says can drive the dopamine pathway once melanin breaks down and the blood–brain barrier is leaky. He likens running a brain on LEDs to powering a quantum computer from a flickering 5-volt supply instead of the sun's broad, steady battery. On stages and in conference halls he wants red light, not blue-heavy LED blasts.

Sunlight, he adds, comes from countless atoms and transitions at once, which makes it the most varied and least polarized light there is. Any lamp is a far smaller set of sources, which is why by day he sends people outdoors rather than to sun-imitating lamps.

#### What it does to the body

Indoor spaces, Kruse says, are both blue-dominant and "devoid of magnetic effects." That combination forces a spin change in the mitochondrial energy chain (intersystem crossing) that drives insulin spikes and dulls the body's response to insulin; he holds that light and missing magnetic "pinning" raise blood sugar and insulin more than any food. A burst of red light while grounded, he adds, can blunt a glucose and insulin spike.

Night-time blue is the worst case for blood sugar. In his account it strikes melanopsin in the lower retina, scrambles the hypothalamus's clock and throttles hypocretin, the brain's alertness signal, which he treats as a brake on heavy mass. Deuterium (heavy hydrogen) then floods tissue and cells call for more glucose.

Bad light, Kruse says, also changes where deuterium sits. In the blood it is fairly harmless and mobile, and the pancreas can pass it to the bicarbonate system to be cleared through the gut. Blue-heavy, infrared-poor, pulsing light breaks the daily rhythm and lets it seep into tissue and spinal fluid, where it lodges in the ordered water around proteins and slows their motion. Sensing that heavy floor, the hypothalamus locks into a lasting cell danger response (a defensive shutdown mode), felt as anxiety or headache.

Wrong-way light also makes singlet oxygen, an energised form of oxygen that bypasses the body's normal safety controls. Kruse calls it a "molecular buzzsaw": it burns the fatty membrane rafts that anchor PER2, a core clock protein, which he names as the root of every circadian mismatch. In his model the body clock is physical hardware being damaged from inside, not an abstract genetic timer. He adds that most clock control happens after proteins are made, as they fold: proteins take their final shape under forces from light, so incoherent LED light keeps them from reaching it, and strained mitochondria send out GDF15, a distress signal.

Light stripped of sunlight's infrared and UV also blocks the POMC hormone pathway from making its tanning and calming signals. In his telling the skin–brain melanin loop then drifts, the brain adds AMPA receptors (fast receivers for the excitatory messenger glutamate) and nerves turn jumpy, which people feel as racing thoughts and jitters.

The eye takes the first hit. He says nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring), blue light and a weak local field damage melanopsin (the eye's blue-sensing clock receptor), mitochondrial DNA and heme proteins, dry out the retina's melanin and let deuterium into the eye's water. Contact lenses worn around the clock, he adds, trap blue light and EMF right at the eye.

Isolated indoor blue, roughly 400–465 nm, keeps the energy chain stuck on the singlet-oxygen path and browns the brain's neuromelanin, the pigment he treats as its light processor.

#### When to block blue — and when not to

After sunset, blue light should be gone. But Kruse is equally firm that blue blockers of any tint, red or yellow included, should never be worn in daylight. There, he says, they act on the clock "like a drug": they change the body's own feedback loops in the same way a supplement or medication does, and they block the daylight signals that build melanin.

The hour before sunrise matters too. Habitually switching on artificial light before dawn, he says, keeps Complex IV from being primed by the day's first natural light, so the cell never makes the water that hydrates melanin and runs the whole day in a fermentation-style, low-efficiency mode.

#### Screens and "reality subtraction"

Kruse calls social media and AI feeds "reality subtraction": algorithms that replace direct perception of the real world with a profit-driven version, exploiting dopamine while users sit ungrounded under blue light. The weaker the magnetic field a person senses, he says, the more marketing and manipulation get through. Wild animals simply stay locked to the planet's rhythms; the human frontal lobe lets us plan ahead but also lets us ignore the present, which makes us, in his view, the one species that chooses to live out of sync.

A phone's blue light is, in his phrase, a "counterfeit timestamp": it tells the body clock it is always noon, so the brain's night-time waste flush never gets going. Fast scrolling adds strobing spikes of blue light down the optic nerve. In healthy brain fluid they would pass cleanly, he says, but in deuterium-heavy spinal fluid they scatter into noise that the brain's vomiting centre reads as a toxin, bringing on nausea and headache.

Screens also spoil exercise. In his account blue light from TV leaves the clock genes and DNA weighed down, and training on top of that only multiplies the strain, so going straight from the television to a workout makes exercise partly toxic.

#### In practice

His blue-light-hygiene rules: swap LED and fluorescent bulbs for incandescent; after sunset, use red incandescent bulbs or glasses that block 100% of blue; never wear blue blockers in daylight; avoid switching on artificial light before sunrise; and by day, open a window rather than buy sun-imitating lamps. His screen-light-hygiene rules: cut screen and wireless time, don't go straight from TV or screens into a workout, and keep devices out of the bedroom.
