### Elephants, rhinos and why big brains grew
- **id:** brain-growth-light
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse argues big brains grew from how a species handled sunlight and vibration rather than from lucky genes: the thin-skinned elephant and the hairless human invested in light capture and time, the armored rhino in mass, and indoor life is now shrinking the human brain.
- **protocols:** sun-exposure
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says big brains grow when a species captures enough sunlight through its skin and keeps its body water clear, not from lucky genes.
  - In his model the thin-skinned elephant bet on light and time, while the armored rhino bet on mass and blocked solar input.
  - He frames evolution as "Symmetry Selection," with each species settling into the state that keeps its physical Action lowest.
  - He calls large brains a vibrational achievement, built from coupling sound and light in body water.
  - He reads the human brain's shrinking as the cost of indoor life, and wants bare skin in sunlight plus daily humming, laughing and chanting.

Kruse argues that big brains are not a genetic jackpot. In his model a brain grows large when a species finds a way to capture enough sunlight, and to keep its body water clear enough, to power it. He uses the elephant and the rhinoceros, two large animals that lived under the same sun and ended up with very different brains, as his main test case.

#### Same sun, different bets

Rhinos, elephants, gorillas and humans all evolved on the same high-UV savanna. What decided whether each invested in armor and bulk or in brains, Kruse says, was the coupling between skin and brain that he calls the transdermal MITF–AMPAR loop: the circuit linking the pigment switch in skin (MITF) to the brain's fast glutamate receptors (AMPA receptors). He calls the result a "Solar Panel Efficiency" law of evolution. The rhino's armored hide and keratin horn form an "Ohmic shield," a resistor that blocks solar input, so it stays a low-complexity tank. The elephant's thin, blood-rich skin and its sensitive trunk harvest enough light to run the trunk's roughly 100,000 muscles and a large brain.

Hair tells the same story. Gorillas, he says, kept their coats, choosing a stable body temperature over capturing light, so their brains were capped at what digestion alone could supply. Humans, a change he ties to the fusion that formed our chromosome 2, shed most of their hair and turned the whole skin into a neuroectodermal semiconductor (light-handling tissue from the same embryonic layer as the brain) that "remote-starts" the brain's mitochondria.

He adds a twist on what the big brain is for. Humans, in his account, also lost the external magnetic hardware other animals keep, such as long hair, horns and large canine teeth, and needed a bigger neocortex to process the "noise" of an isotopic exhaust that no longer clears as well. He calls the rhino "hardware-dominant" and the elephant "software-dominant," with humans in between: hair, but no tusks or horns.

#### Mass or time

Kruse frames this with the physicist's "Action," the running total of a system's Lagrangian (kinetic minus potential energy) over its lifetime, which nature tends to keep as small as possible. Bulk, as in the rhino and gorilla, is a strategy heavy on kinetic energy: it wastes energy as heat and shortens relative lifespan. Complexity, as in the elephant and human, leans on potential energy and information, and maximizes what he calls UV-to-near-infrared "income." He notes that the Human Genome Project found species' gene "parts lists" strikingly similar, so the difference, in his reading, lies in the strategy: the elephant chose potential energy and time, the rhino kinetic energy and mass, as ways of dealing with the sun, which he nicknames "the Thief."

From there he restates his long-held "Symmetry Selection" idea, which he says he has proposed for 25 years. Evolution, in his view, selects a symmetry group rather than accumulating random mutations, much as a cooling crystal settles into one lattice. An organism "chooses" the symmetry that keeps its Action lowest under the boundary conditions around it, chiefly UV, near-infrared and nnEMF (man-made wireless and electrical fields). Species are stable phases of a "biological Lagrangian," the MITF–AMPAR axis plays the part of the force-carrying particle, and every cell evaluates its surroundings locally, so DNA is not the boss. In a noisy world of wireless fields and LEDs, he says, that symmetry collapses into simpler, higher-entropy states.

#### The elephant's toolkit

The trunk, Kruse says, is both antenna and actuator. Its 100,000 muscles are coordinated by phase-locking, through spin filtering (CISS) and an infrared water harmonic near 1878 nm, rather than by heat-generating nerve "computation." He agrees that the trunk works as a water-cooled actuator that grounds the animal and samples Earth's field. Bursts of brain growth 20–26 million years ago, during unstable climate, were in his reading not mutations: the rhino added resistance (armor) to block noisy flux while the elephant raised its "optical indexing" (brain) to process it, conserving its melanin hardware as Noether's theorem (the physics rule tying each symmetry to a conserved quantity) would predict. He also claims the elephant's large brain serves as a resistor, letting it drive the trunk without frying its own circuits, and that elephants lack the brain melanin humans carry.

Tusks have a role too. Ivory is collagen plus hydroxyapatite, the mineral of bone and teeth, and Kruse says it turns mechanical stress into voltage, matching seismic and infrasound vibration (sound below human hearing) to the trunk's muscles. Through the living pulp it connects to blood and nerves, a point he says he made in a talk at a dental school in 1986. He calls the tusk a "dielectric mirror" that deflects optical noise away from the brain, and agrees that its spiral growth uses twisting forces to separate isotopes.

Pregnancy is calibration time in his account. At 22 months, the elephant's pregnancy is in his view the longest "optical calibration" of any mammal, needed to phase-lock the 30-million-volt-per-meter field of the inner mitochondrial membrane across a 5,000-gram brain. The rhino's 15 months saves seven months but spends the savings on armor rather than on an optical buffer.

#### Brains built by vibration

Kruse adds that large brains are a "vibrational achievement." The big-brained mammals, he says, all couple sound and light through what he calls a quantum acoustic mirror. Whales and dolphins use sonar in the dense medium of the sea to shake their body water into that mirror. Elephants use infrasound and the rumble of the ground carried up the skeleton's central axis as their own version of a hummed "M." Humans have to hum, laugh and chant on purpose. In his view brains grew large because the water mirror became clear enough for fast signaling by light, not the other way round.

#### Shrinking again

He endorses the idea that the human brain has lost roughly 10% of its volume over the last 10,000 years, and reads it as our own "Neanderthal Event": moving indoors cost people the ultraviolet "clutch" that engages the brain's light machinery. Sunscreen, in the same picture, is a chemical tarp over the skin panel, so the brain that shares its embryonic tissue with skin is left underfed and shrinks further — especially under high-latitude indoor life. Artificial light and electromagnetic interference, he warns, add to that loss.

#### In practice

Because in his model the skin is the solar panel that builds and runs the brain, Kruse wants as much bare skin as possible in real sunlight, not just the eyes, as in his sun-exposure routine, without sunscreen. He warns against building bulk under artificial light at the brain's expense, and asks people to hum, laugh and chant daily to supply the vibration humans no longer get from the ground, while cutting the wireless and indoor light he blames for the shrinking.

- **sources:** [369000](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369000/), [369001](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369001/), [369420](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369420/), [368938](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368938/), [368962](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368962/), [368940](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368940/), [368941](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368941/), [368774](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368774/), [368775](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368775/), [368776](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368776/), [368783](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368783/), [368784](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368784/)

## Water

### Deuterium and what to drink
- **id:** deuterium-and-water
- **pillar:** water
- **summary:** Kruse says healthy mitochondria make their own deuterium-depleted water, that deuterium can only be excreted and should be controlled rather than erased, and that it matters most once magnetic or electrical interference has already damaged the cell's engine.
- **protocols:** drinking-water, ddw-targets, sea-salt-ddw
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says healthy mitochondria make their own deuterium-depleted water at Complex IV, and deuterium can only be excreted, never converted.
  - In his model deuterium becomes a problem only after magnetic or electrical interference has deformed the cell's engine.
  - He says a strong, stable magnetic field sorts light hydrogen from deuterium, which matters more than cutting intake.
  - His water choice is reverse osmosis with UV, copper over plastic, no fluoride, and DDW at 25–105 ppm, never aiming for zero.
  - He counts pressure from diving or immersion as a broom that clears deuterium, but puts location and light first.

Deuterium is hydrogen carrying an extra neutron, which makes it twice as heavy as ordinary hydrogen. Kruse calls clearing it "the real game" for human health, but he argues the body is built to do this itself. The main goal, in his model, is restoring that ability; drinking water with less deuterium is a support, not the fix.

#### How the body handles deuterium

In Kruse's model, Complex IV — the last step of the mitochondrial energy chain, where oxygen is turned into water — acts as an "isotope mass gate" that makes deuterium-depleted water (DDW). Healthy mitochondria keep their internal water near 90 parts per million (ppm) of deuterium, so only light hydrogen enters ATP synthase, the cell's energy-making motor. Deuterium cannot be converted into anything else; it can only be excreted. Sunlight carries no deuterium, he notes, which is why life built its energy system on light hydrogen.

Deuterium in the blood is normal. Kruse points to a serum table listing it at 12–14 millimoles per litre, two to three times the level of glucose or potassium, and treats it there as a harmless buffer so long as it stays out of the Krebs and urea cycles inside cells. It travels not as a free ion but inside metabolic water from struggling mitochondria, in glucose and amino acids, and in the bicarbonate buffer. The vagus nerve acts as gatekeeper, firing when it senses the electrical charge of the blood drop, and the pancreas, with its rich blood supply, gathers the heavy load and empties it into the upper gut in its bicarbonate juice.
Kruse stresses that deuterium becomes a problem only after the cell's "engine" has been deformed by magnetic or electrical interference: a weak or unstable local field, nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring), or artificial light. When the field holding the mitochondrial membrane together collapses, deuterium floods in and jams the motor. A strong, stable horizontal field, by contrast, sorts light hydrogen from deuterium, because their nuclei respond differently to magnetism: ordinary hydrogen's nucleus is a magnet about 3.25 times stronger than deuterium's, so a field can steer the one far more than the other. That, he says, is why coconuts and bananas carry no penalty in El Salvador that they would in Boston. Cutting deuterium intake is always wise, in his view, but sorting it well matters more.

Sorting has limits, though. Someone who grounds but keeps drinking ordinary water of about 155 ppm in a poor-field area, he says, will tan on the outside yet still lock up the inside, because deuterium keeps arriving through the gut.

#### Water in nature

Light water evaporates first, so heavy water rains out early as weather moves toward the poles, and high-latitude water and food are naturally low in deuterium. Kruse accepts this as the explanation for long-lived Siberians. Yet he says northerners who live indoors under artificial light load up anyway, because a weaker shield and indoor life stop them sorting it. Warm, stagnant seas and desert surface water grow richer in deuterium as light water evaporates.

Big river floodplains gather heavy water too. The Mississippi's draws runoff from vast basins, and he says rain over regions where the compass points far from true north is heavier still, so local water and food carry more deuterium. Each source, he adds, has to be judged by its own hydrology. Deep "primary water," which the geologist Stephan Riess held forms new deep in the earth, has never evaporated and is naturally low in deuterium.

#### What to drink

Kruse's choice is reverse-osmosis water with a UV stage. He warns against hard, metal-laden well water, especially for people working in heat around farm chemicals. He ranks fluoride worse than iodine in an unstable field: it mimics deuterium, collapses structured water (water ordered into a charge-holding layer) and blinds the body's light sensors. Clean local spring water drunk in the sun is his source of minerals, and deep primary water from volcanic fissures or ancient artesian wells beats surface water.

For DDW he gives a range of 25–105 ppm, against about 150 ppm for tap water, and says how much a person needs is individual — as is whether to drink it daily or in cycles. It works quickly, he says, only when the body's own clearance routes are working. Feeling worse at first is expected in a deuterium-loaded body. He makes his own DDW by running a small current through zinc and copper electrodes in a decanter, which he says copies how mitochondria do it. He warns that commercial hydrogen water, made by splitting ordinary water, carries that water's deuterium with it.

He does not aim for zero. A vortex, in his model, needs a little heavy mass as ballast to catch and hold its spin, and water of pure light hydrogen would let ATP synthase spin out and lose its rhythm. The goal is to control deuterium, not erase it.

#### Pipes and structure

Plumbing matters too. Copper pipes, he says, give water free electrons and expand its structured layer, while plastic strips its charge — his "plastic static trap" — and weakens the gut microbes' ability to sort deuterium. He extends this to copper drinking vessels: water breaks apart faster on a copper surface, light hydrogen reacts first, and deuterium is held back at the metal's oxide layer, so he says letting water stand in copper for 4–16 hours shifts it toward light hydrogen.

Older cities built vortices into their water systems; at the centre of a water vortex, he says, the lighter, thinner water gathers while heavier water is flung outward. Water cascading down a stepped wall restructures it through the Lenard effect (the charge separation produced when water splashes and breaks).
#### Pressure as a broom

Kruse also counts pressure as a clearing tool. Outside pressure, he says, wrings deuterium out of the thin shells of water that cling to proteins, which is why he suggests everyone may one day need to learn to dive. Deep-diving mammals, he says, use depth this way to lighten their large bodies. He prefers plain pressure, from diving or from immersion at two to three atmospheres on ordinary air, to hyperbaric oxygen, because flooding a body that has slipped into fermentation with extra oxygen feeds disorderly reactive oxygen. As he puts it, oxygen is the fuel and pressure is the broom.

#### In practice

His drinking-water steps: filter by reverse osmosis followed by UV; don't rehydrate on hard well water; avoid fluoride; get minerals from clean spring water in the sun; prefer copper plumbing to plastic. Prefer coconut meat over coconut water as a daily drink (he treats the water as a deuterium load best reserved for blood-like trauma use), and egg yolks over whites for the same isotope reason. His ddw-targets: 25–105 ppm in general, made at home or bought, paired with carbonated water and mineral salt (sea-salt-ddw), and checked by sending a sample to a lab. Diving or other time under water pressure adds a mechanical way to clear it. He treats deuterium as secondary — location and light come first.

### How deuterium jams the motor
- **id:** deuterium-atp-synthase
- **pillar:** water
- **summary:** Kruse's account of how deuterium disrupts the cell: it scrambles electron spin, pays a heavy energy "tax" to move, chokes Complex I, makes the ATP synthase motor stutter and stiffens the water around it.
- **protocols:** ddw-targets
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says deuterium, hydrogen with an extra neutron, disrupts both the quantum and mechanical steps of making energy, with ATP synthase as its main target.
  - In his model its doubled mass and different spin slow enzymes, scatter spin-sorted electrons and stiffen the water around proteins.
  - He names Complex I as the most deuterium-sensitive step and reads a high NAD⁺ to NADH ratio as a sign of a deuterium-depleted cell.
  - He argues the cell's design, from glycolysis to spinning blood and spinal fluid, works to keep deuterium out of the mitochondria.
  - He pairs deuterium-depleted water with fixing light and location, which he treats as the primary levers.

Kruse argues that deuterium (hydrogen with an extra neutron) harms cells not simply by being heavier, but by disrupting both the quantum and the mechanical steps of energy production. Its main target is ATP synthase, the tiny rotary motor in mitochondria that, in his figure, spins about 9,000 times a minute to make ATP, the cell's energy currency.

#### Why the extra neutron matters

Deuterium is not rare. Kruse puts its level in blood serum at roughly 150 parts per million, more than the calcium, magnesium or potassium doctors routinely measure. That is normal in blood; the trouble starts when it reaches the mitochondria's inner chemistry, where he likens it to a bowling ball hitting a desk fan.

Deuterium has the same charge as ordinary hydrogen but double the mass, and its nucleus has a different spin. Kruse says that spin drifts out of step with the chiral (one-handed) proteins that sort electrons by spin, a process called CISS. He calls deuterium a spin-phase "wrecking ball" that scatters spin-sorted electrons and shifts the outcome of the magnetically sensitive reactions that depend on them.

Every chemical bond vibrates at a minimum energy, its zero-point energy. In Kruse's account deuterium's sits lower, so its bonds need more energy to break and it tunnels (slips through energy barriers by a quantum effect) far less easily. Light hydrogen tunnels almost "tax-free"; deuterium pays a heavy "tax." He says a single deuterium atom locks about 96 surrounding water molecules into a stiffer lattice and drags cell water from its structured, charge-holding state toward ordinary water. Morning infrared, he says, builds exclusion-zone water, the ordered layer water forms beside surfaces, whose walls push deuterium to the edges; nnEMF, blue light or weak spots in Earth's field collapse those walls and let it in.

The slowdown has a name, the kinetic isotope effect: heavier isotopes react more slowly. Kruse says it slows the enzymes of the energy chain and of fat burning two- to sevenfold, weakening the proton gradient that drives the motor and leaking reactive oxygen. Healthy mitochondria normally scrub deuterium by pumping light hydrogen in preference, but a rising load from food overwhelms them.

#### Complex I takes the first hit

Kruse names Complex I, the entry point of the energy chain, as its most deuterium-sensitive step, and links falling NAD⁺ levels to it. A heavy glucose load forces electrons through this bottleneck. Wireless exposure feeds the jam: citing a 2011 finding that cell-phone fields raise the brain's glucose use, he says nnEMF disorders the water around proteins, the body answers with more blood sugar, and glucose carries deuterium straight to Complex I.

He treats a high ratio of NAD⁺ to NADH, its electron-loaded form, as one of the most reliable signs of a deuterium-depleted cell. In a heavy system NADH carries deuterium where ordinary hydrogen should be, and the enzymes that strip it off slow down. Rebuilding NAD⁺ by the back route, the long kynurenine pathway, is slowed at each step in deuterium-heavy water, yielding a "heavy" NAD⁺ that ferries electrons poorly.

Under that load the cell switches to a side route, the glycerol-3-phosphate shuttle, which drops electrons further down the chain without pumping protons at that step, so heavy hydrogen is left behind. He calls it "a proton thing, not a food thing." A well-running adult, in his ranking, uses full Complex I, which clear thinking needs; a stressed adult falls back on the shunt. Clearing deuterium and restoring grounding lets the person leave it, and he says the mental haze lifts.

#### The stuttering motor

On ATP synthase's rotor, a deuteron (a deuterium nucleus) takes a proton's place, binds too tightly and lets go too slowly. The motor stutters, its parts warp, and in his phrase deuterium "strips the gears." ATP output and membrane voltage fall, and the cell drops back to fermentation, the Warburg shift. In his reading, the high blood sugar and insulin that follow are a compensation rather than the root problem: the body floods the jammed engine with fuel, paying what he calls an "entropy tax." In the brain, the same shortage of electrons is misread by reward centres as famine and drives cravings.

The heart and brain feel the shortfall early. In models he cites, a drop of about 5% in ATP efficiency can push heart rate up by as much as 28%. In the brain, the sodium–potassium pump that keeps nerve cells charged uses 20–30% of all its energy; once mitochondria stop making their own water, he says, the pump falters and the brain's alarm centre misfires.

Drawing on Szent-Györgyi, Ling, Prigogine and Becker, he describes ATP synthase as a motor that must stay far from balance. Deuterium pulls it back toward a settled, symmetric state, which for a living system that runs by constantly dissipating energy means death, his "internal rigor mortis." Much of what is called inflammation, he adds, is this lost energy: a slowed motor pushing against thick, low-charge water dumps its effort as trapped heat.

The damage spreads. Deuterium-loaded NAD⁺, which he writes as "NADD+," produces pseudohypoxia: oxygen is present but the cell cannot use it. He says the carbon–deuterium bond also slows the first enzyme of glutathione production by up to tenfold and upsets the last step in making melatonin, so antioxidant protection and night-time repair both falter.

#### How the body fights back

In Kruse's view the design of complex cells is itself an answer to deuterium. Archaea, one of the two partners that merged to form such cells, tolerated it; bacteria, the ancestors of mitochondria, did not. The merged cell keeps the two apart: the heart's "gravity well" and the swirl of fluid in the brain's fourth ventricle spin deuterium into blood and spinal fluid, which serve as a sink, so the inside of mitochondria stays almost pure light hydrogen.

The ten steps of glycolysis and nine of the Krebs cycle act, he says, as an "isotope centrifuge," harvesting light hydrogen and holding back deuterium before it reaches the motor. Spinning blood and spinal fluid fling deuterium-heavy water outward, where melanin in vessel walls catches it, and joints spin it out by moving. He borrows the power-plant maxim "the solution to pollution is dilution": in any swirling flow the lightest isotope moves fastest, so deuterium is flung aside and diluted. The spinning ATP synthase does the same at the smallest scale, and when the inner mitochondrial membrane holds its full voltage, he says, deuterium cannot get in at all.
#### In practice

The linked ddw-targets protocol calls for deuterium-depleted water in his 25–105 ppm range, paired with carbonated water. He expects people to feel worse at first in a deuterium-loaded body, and treats deuterium as secondary to fixing light and location, since those determine whether the body can sort it. His waking readout: warm in the core but cool on the skin means the membrane is back at full voltage, while waking sweaty or cold and clammy means deuterium is jamming the motors and their output is scattering as heat.

### Carbon dioxide: breath, sauna and carbonated water
- **id:** co2-brain-clearance
- **pillar:** water
- **summary:** Kruse treats carbon dioxide as a brain protector and deuterium flush, raised by gentle under-breathing, rhythmic CO₂ pulses, sauna and naturally carbonated water.
- **protocols:** carbonated-water, sinus-hum-reset, co2-pulse-breathing, buteyko-breathing, volcano-protocol
- **key points:**
  - Kruse sees carbon dioxide as protective, not waste: it shields the brain, releases oxygen into tissues and helps clear deuterium.
  - In his model CO₂ hands oxygen to tissues through the Bohr effect, so under-breathing can deliver more oxygen than heavy breathing.
  - He says rhythmic CO₂ pulses wake the brain's waste-clearance system while awake, pumping spinal fluid through tissue.
  - He pictures the brain's fluid spaces as a motor whose vortex flings deuterium outward and leaves light water at its core.
  - His practices are Buteyko breathing, CO₂ pulse breathing, traditional sauna and naturally carbonated water with deuterium-depleted water.

Most people think of carbon dioxide as waste. Kruse sees it as protective: in his model CO₂ shields the brain from magnetic degradation, releases oxygen into tissues and helps clear deuterium (heavy hydrogen). He argues for holding on to more of it, through breathing, sauna and carbonated water.

#### Copying the planet

Kruse takes Earth's behaviour as his model. During a decline in the magnetic field, he says, volcanoes release CO₂ that dissolves into seawater as a "bicarbonate blanket," changing the water's electrical properties and keeping iodine locked in the sea. He reads rising atmospheric CO₂ (he cites 370 to 426 ppm) and the roughly 5% greening of the planet as Earth's "dielectric booster" — a boost to its charge-holding capacity — as its magnetic shield weakens. His Volcano Protocol copies this response at body scale.

#### How CO₂ delivers oxygen

CO₂, not oxygen, sets the rhythm of breathing. Through the Bohr effect — CO₂ making blood slightly more acidic so hemoglobin lets go of its oxygen — CO₂ is what hands oxygen to tissues. Without enough CO₂ and the nitric oxide made in the sinuses, Kruse says, oxygen stays stuck on hemoglobin: a pulse oximeter reads normal while tissue starves. He calls this the "Deuterium Ghost" and says it shows as dark circles under the eyes and as fatigue, anxiety and brain fog. The less sun a person gets, the better their breathing must be, and he calls mouth-breathing in a high-tech office a "death sentence."

#### Waking the brain's waste clearance

Kruse cites Dr. Sephira Ryman's team at the University of New Mexico, who alternated room air with CO₂-raised air every 35 seconds for 30 minutes. This woke the glymphatic system — the brain's waste-clearance network that normally runs in deep sleep — while people were awake: vessels opened and closed rhythmically, as in slow-wave sleep, pumping spinal fluid through tissue and flushing out tau and amyloid and, he adds, deuterium. He stresses that the rhythm does the work, and says clearing that debris lets the brain's water restructure.

He reads Kochunov's findings of reduced fluid flow in the brain as a viscosity map. Blood and spinal fluid are thixotropic — they thin as they move — and deuterium's stronger hydrogen bonds thicken them. Where Earth's field is weak, he says, melanin cannot sort deuterium out, so flow slows.

He also points to Russia, which uses CO₂ in its space program — training cosmonauts on carbonated water — and once sold "gazirovka" from street machines. In his reading those machines treated citizens as astronauts on Earth: a street-corner stand-in for missing sun and field, and a cheaper everyday substitute for bottled deuterium-depleted water. He says he spent close to $200,000 translating Russian biophysics papers two decades ago, and that this is why he drinks carbonated water.

#### The brain's fluid motor

Kruse pictures the brain's fluid spaces as a motor. Tiny beating hairs (motile cilia) on the cells lining the ventricles drive a travelling wave through spinal fluid, much like a stadium wave. Because that fluid is rich in charged salts, he says, the moving wave throws off ribbons of magnetism, with the C-shaped lateral ventricles and the diamond-shaped fourth ventricle acting as the motor's fixed housing. He recalls watching fluid waves in the third ventricle change at sunset during an operation some fifteen years ago, says he spent a decade researching it, and ties the pulsing flow to the timing of the twisting heartbeat.

A vortex, in his view, is the only shape that concentrates energy without heating up. A fast spinal-fluid vortex lowers the pressure at its centre, flings deuterium to the edges and leaves a core of light water where ATP synthase can spin freely. Fluid heavy with deuterium tumbles instead, scattering its energy as the brain fog and inflammation he sees so widely.

Unlike rhinos, which drain passively, humans clear the brain actively, he says: the voice and the jolt of each step help push waste toward the brain's outlet points. Silence, long sitting and artificial light clog those outlets, which he says brings back a metallic taste and mental fog.

#### Breathing less: Buteyko, Tummo and sleep

Buteyko breathing (deliberately breathing less) raises arterial CO₂ to about 40–45 mmHg without lowering oxygen saturation. In Kruse's account this dilates small brain vessels and, through the Bohr shift, sends more oxygen to the brainstem — and, more fundamentally for him, raises magnetic flux so electron spins stay parallel and chiral molecules work. That is why he ranks Buteyko above hard hyperventilation styles that dump CO₂: cold thermogenesis can still fractionate deuterium, but the pairing he prefers is Buteyko with cold, the combo he credits to Russian space practice. In Tummo breathing it is the breath hold, not the active phase, that raises CO₂. Humans breathe at low pressure, he says, so the breath is an under-used exit for deuterium; deep breathing, breath holds and even brass or wind instruments that train the diaphragm for hypoxia can widen it. He says a Buteyko belt slowing breathing during sleep raises CO₂ and magnetic flux in the diaphragm, toning the vagus. A traditional sauna's real benefit, in his view, is its CO₂, not its heat.

#### Carbonated water and baking soda

Carbonated water with deuterium-depleted water (DDW) is his "gaseous isotope flush": it builds a CO₂–bicarbonate loop that draws deuterium out of spinal fluid and gut. Carbonation also dissolves minerals, making sulfate and magnesium more available; he names San Pellegrino for sulfate and Muszynianka for magnesium. The source rock matters: San Pellegrino flows through dolomite and gypsum and has its CO₂ added at bottling, while Muszynianka is carbonated naturally in volcanic Carpathian rock, and natural CO₂, he says, dissolves more of the rare-earth trace elements he values. Drinking carbonated water in the cold, he adds, strengthens the Bohr effect.

Trouble tolerating carbonated water, he says, signals deeper "lattice lock" (stiff, deuterium-loaded body water). Oral baking soda, his "bicarb hack," copies the roughly two litres of bicarbonate the pancreas releases each day, which in his model flushes deuterium. Citing a 2018 study, he says it signals the spleen through acetylcholine to calm inflammation.

#### In practice

The linked protocols: drink carbonated water with DDW, favouring naturally carbonated volcanic sources (carbonated-water); breathe less with Buteyko — a small breath in and out, a 3–5 second nose-pinch hold, then 10 seconds of easy breathing, for 5 minutes — and use a traditional sauna and, if desired, a sleep belt (buteyko-breathing); alternate room air and CO₂-raised air every 35 seconds for 30 minutes (co2-pulse-breathing); for a blocked sinus, hum, position the head, do CO₂ breathing and ground in morning sun (sinus-hum-reset). His Volcano Protocol combines a high-pressure water jet with a 40 Hz tuning fork on the spine, carbonated water, DDW and sauna, and barefoot sunrise on volcanic ground in a pinned Pacific Rim location.

- **sources:** [369965](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369965/), [375978](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375978/), [381025](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381025/), [379564](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379564/), [379565](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379565/), [381107](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381107/), [378191](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378191/), [375631](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375631/), [384898](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384898/), [371222](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371222/), [371751](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371751/), [373683](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373683/)

### Seafood, DHA and careful iodine
- **id:** seafood-meal
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse favors wild seafood for DHA and minerals, prefers cold, well-mixed waters such as the Pacific Northwest, accepts antioxidants only within whole marine food, and says iodine is safe only for someone who is pinned.
- **protocols:** seafood-meal
- **key points:**
  - Kruse calls wild seafood his go-to food because it supplies DHA, magnesium and marine antioxidants in forms the body can use.
  - In his model DHA is a semiconductor that turns sunlight into current and shields the brain's appetite and energy circuit.
  - He says where a fish grew matters, ranking cold Pacific Northwest waters above the US East Coast and southern oceans.
  - He accepts antioxidants such as astaxanthin and vitamin C only as whole food, saying isolated pills scramble membranes and silence useful alarms.
  - He treats iodine as powerful but conditional: safe for someone who is pinned, risky for someone indoors and unpinned.

Wild seafood is Kruse's go-to food. In his model it supplies DHA and magnesium in a form the body can use, along with marine antioxidants bound into whole food. But he attaches conditions, above all to iodine and to where the seafood comes from, that depend on a person's light and magnetic environment.

#### DHA as a light conductor

DHA is the long omega-3 fat concentrated in the retina and brain. Kruse rejects the textbook view of it as a generic fat for membrane flexibility. Citing Michael Crawford, he treats DHA as a magnetic fat whose semiconductor chain of six double bonds has been conserved for hundreds of millions of years; cholesterol shares that magnetic role, and cardiolipin is the matching fat inside mitochondria that holds Complexes I–IV at the right ångström spacing. Packed into the retina, the brain's master clock and its links to the thalamus, DHA pushes surrounding water into a structured, charge-holding state, so these pathways carry sunlight's signal like fibre optics. How much it helps, he says, depends on how damaged a person's mitochondrial membrane voltage already is — and on whether cardiolipin has been enriched with linoleic chains after the matrix lost its magnetic ground.

DHA is also the raw material for healing signals. Citing Bazan's work, he notes that photoreceptors and neurons lengthen DHA into elovanoids and neuroprotectin D1, messengers that tell inflamed tissue to resolve. When the fat carries deuterium (heavy hydrogen), its bonds vibrate at a different frequency, so these "heal" signals arrive mistuned while the inflammatory signals made from arachidonic acid carry on, his "signal-jamming."

He also calls DHA a Faraday cage (a shield that keeps out stray fields) for the leptin–melanocortin pathway, the brain circuit that reads the fat hormone leptin and sets appetite and energy use. Its fast-moving electrons protect the leptin receptor from oxidative noise, so the brain reads the body's energy stores correctly. Eaten with saturated fat, he says, high-DHA seafood gives the thalamus the shielding it needs to take in magnetic signals.

#### Why seafood rather than plants

Plant protein cannot stand in, in his view. Lentils supply protein but no ready-made DHA and none of the iodine-to-selenium balance of marine food, and treating all animal protein as one category misses that seafood carries electron "instructions" in its DHA. He points to Kerala, where fish is a daily food and the Indian state has the country's highest life expectancy and notably tall young men, crediting small oily fish such as sardines and mackerel.

#### Where the seafood comes from

That makes the water a fish grew in matter. Kruse says southern-hemisphere seafood has become a concentrated source of deuterium: the water cycle leaves heavier water at those latitudes, and aquaculture, industrial processing and seed-oil contamination of marine food chains add to it. In the Atlantic, he says the slowing of the great overturning current that carries surface water north and sinks it into the deep is leaving deuterium in US East Coast surface water.

His regional ranking follows. The US East Coast is suboptimal. The Gulf is mixed: evaporation adds deuterium, but the Loop Current keeps the water moving. The Pacific Northwest is his preference, because he sees the Pacific's field as steadier and its cold, deep upwelling as lower in deuterium. Oysters show the trade-off: one filters up to 50 gallons of water a day, concentrating whatever is around it, so for people already deuterium-loaded an East Coast oyster's heavy hydrogen may now outweigh its DHA. He says he has switched to Gulf and Pacific oysters.

#### Antioxidants only as food

Astaxanthin, the red carotenoid in salmon and krill, Kruse accepts only as food, ideally after time barefoot in the surf. Food-form astaxanthin arrives bound to DHA and lines up across the membrane like a wire; isolated or synthetic forms, he says, insert crookedly and scramble the membrane's structure. A long, oil-loving body with water-loving ends, it can span the whole mitochondrial membrane like "structural rebar," acting as a "dielectric sealant" against the shaking wireless fields cause. Its chain of alternating double bonds also quenches high-energy excited states, so he calls it an internal blue-blocker that soaks up the noise mitochondria release before it reaches the nucleus.

Isolated antioxidants also silence a useful alarm. Citing Martin Picard's work, Kruse calls brief bursts of singlet oxygen "mitoception," the cell sensing its own distress, which tell the nucleus and the brain's clock that the local field is compromised and prompt mitochondria to make their own melatonin. Scavenger pills cut that wire. Reactive oxygen, in his view, is a readout of a broken spin filter, not a chemical mess to mop up.

Vitamin C, in his account, is the main guard of brain DHA: when it runs low, DHA oxidises and the brain shifts into an emergency low-oxygen mode. Whole-food vitamin C, together with fat-soluble partners such as vitamin E, protects DHA at the membrane. Extra vitamin C pills often fail to raise levels inside cells, he says, because glutathione normally recycles spent vitamin C; if sulfur metabolism is impaired, the vitamin is used once, breaks down into oxalic acid and leaves. He puts light first: infrared that frees cytochrome c oxidase lowers the oxidative load eating up the vitamin, and he says nothing else works until the cell's water has changed state.

#### Magnesium

Magnesium pills help briefly, Kruse says, but a low-voltage cell cannot hold the mineral. Under nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring) and LEDs the cell's charge flips, it loses its grip on the ion, and the magnesium leaks out, bringing loose bowels or a false calm. He compares it to delivering parts to a factory with no power. His source is wild marine food or local spring water drunk in the sun.

#### Iodine: powerful but conditional

Citing Küpper and colleagues (2008), Kruse notes that iodide neutralises reactive oxygen up to 500 times faster than vitamin C, and that kelp moves iodide to its surface under stress. He sees iodine as a heavy atom that steadies electron spins in the thyroid, the stomach lining and the brain's fluid. But in people who are indoors, unpinned and out of rhythm, singlet oxygen turns incoming iodide into radicals that damage cardiolipin (the membrane fat that holds the energy chain together) and drop the cell's voltage. Isolated or high-dose iodine, he says, sets off an "uncoupled halide storm." Inside the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), a large weak patch in Earth's field over South America and the South Atlantic, he calls iodine use right now "a death sentence."

Mercury, by contrast, does not worry him: the selenium in seafood is, he says, nature's built-in antidote.

#### In practice

His seafood-meal guidance: eat wild-caught seafood for DHA and magnesium, including small oily fish such as sardines and mackerel, alongside saturated fat, favouring cold Pacific waters over the US East Coast and the southern oceans; take astaxanthin only as food, such as wild sockeye or krill, ideally after barefoot time in the surf; get vitamin C from whole food; limit iodine if you are not pinned (living grounded and sunlit under a stable local field), avoid isolated or high-dose iodine, and take no iodine supplements while living in the SAA.

- **sources:** [382654](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382654/), [382483](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382483/), [381069](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381069/), [370446](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370446/), [381076](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381076/), [375606](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375606/), [382093](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382093/), [373427](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373427/), [372307](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372307/), [373025](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373025/), [373212](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373212/), [369564](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369564/)

### Meal timing and the heme clock
- **id:** early-dinner
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse says when, and under what light, you eat shapes how the body handles sugar more than what you eat, through a heme-based clock that should switch off the liver's night-time sugar output.
- **protocols:** early-dinner
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says the timing and lighting of a meal matter more than the food on the plate: light outranks food.
  - In his model a heme-based clock in the liver should switch off night-time sugar production, and late meals under LEDs break it.
  - He wants a first meal in the morning after sunrise light, near the cortisol peak, to keep the pancreas's bicarbonate flush running.
  - He calls eating on a plane the worst case, because the metal fuselage cuts the steadying pull of Earth's field.

Kruse argues that the timing and lighting of meals matter more for metabolism than the food on the plate. A steak eaten at 10 p.m. under LEDs, he says, drives night-time glucose output and fat build-up in the liver even at zero carbohydrate, while the same ribeye eaten at solar noon in sunlight does not. His rule is that light outranks food.

#### The heme clock

In Kruse's account, the liver's night-time brake on sugar production runs on heme, the iron-holding molecule also found in hemoglobin. Heme production inside mitochondria follows the light day: the enzyme that starts it, ALAS1, peaks in the late afternoon, heme itself peaks around dusk, and later in the night the enzyme heme oxygenase-1 turns heme into carbon monoxide. Heme is the key that lets the clock protein Rev-Erbα switch off the liver's glucose-making enzymes overnight, and the carbon monoxide quiets the daytime clock genes.

Because Rev-Erbα must bind heme to work, Kruse calls it a sensor of both mitochondrial health and the local magnetic field. With morning 380 nm light and strong "pinning" (living grounded and sunlit under a stable local field), it rhythmically holds down inflammatory signals such as IL-6 and IL-1β. When its heme "rusts" (oxidises), it fails, inflammation runs unchecked and, he says, the body walls off the damage with adhesions.

Dietary iron, in his view, is only raw material: it sets how strong the heme rhythm is, never its timing. So, against Nick Norwitz and Harvard researchers, he argues that iron from red meat is not what disturbs sugar handling.

#### What a late meal does

Eating at night under blue-heavy LED light, Kruse says, disrupts melanopsin (the eye's clock receptor) and the retina–clock–liver connection, and flips oxygen into its reactive singlet form. The liver's energy chain stalls and the heat melts its structured water. Without the heme brake, the liver makes sugar and fat through the night and deuterium (heavy hydrogen) pours into the portal vein, his "Sludge Shunt." Eating late also disturbs the spinal-fluid vortex through the gut's vagal nerves and blocks hormone release after dark. He adds that blue-light exposure in newborns disrupts this same heme clock for life.

A late meal also undoes the morning's work. A heavy evening meal under LEDs, he says, silts the engine up again and cancels the deuterium clearing achieved since sunrise. Even a ketogenic diet only pays off for longevity, in his view, when it follows the light: sunset's infrared-rich, blue-fading light flips a melanopsin switch that steers the breakdown of the amino acid tryptophan toward ketones, while the same keto meal eaten under LEDs at 9 p.m. still runs down the sugar-making route. Keto, for him, is a timing loop rather than a food list.

#### Why breakfast matters

Kruse wants a first meal in the morning, at the peak of the cortisol awakening response. In his model that morning surge raises blood pressure enough to restart the brain's blood and spinal-fluid vortices, and it primes the pancreas to release its roughly two-litre bicarbonate flush, which sweeps deuterium out of the stomach and upper gut. Skipping breakfast stalls the flush, and deuterium pools in the upper gut.

The order still runs light first. Sunrise is the signal that locks the day's rhythm in place, and morning light switches on the Krebs cycle (the mitochondrion's main fuel-burning loop) and the urea cycle so deuterium can be cleared; food belongs after that. He calls breakfast loading the capacitor, and faults the usual approach for eating before light has shaped the system to receive it.

#### Never on a plane

Eating on a plane is the worst case. Inside a metal fuselage at altitude, he says, the steadying pull of Earth's field drops while cosmic radiation and nnEMF from wiring and Wi-Fi rise, so both food and gut tissue lose their magnetic footing. He calls it one of the fastest routes to pseudohypoxia, oxygen present but unusable, in the gut and brainstem.

#### In practice

His early-dinner protocol: eat the main meal near solar noon, ideally outdoors; eat nothing after dark; never eat on a plane; and eat a first meal in the morning, after sunrise light and near the cortisol peak, so the pancreas's bicarbonate flush keeps running.

- **sources:** [383821](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383821/), [383820](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383820/), [380976](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380976/), [381099](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381099/), [374284](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374284/), [379445](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379445/), [369983](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369983/), [370436](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370436/), [370295](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370295/)

### Food: substrate, isotopes and local information
- **id:** food-substrate-isotopes
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse says food supplies fuel and isotopes but not the magnetic field the cell needs; he favors fat over glucose, low-deuterium plant parts and fresh local food, and rejects any diet as a fix for a location problem.
- **protocols:** saturated-fat-fuel, zip-code-food
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says food supplies fuel and isotopes but not the magnetic field the cell needs, so no diet can fix a location problem.
  - In his model the key questions about food are how much deuterium it carries and the spin state of its electrons.
  - He ranks animal fat as the most deuterium-depleted fuel and calls glucose a "deuterium bomb," with corn syrup the worst.
  - He favours leafy greens, saturated fats and wild marine food, and says food should be fresh, local and in season.

For Kruse, nutrition is secondary to light and location. Food supplies substrate — electrons and hydrogen to burn — but not the external magnetic reference that, in his model, folds and orients the mitochondria's inner membranes, and it cannot reach the light-run clock that governs fat synthesis. Spaceflight is his proof case: the same carefully measured rations that look ideal on paper fail once magnetic and electric fields and solar plasma change. So no diet can fix what he calls a geometric problem caused by place, light and nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring). Cells, he says, count electrons, not calories.

#### Food imports spin and isotopes

Kruse reduces diet to one idea: food brings in electron spin states and hydrogen isotopes that either support or disrupt the cell's dynamo. What decides the outcome is how much deuterium (heavy hydrogen) the food carries, the spin of its electrons, whether NAD⁺ ends up loaded with deuterium (his "NADD+"), and whether oxygen stays in its stable triplet form. All of these depend on the field you are standing in, so without a stable local field even the cleanest organic diet ends, in his words, in a singlet-oxygen crash.

That is why he treats more fuel as no answer for a stalled network. A keto flu that never lifts is, in his model, a "Marcus crash." Keto shifts electron input from Complex I to Complex II, which depends on very precise spacing; under magnetic interference the fat-derived electrons pile up and the chain stalls (Marcus inversion — electron flow stalling when pushed too hard). By contrast, if switching to fat brings a felt rise in CO₂ and easier, clearer breathing, he reads that as fat doing its job.
#### Ranking the fuels

Kruse ranks fuels by their isotopes. Glucose is a "deuterium bomb," because plants pack deuterium into their storage sugars. Burning fat yields deuterium-depleted water and plenty of CO₂, and he calls animal fat the most deuterium-depleted fuel — keto, for him, is a hack of the body's water, not of its macronutrients. Protein is a fuel of desperation. Carbohydrates suit life near the equator under full sun. Fructose drains ATP and halts the cell's clean-water production; he says it evolved as a winter fat-storage switch. Because UV-B slows electron flow, heavy fat and protein under tropical sun fit poorly.

He applies the same rule to all-meat eating indoors. Fat and protein feed electrons in at Complexes II and III, which he calls the most magnetically sensitive links of the chain. Under artificial light, without UV, infrared or grounding, he says that flow backs up and blood-sugar control slips even in lean people.

The same logic works inside a single plant. As fruit and tubers ripen, plants park deuterium in their sugars and starches while the leaves stay light, so Kruse calls it an oversight to chase potassium with potatoes, sweet potatoes or coconut water while trying to cut deuterium. His lighter sources are leafy greens, animal fat, avocado, salted nuts and wild marine foods such as squid, octopus, halibut, salmon and clams. Wild ocean fish beat freshwater fish, which he says carry more deuterium from still inland water.

Plant-only and juicing diets often help at first, he says, because people stop eating deuterium-heavy processed oils, wheat and corn, a "system restore" on a crashed computer. But he calls whole-food plant eating a clean, low-torque fuel that in time cannot make enough internal light to keep the system running.

Season changes the engine too. Summer, in his model, leaves metabolism open to deuterium from tropical carbohydrates, while winter cold and fat switch on brown fat, so cells need less insulin.

Saturated fats such as ghee, coconut oil, lard and clarified butter template structured water: citing Abha Sharma and Gerald Pollack, he says they expand the ordered, charge-holding layer of water, with cold ghee producing especially large ones. Seed oils, in his view, harm mainly an ungrounded engine. Egg-yolk choline helps the liver clear deuterium and improves sleep. And salt cannot rescue a deuterium-loaded system.

He also recasts antinutrients, the plant compounds said to block mineral uptake, as largely a deuterium problem. Melanin pulls the deuterium out into the blood, where the glucagon system clears it, so in his view the sun, not avoiding foods, is what neutralises them. Spinach is his cautionary tale: its high oxalate binds iron and calcium, so little of its iron is absorbed compared with the heme iron in meat.

#### Carbon spin and corn syrup

Isotopes of carbon carry spin too. Carbon-12 has no nuclear spin; carbon-13 does. C3 plants — wheat, rice, trees and most temperate vegetables — prefer carbon-12, giving what Kruse calls a "low-noise" fuel. C4 plants such as maize, sugar cane and sorghum load carbon-13, whose spin adds noise to the electrons food delivers. Because commercial glucose and many supplement bases come from maize, he says they clash with a body tuned to local C3 food.

Corn syrup, in his account, is worse than sugar on three counts. Corn hoards deuterium because it loses little water through its leaves; enzymatic processing in ordinary water enriches it further, since each step uses up light hydrogen first; and the syrup is about a quarter unstructured, deuterium-heavy water with its sugars floating free. Crystalline beet sugar, from a C3 plant, is the lesser harm.

#### Soil, farming and glyphosate

Glyphosate acts, in his model, as a "dopant" — an impurity in a semiconductor — that breaks spin filtering. He also says it pulls magnetic iron out of soil: it props up crops in weak-field land but leaves them deuterium-heavy, and he calls it a melanin inhibitor. He reads the spread of microbial nitrogen fixation on Brazilian soy as a sign that farming inside the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), a large weak patch in Earth's field, has had to turn to microbes. The gut's microbes, he adds, have the same main job: depleting deuterium.

#### Fresh, local and in season

Food also carries information from where it grew. A freshly picked garden tomato, Kruse says, holds solar infrared, structured water and faint coherent light that the gut reads to set its clock; shipping and display under blue LEDs destroy all three. Hence his long-standing advice to eat within your zip code and in season — a tropical banana in Boston in December is, for him, a quantum error. Weston Price, he argues, mapped local magnetic dynamos, not a food formula.

He adds that deuterium-heavy rain from stalled ocean circulation can turn local food toxic. Mississippi-valley water and food, he warns, are more deuterium-heavy than ever.

#### In practice

His saturated-fat-fuel protocol: favour ghee, coconut oil, lard and clarified butter; eat egg yolks for choline; keep carbohydrates for equatorial life under full sun, avoid year-round fructose and never use corn syrup; don't make protein a main fuel (no daily protein powder, no carnivore in the tropics or indoors); and treat a keto flu that won't lift as a light and magnetic signal, starting with deep cold. His zip-code-food protocol: grow or buy food from where you live, eat in season, get nutrients — potassium included — from local whole food eaten in sunlight rather than pills, avoid maize-derived glucose and supplement bases, and avoid food and water from weak-shield zones such as the SAA.

### Fat as a capacitor: body fat, fasting and the buffer
- **id:** adipose-capacitor
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse treats body fat as an electrical capacitor and deuterium store that protects heart and brain, so stripping it, or dry fasting without enough of it, backfires; in his view weight gain reflects lost efficiency rather than a calorie surplus.
- **protocols:** saturated-fat-fuel, coherent-dry-fast, glp1-damage-offset, anorexia-recovery
- **key points:**
  - Kruse calls body fat an "adipose capacitor" that soaks up excess charge and heavy hydrogen to protect the heart and brain.
  - In his model fat builds up when mitochondria cannot burn the fuel passing through them, so the body parks it as a pressure-relief valve.
  - He says burning fat yields deuterium-depleted water, which is why he favours saturated fat and cold in a weakening field.
  - He argues that too little fat, not too much, is the danger, and that stripping fat without fixing light leaves cells "redlined."
  - He says a dry fast only works with enough body fat, done grounded in morning sun and broken with saturated fat.

Kruse turns the usual view of body fat around. In his model fat is an "adipose capacitor," a store that soaks up excess electrical charge and heavy hydrogen to protect the heart and brain, and carrying more of it is a protective response to a struggling energy system rather than the root problem.

#### Why the body stores fat

Drawing on Enzo Nisoli and Martin Picard's model of fat as a capacitor-like organ, and on Cameron Borg's idea of "energetic resistance," Kruse says fat builds up when mitochondria are stuck in Marcus inversion (electron flow stalling when it is pushed too hard). Under nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring), blue light and lost grounding, cells cannot burn the fuel passing through them. To avoid a blowout, the body routes the unburned fuel into white fat, which acts as a pressure-relief valve. Nothing raises that resistance, he adds, like deuterium (heavy hydrogen).

Fat also stores deuterium. Visceral fat and fat stored in the liver hold heavy hydrogen away from core organs, and later the fat can be burned for water: citing Kenneth Mellanby's 1942 note in Nature, Kruse says 100 grams of fat yields about 110 grams of metabolic water, twice the yield from the same weight of carbohydrate, and that this water is deuterium-depleted. Babies, he notes, are born fat to build the brain, and fat cells protect the brain by holding deuterium. Extra fat, in his view, may be a "safe mode" that buys time while the environment is put right.

Fat does more than store. It acts as an electrical insulator, a kind of Faraday cage around the gut and heart, and as a reservoir of infrared and faint cell light that gives tissues the inner vibration they need to overcome deuterium's drag. As food chains grow heavier, he predicts, body fat may become a person's last dependable source of light hydrogen, though it only helps if the brain is not already locked up by deuterium.

Fat even senses light. Mammals carry neuropsin (OPN5), the UV-A sensor, in the cornea, skin and fat cells, while birds keep it only deep in the brain and retina. Without 380 nm light reaching fat cells, Kruse says, deuterium backs up into other tissues, the brain included. He frames it as a mammalian bargain: mammals swapped the bird's light-driven energy flow for insulin-sensitive fat storage, which lets them outlast darkness and famine but ties them to a leptin signalling loop that deuterium easily disrupts.

Desert animals show the strategy. Camels, jerboas and desert ants store saturated fat and burn it for their own deuterium-depleted water, rather than relying on surface water that evaporation has made heavy. Their melanin-rich coats, he says, turn sunlight into the electron current that builds that fat. In a weakening magnetic field, he says, "we are all desert mammals," and should shift our baseline fuel toward fat to make the most of this internal light water.

#### Fats that burn on the spot

Not all fats behave alike. Medium-chain triglycerides (MCTs), especially C8 (caprylic acid), go straight from the gut to the liver and must be burned at once, so they deliver deuterium-depleted water and ketones on demand; long-chain fats can instead be parked in white fat.

Burning fat also routes more electrons through FADH₂, a carrier that enters the energy chain after its deuterium-sensitive first step. That, Kruse says, means fewer protons forced through a deuterium-burdened motor, less leak, heat and reactive oxygen, and intact folds in the inner mitochondrial membrane. It is why he calls saturated fat and cold "quantum engineering tools" for a time when Earth's field can no longer steady mitochondria.

#### The BMI paradox

The danger, in Kruse's model, is low fat mass. Heavier people ride out acute physical stress because their fat buffers the surges, while frail, thin older people have no buffer left. Lean athletes lack the capacitor and can falter suddenly under stress such as heavy EMF exposure, while heavier people survive the first hit. He calls this the "BMI paradox" and puts the danger zone below roughly BMI 20–21.

He expects a weakening field to sort people the same way. Lean, blue-lit, high-carbohydrate people, with no fuel buffer and a deuterium exhaust that depends on the failing field, falter first, while fat-adapted, grounded people keep their internal vortex spinning. Ketosis and cold, which burn fat into internal light, are in his view the ways to keep the brain stimulated from within.

Forcing fat off with calorie restriction or appetite drugs while the light and nnEMF environment stays the same strips the buffer and leaves the cell's voltage "redlined." He warns trainers the same way: shredding tens of pounds before arterial melanin and the blood vortex can hold deuterium dumps heavy water into an unprepared tree. Fix the light signals, clear heavy hydrogen and restore the energy chain, he says, and the body dissolves the capacitor on its own.

Calories, for Kruse, do not explain rising body weight. He points to US national survey data showing calorie intake roughly flat since about 1999 while average weight kept climbing, and reads that as a failure of efficiency, rising deuterium in the food chain plus a disrupted light and magnetic environment, not a surplus of energy. Poverty makes the same point: if weight depended on being able to buy calories, the poor would be thin, yet the poorest groups often carry the most fat. He reads this as field deprivation: cheap LED and fluorescent light, constant screens, dense urban nnEMF, and no time or space for sunrise or bare ground.

#### Dry fasting needs fat

A dry fast (no food or water) makes the body "drink" from its own fat, so Kruse says it only works if there is fat to draw on: not below about 20% body fat in strong sun (elsewhere about BMI 24). His "coherent" dry fast is done barefoot and grounded in morning sun, ideally with cold, which, he says, draws membrane layers closer so electrons pass more cleanly, and is broken with saturated fat. An indoor fast under LEDs and wireless backfires: no clean metabolic water can be made, and extra fat or ketones only speed the damage.

When the fat that holds the body's light signal is missing, he adds, leptin, the fat hormone he treats as the brain's report on stored energy, falls toward zero and the brain reads endless starvation.

#### In practice

The linked protocols: run mainly on saturated fat such as ghee, coconut oil, lard and clarified butter (saturated-fat-fuel); dry fast only with enough body fat, grounded in morning sun, ideally with cold, broken with saturated fat, and never indoors (coherent-dry-fast). After drug-driven fat loss he gives morning sun in a clean field away from wireless, deuterium-depleted water at 90–105 ppm with frequent cold immersion, unshielded early infrared and UV-A, timed (2S)-hesperidin, and the oral baking-soda "bicarb hack" (glp1-damage-offset). For a very low fat store he uses the same base with gentle 80–85 °F water dips instead of deep cold, plus liquid ionic magnesium and trace copper-histidine timed with morning sun (anorexia-recovery).

- **sources:** [383740](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383740/), [379924](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379924/), [381027](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381027/), [376166](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/376166/), [384272](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384272/), [370237](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370237/), [383944](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383944/), [383936](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383936/), [379285](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379285/), [372321](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372321/), [372119](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372119/), [372011](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372011/)

## Magnetism

### Field direction, local geology and what global maps miss
- **id:** earth-field-vs-geology
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says cells respond to the direction of the local magnetic field and how fast it is changing, not its average strength, and that global field models and ground readings smooth those local changes away.
- **protocols:** magnetic-sleep-pad
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says cells respond to which way the local magnetic field points and how fast it changes, not its average strength.
  - In his model a strong horizontal field steadies the cell's spin filter, while a field tipping toward vertical undermines it.
  - He argues global field models and ground readings smooth away the local jerks and drift that mitochondria actually register.
  - He says local rock can hold or scramble the surface field, so places must be judged lot by lot.
  - He tells people to check satellite data for their own area and, where the field is poor, use a magnetic sleep pad.

Kruse argues that what your cells respond to is not how strong Earth's magnetic field is where you live, but which way it points and how quickly that direction is changing. Official maps average those local shifts away, so in his model a place can look normal on paper while its field is tilting in ways that undermine mitochondria, the energy-producing parts of every cell.

#### Direction matters more than strength

At any spot the field has a horizontal part that runs parallel to the ground (he writes it "Bh") and a vertical part that dives into it ("Bz"). In Kruse's model the horizontal part works like a shock absorber for the cell's "spin filter": his name for the way one-handed (chiral) proteins let through only electrons spinning in one direction, an effect physicists call CISS. When a local field tips toward vertical, he says, that filter fails even if total strength is unchanged, and a vertical-dominated zone has only a "razor-thin" margin when the compass direction shifts.

Declination, the angle between compass north and true north, is in his view a measure of mismatch. The body runs on two references at once: true north, fixed by the planet's spin, keeps time for body clocks and the rotation of body fluids, while magnetic north lines up electron spins. He calls the spinning planet the "stator," after the fixed part of an electric motor, and treats declination as the "error rate" between it and the wandering dynamo inside. As the gap widens, the two gears stop meshing, and the body's internal whirlpools lose their timing, so heavy hydrogen is no longer cleared.

He applies this to real places. Citing the World Magnetic Model (the official field map used for navigation) and ESA's Swarm satellites, he says the US East Coast is losing its horizontal component quickly. The geometry he prefers is the one found nearer the magnetic equator: flat field lines, a strong total field and a compass that points close to true north.

The horizontal part also explains, for Kruse, why the Southern Hemisphere has always been the harder half: field lines gather toward the north and spread apart over the south, and southern lands stayed livable only where a strong horizontal component acted as a "magnetic dam," as across Australia, a component he says is now collapsing.

#### Speed of change

Kruse puts even more weight on how fast the field changes. Geophysicists call the speeding-up of that change "secular acceleration," and sudden lurches "geomagnetic jerks." In the past, he argues, shifts were slow enough for each generation's body clock to recalibrate; today's pace is new. A chart showing a steady 50,000 nT (nanotesla, the unit of field strength) over a decade can therefore hide a jerk that tilts a horizontal field toward vertical within a year. A fixed offset between compass and true north may be survivable, he says; erratic wandering is the warning sign, because it means the outer core itself is unstable.

Behind this sits a rule he repeats often: almost every natural magnetic field lasts only as long as a conducting fluid keeps moving. Where the molten iron beneath a region slows, the field above it decays, and the surface feels it as drift and jerks.

#### What global models miss

The standard models, the World Magnetic Model and the IGRF, are smooth mathematical averages built for navigation, and Kruse says they deliberately iron out regional accelerations. Mitochondria, in his view, register the local ripple as it happens, so the models produce a "linearization mirage" of stability. He sees the real driver as the "non-dipole" field: patches at the surface of Earth's core with reversed polarity, which he says are deep and long-lived (citing de Oliveira, 2024) and speed up local decay. Declination stays useful as a shortcut; he calls it a "geographic multiplier" rather than the root cause.

Timing makes this worse. The World Magnetic Model is refreshed only every five years, while non-dipole features such as the South Atlantic Anomaly move and pulse faster, so jerks like those of 2025–2026 appear only after the models are corrected. Ground readings have their own blind spot: local iron deposits, basalt and wireless fields form what he calls a "magnetic fog" over the core's signal. Only satellites above the crust, such as Swarm, can tell a global trend from local interference. Even they record strength rather than the field's twist and coherence, which he likens to measuring a tornado with a thermometer.

#### The brain senses direction

For proof that people register field direction, Kruse cites the 2019 Caltech study by Joseph Kirschvink and Connie Wang: alpha brain waves dropped by about 25% when the direction of an Earth-strength field was rotated, with its strength unchanged. He takes this to mean that the thalamus, the brain's relay center, works as a magnetic sensor.

Heavy hydrogen, he argues, dulls that sense further. Citing an *eLife* paper, he says a radical pair (two molecules sharing linked unpaired electrons, the basis of many proposed biological compasses) grows less sensitive to weak fields as "hyperfine" interactions with nearby nuclei multiply. Deuterium's nucleus adds exactly that noise, so in his model it blinds the sensor, his "hyperfine noise trap."

#### The rock under your feet

Local geology, Kruse says, modifies all of this. Volcanic or iron-rich rock carries magnetism frozen in when it formed and can hold a flat "micro-field" at the surface even while the regional field tilts; he names El Salvador's volcanic front and the iron-rich bedrock of New York's Adirondacks. Natural currents in the crust also travel along faults, and saltwater-soaked limestone can carry electrical noise for miles. For these reasons he judges places lot by lot rather than from a national map.

He sums this up as the "local stator" rule: global averages can no longer be trusted anywhere, and the rock underfoot decides. Because cooling lava records the field of its day, old rock can work against the present field. On Hawaii's Kīlauea, he says, compasses spin or point south because older basalt below set during the reversed field before the last pole swap, about 780,000 years ago, and overpowers today's weaker field. He likens such patchworks, in Hawaii and New Zealand's North Island, to standing on a reversed battery: the mineral fuel is there but the ignition is noisy. He prefers thick crust of consistent normal polarity near zero declination, noting that Central America was laid down about 80 million years ago.

In practice, he tells people to look beneath the smoothed maps: compare Swarm data and secular-acceleration charts for their own zip code over time, and move to stable ground if the area shows a jerk or turns toward vertical. Where the local field is poor, the linked magnetic sleep pad protocol uses a static magnet pad at night as a personal stand-in, though he warns that a strong vertical field at night and a very different field by day can confuse the brain's sense of direction.
