### Grounding, and why the ground matters
- **id:** barefoot-earth
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says barefoot contact with conductive earth feeds the body electrons that keep oxygen in its safe state and drain the day's built-up charge, but how much it helps depends on the ground, your posture and the local magnetic field.
- **protocols:** barefoot-earth
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says barefoot contact with conductive earth feeds the body electrons that keep oxygen in its stable state and drain built-up charge.
  - In his model grounding fixes only the electric half of the problem; a weak or shifting magnetic field still needs addressing.
  - He says the ground matters: salt-soaked limestone near infrastructure carries stray current, while dense basalt insulates against it.
  - He prefers feet and hands on wet sand, surf, dewy grass or basalt at dawn, with as much skin as possible in open air.

Grounding, or "earthing," means skin-to-earth contact: bare feet on soil, sand or rock, or in the sea. Kruse argues it gives mitochondria a steady electrical reference that indoor life removes, and that this matters more as Earth's magnetic field weakens. In his model, though, grounding is only as good as the ground you stand on, and it cannot replace a good magnetic field.

#### What grounding does

Kruse describes the planet as a battery: the churning iron core (the geodynamo) keeps a large reservoir of free electrons at the surface, and the body is conductive enough to draw on it. Those electrons, he says, keep oxygen in its "triplet" state, its normal and stable spin arrangement, instead of letting it flip into reactive "singlet" oxygen. He adds that grounding restores Zeeman splitting (the way a magnetic field separates electron energy levels) in heme, the iron-containing core of blood and respiratory proteins, which he calls the body's magnetic antenna.

The result, in his account, is a steady electrical baseline for the inner mitochondrial membrane, the surface where energy is made, that bypasses swings in the local field. That is why he treats grounding as a stabilizer where the field is declining or shifting, and why he says sleeping grounded lets the body's night-time repair circuit run.

He borrows a picture from boatbuilding. Boat owners bond their hulls to ground so that stray currents cannot eat the metal away by galvanic corrosion (metal slowly dissolving when an unwanted current runs through it), and he sees grounding a person as the same protection. Through the day, he says, wireless fields and blue light leave the body holding excess positive charge and heat-like disorder; contact with the earth dumps that load into the soil, which he treats as the heat sink for the night's repair work.

He lists what follows. Earth electrons cancel the positive charge that builds up when cells switch into their defensive shutdown mode. Red blood cells regain the surface charge that makes them repel one another, their zeta potential, so the blood runs thinner. Cortisol settles back into its 24-hour rhythm, and mitochondria work better, clearing the way for the bicarbonate flush that carries deuterium out.

He links this to Robert Becker's "current of injury," the small natural current that steers tissue repair, and to a surgeon's chance finding that a weak current can reshape cartilage. The Hunza, he says, get such currents free from their mountains' steady natural field and the sun, which keeps their collagen and elastin supple. He pictures the grounded body as a pyramid, the brain at its apex catching light and the feet as its base on the earth; cut off the ground and, in his phrase, you become a "Maillard oven," slowly browning from inside the way food browns when cooked.

He extends the antenna idea to hair and skin, which he describes as a liquid-crystal "phased-array radar" tuned to the planet's field. Drawing on a clairvoyant's account of needle-thin blue rays (about 435–465 nm) standing at right angles from each hair even when the hair lies flat, he calls these rays a "topological surface state" and the lead wires for Becker's current. Deuterium, he says, "neutralizes" hair and thickens the skin until it carries this information poorly. Acupuncture, in his reading, works by inserting a better antenna: the metal needle acts as a "lightning rod" that re-pins the protons in local tissue water and bypasses the deuterium-frosted skin. He calls shaving a man's beard "magnetic castration," because it removes the anchor for those rays and, in his account, leaves the vagal exhaust unable to set his field right.

#### Only half the solution

Grounding fixes only half the problem, Kruse argues. Melatonin, which cells make at night as an antioxidant shield, needs both electric and magnetic containment. Grounding restores the electric side, but magnetic disorder from a weak field or wireless devices is harder to block. So in a magnetic "dead zone," or where declination changes steeply, standing on grass is not enough on its own.

#### Not all ground is equal

The type of ground matters. Kruse's concern here is not Wi-Fi, which water absorbs quickly, but low-frequency electrical noise such as power-grid leakage and stray currents. Limestone riddled with water channels and soaked with saltwater (karst) conducts that hum sideways for miles, so grounding on it near resorts or heavy infrastructure is, he says, like grounding to a live wire. Dense cities are worse still: in New York underground utilities create jump conduction into sidewalks and manholes, so bare feet on city metal mimics a weakened dynamo — he points people to bedrock parks instead. Quiet rural karst is less of a problem, but he still rates Florida and Mexico's Yucatán as poor places to ground during the decline. Dense basalt, by contrast, insulates against that sideways noise and connects you to the steady local charge of the rock.

#### Posture at dawn

How you ground matters too. At the beach he prefers only the feet and ankles in wet sand or shallow surf, with as much skin as possible in open air. Water absorbs the near-infrared light of sunrise and, he says, acts like a shield, so neck-deep immersion blocks the dawn signal the skin is trying to catch. Salty water at the shoreline, as on El Salvador's Costa del Sol, links you to the earth's steady current while bare skin charges the melanin antenna, which he says helps move heavy isotopes out through the feet.

His fullest version he calls "mammalian sphinxing": facing east at sunrise on all fours, so that hands as well as feet, with their sweat glands, touch the earth. The extra electrons, he says, let the body capture more of the dawn light, provided its water is in the structured state he scores at about 160 on the dielectric scale (a measure of how well water stores charge) rather than the 78 of ordinary water; sunlight itself pushes that number up. In his account this lowers the body's need to pull electrons from food and quiets mTOR, the cell's main growth signal, at no cost.

Even occasional contact counts in his model. People who sometimes ground or catch morning sun fare differently from those who never do, which he calls the "Earth–Sun circuit breaker": the earth supplies electrons that restore the charge of blood-vessel linings, while sunrise infrared frees cytochrome c oxidase, the mitochondrial enzyme that hands electrons to oxygen. He says people with stiff, weak collagen typically get too little of both.

In practice, the barefoot grounding protocol asks for 20–60 minutes a day barefoot in surf, wet sand or dewy grass or on basalt, ideally at dawn, with basalt or wet volcanic black sand preferred. Avoid salt-soaked limestone near resorts or big power lines, check a spot with a meter if stray current worries you, never join dissimilar metals in a grounding connection, and use a low-resistance indoor grounding system as the fallback.

- **sources:** [383534](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383534/), [383499](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383499/), [382383](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382383/), [382413](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382413/), [379936](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379936/), [382388](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382388/), [383983](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383983/), [375640](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375640/), [373432](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373432/), [371346](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371346/), [371727](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371727/), [369466](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369466/)

### Volcanic ground, basalt and passive anchors
- **id:** volcanic-basalt-pinning
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says magnetic volcanic rock locks in a flat local field and holds water and clouds in place, so grounding on basalt beats any device, and passive stone or copper anchors beat powered ones.
- **protocols:** barefoot-earth, el-salvador-guide, copper-ring-basalt, volcano-protocol
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says basalt's frozen magnetite holds a flat, stable local field at ground level even as the planet's field weakens or tilts.
  - In his model, grounding barefoot on volcanic rock charges the body's melanin antenna and helps it shed deuterium, beating any powered device.
  - He calls El Salvador a "geological ark" because of its low latitude, east–west volcanic chain and distance from the South Atlantic Anomaly.
  - He reads megaliths and pyramids as ancient attempts to copy a volcano's steadying effect on the field and on water.
  - Where volcanic ground isn't available, he prefers passive basalt, copper rings and copper–rare-earth jewelry to stimulators that push current into tissue.

"Volcano Rx" is Kruse's name for living on, and grounding barefoot on, magnetic volcanic rock. In his model basalt holds a stable, flat magnetic field at the surface even when the planet's field weakens or tilts overhead, which makes volcanic regions refuges during the current decline. He ranks this above any device and offers passive stone and copper tools for people who cannot yet live there.

#### How basalt holds a flat field

Kruse starts from the rock itself. Lava from the mantle is rich in iron, and as it cools below about 550 °C, its Curie temperature (the point below which a mineral can hold magnetism), tiny crystals of magnetite line up like compass needles with the field of that moment and keep that direction permanently. Most volcanic rock is basalt, so large volcanoes and lava tubes create strong local magnetic anomalies. Drawing on Yannick van Doorne's work, he adds that basalt's iron and magnesium make it paramagnetic, so it gathers and concentrates field lines, though only through its tie to the regional field: basalt sand spread through a house with no such connection, he says, "makes zero sense."

Where the regional field is tipping toward vertical, this frozen magnetism bends the lines flat into a horizontal micro-field at ground level, a "lithospheric straightener." Grounding barefoot on basalt then supplies, in his account, a dense stream of earth-current electrons that charge the mitochondrial membrane, help ATP synthase (the rotary enzyme that makes the cell's energy currency) repel deuterium, or heavy hydrogen, and support melanin. He calls the rock a "second battery" for the body's melanin antenna and calls PEMF (pulsed electromagnetic field) devices the "loser option."

Not every volcanic place works. Hawaii's rock, he argues, froze during the reversed field before the Brunhes–Matuyama pole swap about 780,000 years ago, so it can cancel today's field instead of adding to it; El Salvador's younger rock carries no memory of that flip. Active volcanoes pin best, because every magnetic field needs moving charge, and his own homes sit over water-filled lava tubes he likens to a dynamo. Even a good volcano helps only if it is free of heavy wireless exposure.

#### Cloud caps and black sand

Kruse reads the sky over a volcano as a gauge. Water molecules, being tiny magnets, are drawn into the field the rock gathers around the cone. A cloud that sits on the summit for long stretches, like those over Ometepe or El Salvador's volcanoes, is in his words water "pinned to the stator," shielding the people below from cosmic rays. He says the Maya chose sites near volcanoes and cenotes (natural sinkhole wells) where clouds linger, because that water is lighter in deuterium.

The coast adds a second battery. El Salvador's black-sand beaches are grains of magnetite and ilmenite that keep the field strength of the eruptions that made them, and salty waves washing over them induce a small field of their own. In his view the shoreline is a high-flux zone that resists the South Atlantic Anomaly. He rates the volcano above the beach in the dry season and expects the beach to pull ahead in the wet season, when lightning strikes the lava tubes.

#### Why El Salvador

Kruse calls El Salvador a "geological ark." It lies near the magnetic equator, where the field already runs flat; its fault zone and volcanic chain run east to west, matching those flat lines; and slowly cooled magma bodies deep below act as a hidden anchor. He adds that it has sat near an agonic line (zero declination) for about 11,500 years. Within the Central American Volcanic Arc, El Salvador packs in more volcanoes per square kilometer than its neighbors, and the arc stays clear of the South Atlantic Anomaly.

He also points to heat from below. Young lava and hot-water vents create local highs and lows in the field that, he says, filter out noise and steady the local signal. Groundwater in volcanic regions, he argues, is low in deuterium because the churning magma works for the planet the way the choroid plexus, the tissue that makes the brain's fluid, works for the body; he drills his own wells into this water. Even earthquakes help in his account, acting as a "mechanical dither" that clears background noise so the body's clock genes can pick up Earth's rhythm.

#### Ancient builders

Kruse believes earlier cultures understood this. He reads the perforated magnetite blocks at the Olmec site of San Lorenzo as a deliberate grid to restore a stable reference field when the dynamo sagged. Megaliths and dolmens of basalt and quartz-rich stone were, in his view, "paramagnetic sponges" that concentrated a weakening field inside the chamber.

Pyramids, in his reading, were "reverse volcanoes." The Maya, he says, began in volcanic El Salvador and, as they moved north away from the volcanoes, built stepped forms (he cites ~70° faces) to copy their effect. Built over cenotes and aquifers, and finished with limestone stucco that he says carried rare-earth dust from asteroid-scarred ground, such structures in his model spun deuterium out of the water table, leaving lighter water for drinking and crops when paired with copper. At Tikal he says they purified pool water with rare-earth minerals and zeolite, and that assays later found cyanobacteria, deuterium and mercury there. At Chichén Itzá's ball court he reads rare-earth–loaded plaster as a magnetic–acoustic chamber, the effect he copies with volcanic rock at his clinic. When the Yucatán anomaly and rain-only cenote hydrology outran those fixes, cities such as Copán, Tikal and Chichén Itzá were abandoned; living Maya later pinned again on El Salvador's young basalt around Cuscatlán.

#### Passive anchors: stone, copper and rare earth

On his own buildings he uses laja, a rare-earth–rich volcanic stone from Guatemala and El Salvador, for magnetic-acoustic and magnetic-electric effects like Mayan ball-court plaster. For people not yet on volcanic ground, he prefers passive tools to powered stimulators that push "harsh, non-native" current into tissue. While grounded, rest a smooth basalt stone or volcanic clay behind the ear or on the temple, which he calls a "zero-frequency amplifier," or keep a paramagnetic stone in the pillow. Copper enters in two lineages he now teaches together: Georges Lakhovsky's open rings (ends unjoined) as a Schumann / vagal antenna, and Slim Spurling's closed copper coils, which he links to post-Carrington vineyard hacks that kept oxygen triplet and water light around living tissue. For travel and other ungrounded hours he adds jewelry that mixes copper with a rare-earth such as neodymium — elements he says hold a static magnetic field even when ambient flux is thin — so body electrons stay polarized toward triplet oxygen. The neck remains his preferred target, his "vagal vortex."

In practice, the barefoot grounding protocol says to ground on basalt or wet volcanic black sand. The El Salvador guide lists his preferred spots near active volcanoes such as Ilopango and says to test any lot with mosquitoes and candles before buying. The basalt-and-copper-ring protocol covers the stone, open or closed copper, rare-earth travel jewelry and pillow options, which he frames as a bridge while you fix your location. His Volcano Protocol copies a volcano's answer at body scale, pairing life in a pinned, low-latitude place and sunrise grounding on volcanic ground with steps to shake deuterium from the spine and build up carbon dioxide.

- **sources:** [382254](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382254/), [376081](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/376081/), [382148](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382148/), [382976](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382976/), [379946](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379946/), [384666](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384666/), [380926](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380926/), [375475](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375475/), [378274](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378274/), [376035](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/376035/), [372056](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372056/), [370211](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370211/)

### Choosing a place to live
- **id:** choosing-a-location
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse calls location the final arbiter of mitochondrial health and gives three field numbers, a gravity-and-light case for the tropics, a way to read the declination map, geology to avoid and on-the-ground tests for choosing it.
- **protocols:** relocation-checklist, el-salvador-guide
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says where you live decides how hard your mitochondria must work, and no diet or supplement can outrun land with a failing field.
  - He looks for high field intensity, flat inclination and near-zero declination, ideally where an agonic line meets volcanic ground under strong sun.
  - In his model the tropics win because weaker gravity, steady strong light and a flat field all lower the cost of shedding deuterium.
  - He rules out stressed fault zones, the South Atlantic Anomaly and wireless-heavy "smart" homes, and tests a lot with mosquitoes and candles.

For Kruse, where you live finally decides how well your mitochondria can run: no supplement, diet or exercise plan can outrun land that has lost its magnetic footing. He gives three numbers to look up, a way to read the declination map and tests for a specific lot, and he says your location sets how hard you must work every day to stay well.

#### The three numbers

His checklist uses the World Magnetic Model, the official field map. Total intensity, the field's strength in nanotesla (nT), should be as high as possible: above about 45,000 nT, ideally up to 60,000. Inclination, the angle at which field lines dip into the ground, should be 0–45°; flat lines, he says, anchor the body's internal axes, while lines plunging steeply toward the poles destabilize them. Declination, the angle between compass north and true north, should be close to 0°. A line of zero declination is called an agonic line, and on it there is no twist between the planet's spin axis and its field. He also says to avoid the South Atlantic Anomaly, a huge weak patch that, he says, now reaches the Caribbean.

The best places, in his model, are where an agonic line crosses a volcanic chain at a latitude with strong sun: his "fountain of youth coordinate." He prefers young volcanoes that still hold a strong horizontal field along the Pacific Rim — Bali through Central America toward Panama — and treats South America's rim as blocked while the South Atlantic Anomaly dominates it. That corridor cannot absorb mass migration. Agonic lines move about 55 km a year with the poles, so a good 2026 spot may fail in 2027; tens of yards off the zero line can add about 1% declination — his Marcus "car crash." He warns against chasing an agonic line alone (his "Mayan trap"): marry field to sun.

#### Why the tropics: gravity, light and noise

Kruse adds a reason for the tropics that has nothing to do with the field: gravity. Because the planet spins, it bulges at the equator, so gravity there pulls slightly more weakly than near the poles. Deuterium weighs twice as much as ordinary hydrogen, and he argues that pushing it out of cells and body fluids costs more energy the harder gravity pulls.

He frames this with Maxwell's demon, the imaginary gatekeeper from physics that sorts fast molecules from slow ones. Sorting is never free: Landauer's principle holds that handling information always gives off some heat. In his model each cell is a demon sorting heavy hydrogen from light, and the equatorial bulge is the planet's own, larger demon that lowers the price.

Above roughly 35° latitude, he says, people are squeezed from two sides. Ultraviolet light and the short-wave infrared he prizes are weak or missing for much of the year just where gravity is strongest, so cells must move heavy hydrogen "uphill" with little light to power them. Wireless fields add a third load: in the demon picture they are pure noise the cell must sort, at a heat cost. Weak gravity, steady strong light and a flat field together are why, for him, the tropics always come out ahead.

Altitude counts against a place as well. Cosmic rays, and the tritium they make, increase with height, so within the good zone he favors living nearer sea level. Only some maternal lineages, in his view, cope with high mountain life.

#### Reading the maps

In his reading of the declination map, green (the agonic line) is best — the zero-degree path that, in his model, lines up cristae and inner-mitochondrial junctions for optimal energy. Red (east) is mixed and bold dark blue (strong west) is very bad. Where the lines bunch, the field can change over tens of miles and warp membranes as you move; that alone is a reason to leave. He treats ESA Swarm / World Magnetic Model maps as a picture of that geometry and compares the same date across years.

The standard UV index, he says, is useless for this, because it ignores declination and the local deuterium load. Sydney and Perth can share a UV reading, yet in his view Sydney, under steadier field conditions, works like a "quantum processor," while Perth, on the approach of the South Atlantic Anomaly, is a "Maillard oven" that browns tissue. Deserts give strong light but a weak magnetic background, and dry sand gives no ground, so he advises against raising children there.

#### Faults and plate boundaries

The geology beneath a region can rule it out. Kruse singles out the New Madrid Seismic Zone between Memphis and St. Louis, which sits on a failed rift over hot, wet, weak mantle. Unlike the frozen magnetism of old lava, he calls it an active "induction zone": under crustal stress, he says, the fault gives off broad-band electromagnetic signals, so the local field turns spiky and the body's repair window unreliable. Watching the zone affect people during 18 months in nearby Cape Girardeau confirmed his earlier move out of middle Tennessee. He is similarly wary of the Azores, where three plates meet.

#### Testing a lot

Before buying land, Kruse reads what lives there. Mosquitoes active by day as well as night, a 24-hour beeswax candle that burns out early and a faltering local water cycle all warn of a weak spot; he used mosquitoes and candles to choose his own property. A "smart" house full of wireless devices, he says, cancels even a good location.

#### Where he says no, and where he says yes

He still rules out California's "double deficit" of high declination and weak steady UV-B — Malibu around −13° — and treats deserts as magnetically poor even under strong UV. From 2024–2026 he ranks the West Coast better than the East because the Pacific thermohaline still runs while the Atlantic (AMOC) and Southern Ocean have stalled; Florida's lost oranges since about 2012 mark the same signal, and he now says the East Coast's vertical field drags land as far as the Mississippi into decline. Texas stays weak: Swarm shows its vertical field far above its horizontal. His longer no-list includes Colorado, Hawaii, Minnesota, Canada, the UK, Germany, Mexico's Yucatán and Australia; South America is most dangerous as the anomaly grows. In the US only the Pacific Northwest is really pinned (a growing offshore lava dome). His own choice remains El Salvador's volcanic coast over Yucatán cenotes, avoiding crowded wireless beaches like El Zonte.

#### After the move

Before a permanent move he often recommends a two-to-three-month stay in a candidate place, so the body can answer whether a city such as New York, Chicago or Miami is still livable. After moving, he warns people can feel worse first. Borrowing Becker's "adaptive healing," he says tissue has to rebuild for the new environment. Northern lineages should adapt to tropical sun gradually.

In practice, the relocation checklist gathers his criteria: the three World Magnetic Model numbers, avoiding the South Atlantic Anomaly, reading declination and Swarm maps, steering clear of stressed faults such as New Madrid, grounding on basalt or wet black sand, testing lots with mosquitoes, candles and the water cycle, a trial stay of months when possible, no smart house, and ground-floor living. The El Salvador guide adds his local notes: buy an empty lot and build, favor areas near active volcanoes, and use the Costa del Sol beach for sunrise grounding.

- **sources:** [381530](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381530/), [387653](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/387653/), [382792](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382792/), [379985](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379985/), [387960](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/387960/), [382918](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382918/), [384118](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384118/), [381025](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381025/), [382976](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382976/), [373200](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373200/), [372865](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372865/), [369698](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369698/)

### Electroculture: growing food in a weakening field
- **id:** electroculture-food
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse says a weakening magnetic field and deuterium-heavy water are cutting crop yields, and that electroculture, basalt and low-deuterium water can keep food growing where the field still holds, while melanized fungi keep the rain coming.
- **protocols:** zip-code-food
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says a weakening field and deuterium-heavy soil water slow photosynthesis, and that lighter water raises crop yield by about 40%.
  - In his model, buried magnetic antennas and basalt create soil currents and gradients that push deuterium aside, echoing Steiner and Schauberger.
  - He reads magnetic C4 crops giving way to C3 crops, and an eastward-moving 100th meridian, as signs of the field's decline.
  - He sees melanized fungi seeding rain as nature's backup, and says electroculture only pays where the field still holds.

Kruse argues that plants suffer the same physics he describes in human mitochondria. As Earth's field weakens, soil water carries more deuterium (heavy hydrogen), photosynthesis slows and harvests shrink. He says lowering the deuterium in a plant's water raises yield by about 40%, and that raising it costs about the same. Electroculture, the practice of using buried wires, magnets, antennas and stone to shape the electrical and magnetic environment of soil, is in his view a practical, decentralized way to keep food growing through the decline.

#### Deuterium and the harvest

Deuterium in a plant's water, Kruse says, causes the same "kinetic isotope drag" it causes in people: enzymes slow, proteins and their water shells stiffen, protons tunnel less easily and the chloroplasts (the plant's light-harvesting compartments) lose efficiency. Lighter water reverses this. The plant makes metabolic water faster, its water holds charge better, it takes up nutrients more readily and it photosynthesizes more efficiently, which he gives as the basis of his 40% figure.

He expects photosynthesis itself to come under attack. Over the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), the large weak patch in Earth's field, more particle radiation gets through, loading air and water with heavier hydrogen and raising reactive oxygen in chloroplasts. A weak field also disturbs the plant's own magnetic sensors, the cryptochromes, and the iron–sulfur proteins of its electron chain. Plants, in his account, pay the same entropy price that mammals pay.

#### How electroculture works in his model

He points to Yannick Van Doorne's magnetic antenna: a galvanized steel wire laid north–south 20–30 cm underground and connected to a beeswax-coated magnet with its north pole facing geographic north. Van Doorne reports yield gains of 30% or more in the first year, building over three to five years. Kruse's explanation is that such antennas set up tiny vortices, telluric currents (natural ground currents) and magnetic gradients in the soil water that push the heavier deuterium aside, a plant version of the burst of deuterium-depleted metabolic water that ketones give a person.

He traces the roots to Rudolf Steiner's biodynamic farming and Viktor Schauberger's work on vortexing water and avoiding steel tools that disturb its structure. Neither used the word deuterium, he says, but both were chasing the same coherent, low-deuterium water. He adds that placing basalt, or minerals such as jade, in particular geometries raises the dielectric constant (charge-holding capacity) of water in soil or in the body. Steiner knew it, he says, and growers will have to relearn it to feed people in a magnetic decline. The pyramids of the ancient world, built over aquifers and cenotes, are in his reading the monumental version: devices that concentrated the field into the water table to yield lighter water for crops.

#### Plants as field sensors

Kruse treats plants as living sensors of what he calls the "Universal Stator," his term for the field that organizes living systems. Building on a member's note that the ash of C4 plants is far more magnetic than that of C3 plants, he says C4 species such as maize, sorghum and tropical grasses carry two to four times more iron oxide and raise the soil's magnetic susceptibility (how strongly it responds to a magnet) by 30–40% after burning. He calls them "magnetic athletes," built for a strong dynamo. C3 crops such as wheat and rice are the plant kingdom's "safe mode," and their dominance on the Great Plains is, to him, the signal of the field's "Siberian Jerk" and the SAA's spread, compounded by the oxygen-poor, isotopically heavy water of a failing Atlantic overturning current (AMOC). Glyphosate, he says, chelates (binds and strips) the soil's magnetic iron; it keeps the crops alive but leaves them high in deuterium and low in what he calls magnetic torque.

He reads the map the same way. John Wesley Powell marked the 100th meridian as the line between the humid East and the arid West, a warning Kruse dates to 1860, a year after the Carrington Event. Powell took the line as fixed; Kruse says it is tied to the dynamo and is now moving east; he notes that tornado alley has shifted from Tulsa toward Nashville and that in 2025, for the first time, most confirmed US tornadoes struck east of the Mississippi. The Ogallala aquifer running dry forces surface water that evaporation has made heavy; Texas's Edwards Aquifer and springs such as Barton Springs, he says, face the same emptying and deuterated rain; the Mississippi valley's water is more deuterium-heavy than ever, so the food it grows will be too — the same deuteration he blames for Florida's lost orange industry.

#### Fungi as the backup rainmakers

When the field fails, Kruse says, the biosphere reroutes through older, radiation-hardened life. He cites a new Science Advances paper, summarized in Anton Petrov's April 24, 2026 video and backed by work in PNAS, reporting that Mortierellaceae fungi release soluble ice-nucleating proteins into the soil. The fungi acquired the gene for these proteins from bacteria (a relative of the bacterial InaZ gene) by horizontal gene transfer. The proteins form complexes of more than 100 units, drift into the air and seed ice, and so rain, at warmer temperatures than volcanic mineral dust, and could one day replace silver iodide in cloud seeding.

His addition is the "missing layer": melanin-armored fungi survived the weak-field intervals of the Neoproterozoic, about a billion years ago, and keep the water cycle running when the dynamo falters. Fungi weather basalt aggressively, so he expects basalt soils and melanized fungi to become nature's geoengineering stack in a decline, with measurably more soil fungi inside the anomaly. Mammals, by contrast, have no such backup and pay what he calls a "POMC/SCN time-over-energy tax."

#### Where it works

Electroculture is not a fix everywhere. Asked whether biodynamic vortexed preparations, spiral horns or field-broadcasting "towers of power" could help farms, Kruse says they become superfluous where the field has already gone; the wise watch what happens to living things in the ground and adjust. He sends growers to basalt-rich regions with magnetic promise such as El Salvador, where he says C4-type life still thrives without chemical help, and no longer treats high-latitude basalt such as Iceland as a place to relocate. He says two growers he lectured, Billy and Logan, tested his claims in the US, one of them in North Carolina, in what he calls "magnetic excursion land," found them borne out, and now plan to bring their methods to El Salvador, his "ARK," which he predicts will become North America's new Midwest.

#### In practice

Kruse's zip-code-food approach applies here: eat food grown where the field and water are good, and avoid crops from weak-field zones. He says he would not eat from the Canadian prairie or the US breadbasket now and that North Americans should be eating from the Pacific. For growers in a still-good field, he points to electroculture antennas, basalt in the soil, low-deuterium water and vortexed water, with setups mapped against SAA contours and basalt soils. Where the field has already failed, he says the better move is to relocate rather than spend on vortex preparations and towers. The same deuterium-depletion tools he uses for people, ketones from fat and deuterium-depleted water, are in his view the human side of the same fix.

- **sources:** [372148](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372148/), [372060](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372060/), [372061](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372061/), [372087](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372087/), [372758](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372758/), [373699](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373699/), [373701](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373701/), [373702](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373702/)

### Haplogroup and the "magnetic zipcode"
- **id:** haplogroup-zipcode
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says your maternal mitochondrial lineage sets the gearing of your energy engine and so decides which local field and sun suit you, that a mismatch speeds aging, and that as the field grows turbulent, location matters more than lineage.
- **protocols:** four-phase-sun-day, relocation-checklist
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says your maternal haplogroup is the blueprint of your energy engine and your local field, your "magnetic zipcode," is the dial.
  - In his model each lineage set ATP synthase's gearing for the light and field where it settled, so a mismatch makes the engine misfire.
  - He ranks lineages by how they tolerate a weakening field, and says the mismatch can run both ways, from too little sun to too much.
  - As the field turns turbulent, he argues, where you live now matters more than your lineage.
  - Northern lineages moving to the tropics should build a "solar callus" gradually, never pushing sun exposure to redness.

A haplogroup is a maternal lineage traced through mitochondrial DNA, which passes from mother to child. Kruse calls it the blueprint and your "magnetic zipcode," the field where you live, the dial. When the two don't match, he argues, aging speeds up, so the same place can suit one person and harm another.

He frames it as a chain: the haplogroup is the blueprint, Marcus theory (the physics of how fast electrons jump between molecules) is the law, the magnetic zipcode is the dial, and energy failure is what follows a mismatch. Lineages arose, he says, as answers to past changes in Earth's field. As people left Africa for colder climates, the M and N lineages picked up changes that let some of the mitochondria's energy leak away as heat instead of being stored, a trait called "uncoupling" that helps in the cold.

He builds on Doug Wallace's map of how mitochondrial lineages spread across the globe, reading each one as an electrical adaptation to the field and sunlight of the place it settled. He calls this impedance matching, the engineer's term for tuning a circuit to its power source so little energy is lost. Moving a lineage built for strong light and field into a weak-field, wireless-heavy setting, in his view, makes its engine misfire.

#### Isotopic gearboxes

Kruse calls lineages "isotopic gearboxes." Each one, in his model, set the gear ratio of ATP synthase, the cell's rotary energy motor, to suit local conditions. Tropical L lineages run it flat out, at a speed he puts at 9,000 RPM, which needs strong light and free-flowing, light water; northern lineages such as H and J are deliberately "leaky," dropping to about 6,000 RPM and turning the difference into heat.

In his recovery road map he sorts lineages into three kinds of engine. Equatorial and African L lineages are high-voltage engines that need the most UV and infrared, and they falter in the north's artificial-light "electronic winter." H, J, U and T are "internal heaters," built to uncouple and make their own light; they need cold exposure to set that off, or, he says, deuterium settles in the fourth ventricle. Indigenous American and East Asian lineages (A, B, C and D) are high-pressure "centrifuges" suited to the Ring of Fire, needing volcanic ground and a strong field to keep the brain's fluid vortex turning.

#### Different tolerances

Kruse ranks lineages by how they cope as the field weakens. African L lineages, especially L1, evolved near the equator, where the field is flat and naturally weak, so in his model they function with little magnetic help. Declination is a separate matter: for an L lineage, he says, even 2–4° of offset between compass and true north can stall the engine.

The European H lineage, his own, is "leaky": when magnetic pinning slips it can drop its voltage and shed the surplus as heat, which he says clears deuterium and resets the cell. East and Southeast Asian D5b1 and F lineages are the most vulnerable. They stayed where strong sun and the dense magnetic pinning of the Pacific "Ring of Fire" volcanoes were constant, so they have neither L1's low-field tolerance nor H's heat release.
Mismatch runs both ways. Move an L-lineage person to Canada, with its high declination and weak UV, and the fast engine stalls. Northern lineages placed under strong UV or heavy radiation, such as the anomaly's fringe in Australia or Brazil, overheat instead into what he calls a "singlet flashover" and age faster; for this reason he warned one H-lineage member not to move to Hawaii. As the field shifts, he adds, places that once supported a full-speed engine now support only the slower one.

For D5b1 and F in a weak field he says cold therapy backfires. They should avoid deep cold (water around 55–65 °F) until they are magnetically pinned, first maximizing morning UV and infrared light, grounding fully and shielding strictly from wireless fields.

#### When the field turns, place beats lineage

Under a stable field, Kruse says, matching your lineage to your latitude was enough. With the north pole racing toward Siberia and the anomaly deepening, the planet's magnetic rhythm now wobbles like a slipping timing belt, and Wallace's map now tells him less about where to live than about who will falter first — the environments those lineages evolved in are no longer the planet underfoot. The more turbulent the field becomes, the less a lineage protects you and the more of your environment you must get right, so in most cases, in his view, where you live now matters more than your haplogroup.

#### Running the numbers

Kruse turns this into a personal sum with three inputs: latitude and altitude (how much sun you get and how close you sit to the radiation belts), local field strength and haplogroup. Below roughly 30,000 nT, as under the South Atlantic Anomaly, he says cells need a longer quiet spell to complete repair. If a place demands about seven seconds of that quiet time and your body can hold only three, you must cut your deuterium load, and he says to redo the sum every month. A strong field alone is not enough if the sun is too weak for most of the year to keep melanin production running.

He has grown wary of commercial DNA-testing companies and offers a functional test in their place. If your body "switches on" under the strong ultraviolet found between about 20°N and 20°S, and its systems run freely when you ground on volcanic soil, you have found what he calls your "Home Frequency."

#### Northern bodies and the field

Kruse says northern peoples shed skin melanin, the pigment he treats as a spin filter, and leaned on strong polar field lines instead. He calls this "Zeeman outsourcing," after the Zeeman effect by which a magnetic field splits electron energy levels. A strong polar field plus cold, he argues, let them grow larger, using the body as a bigger solar panel. Paler irises, in his view, let more weak northern light reach the brain's master clock.

#### Moving to the tropics

A northern lineage moving into strong tropical sun must, in his view, adapt gradually. A sudden plunge into equatorial UV can overwhelm a leaky H mitochondrion, while stepping through the day's light and grounding on basalt or wet black sand builds a "solar callus," skin and tissue adapted to strong sun, without changing DNA. Redness, he says, means you pushed too far. That adaptation runs through MITF, the pigment cells' control switch, and takes days to months; a lineage itself changes only over many generations.

In practice, the four-phase sun day protocol lays out that schedule: sunrise red and infrared light with feet on basalt or wet black sand, mid-morning UV-A, a few minutes of midday UV-B that grows slowly as you tan, and late-day sun, never to the point of redness. The relocation checklist reminds you that your maternal haplogroup sets how much declination you can tolerate and that latitude, field strength and lineage have to be weighed together.

### The South Atlantic Anomaly, muons and failing regions
- **id:** magnetosphere-saa-deuterium
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says Earth's weakening field, worst under the expanding South Atlantic Anomaly and across the Southern Hemisphere, lets in particles that load the body with deuterium and tritium, and names the regions he sees failing.
- **protocols:** track-your-field
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says Earth's magnetic shields are failing together, and the damage is worst under the growing South Atlantic Anomaly.
  - In his model the thinner shield lets in muons that cancel membrane charge and let deuterium settle in nerves and the brain's fluid.
  - He says a weak field also raises deuterium and tritium in rain, air and food, so he avoids food grown under the anomaly.
  - He no longer considers the Southern Hemisphere habitable long term and names US regions he calls "cooked."
  - He says to track your own zip code's field data and move before official charts catch up.

Kruse argues that Earth's magnetic shield is weakening and that the damage is worst under the South Atlantic Anomaly, a huge and growing patch where the field is weakest. In his model the thinner shield lets in particles that load bodies, water and food with heavy hydrogen, and he names the regions he expects to fail first.

#### Failing shields

Kruse says three "cosmic shields" are failing together: a filter of interstellar hydrogen around the solar system, the heliosphere (the Sun's protective bubble) and the geodynamo (the field made by Earth's core). He puts the loss so far at about 10% of field strength since about 2012 — a sprint he equates with roughly 320 million years of Cambrian-scale change packed into about fourteen years — falling roughly 5% a decade from a decline he dates to 1859, when the magnetic pole began moving fast. Another 10–30% drop would, he says, be an evolutionary bottleneck, and his main worry is a 30–50% loss within 50 years.

 Comparing a 2005 survey map with the 2025 World Magnetic Model, he notes the north magnetic pole heading for Siberia and the anomaly still deepening. The pole has also crossed the prime meridian, a first in recorded history, and he says that shift alters declination everywhere, moving the "carrier wave" the body's water uses to orient itself so the fourth ventricle's spinning flow struggles to stay in step with the planet.

The body is meant to cope with some of this radiation. In his model melanin converts incoming radiation into a steady direct current cells can use. When blue light has detuned that pigment and the body's water has stiffened to its ordinary state, the same radiation instead becomes a mutagen, something that damages DNA, which he places at the root of the body's breakdown.

#### The anomaly

The South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA) is where the shield is thinnest, below about 22,000 nT against the 45,000 nT or more he looks for. Citing ESA's Swarm satellites, he says it has split into two lobes: the main low drifting west over South America, deepest near Paraguay and southern Brazil, and a second stretching toward southwest Africa. He says it now covers all of Argentina, and its northern edge reaches the Lesser Antilles, which is why he treats the Caribbean as off-limits. Under it, he says, the radiation belts reach down to about 200 km, so residents live in low-orbit conditions without knowing it: "astronauts walking on Earth."

 He explains its location by heat flow: the South American landmass and the Andes, he argues, insulate the core beneath them so the dynamo stalls there, leaving the Western Hemisphere over a "stalled stator."

#### Muons and deuterium

His key study is a paper by Augusto and colleagues. Under the anomaly the "cutoff rigidity," the minimum energy a cosmic particle needs to get through the field, collapses, and muons (heavy cousins of electrons made when cosmic rays strike the air) rise up to tenfold at ground level. Muons pass through walls and tissue, and in his model act as positive "dopants" that cancel the negative charge of cell membranes and knock light hydrogen out of its channels, so deuterium (heavy hydrogen) builds up in the basal ganglia, heart and liver.

He describes a healthy field as a "janitor" that sweeps deuterium to the outer edge of the body's whirlpools, such as blood flow and spinal fluid, where it can be cleared. Under the anomaly the janitor is "on strike": deuterium settles in nerves and in the brain's fluid spaces, and the body's water slides from its structured state (160 on his scale) toward ordinary water (78).

The lower belts also, he says, change how moisture is ionized in the air, raising deuterium in rain and so in grass and groundwater. A weaker field also lets the solar wind energize hydrogen ions high in the atmosphere until they escape into space; the lighter hydrogen goes first, so the water left behind grows steadily richer in deuterium, a "heavy water trap." The Atlantic's great overturning current (AMOC) runs straight through the anomaly, which he calls a "ground fault" that makes it harder for the current to sink near the poles; a stagnant ocean means stagnant air and deuterium-heavy rain.

#### Tritium

Cosmic neutrons striking nitrogen make tritium, the radioactive form of hydrogen, which rains onto South American farms and rivers. As the shields thin, he says, people breathe in more heavy hydrogen just as their means of clearing it is failing; he calls this "isotopic back-pressure" and says wear that once took 70 years to show now appears in people in their thirties. If deuterium is grit in the engine, tritium in his phrase is radioactive shrapnel. The body builds it into DNA as if it were ordinary hydrogen; when it decays into helium, which cannot bond, the chemical link snaps from the inside.

For this reason he avoids food from the SAA, and he says seeds germinate poorly in its weak field. Heavier water slows the enzymes of photosynthesis, he says, while extra cosmic radiation damages chloroplasts. The deep sea, whose "fossilized" light water has been cut off from the surface for centuries, he sees as a cleaner food web.

#### The Southern Hemisphere

Kruse treats the whole Southern Hemisphere as exposed. Field lines gather toward the north, his "input," and spread out over the south, the "exhaust," so as the main field weakens the anomaly behaves like a low-pressure hole drawing deuterium and tritium down from the upper atmosphere, which he calls "isotopic insufflation." With the radiation belts lower there since 1859, he says, the southern food chain has been loading with deuterium for about 150 years. He no longer considers anywhere in the Southern Hemisphere habitable for the long term, and he says the north magnetic pole's rush toward Siberia, his "Siberian jerk," adds strain to the southern weak spot like one side of a tug-of-war.

#### Who fails first

Zip codes near the expanding anomaly, Kruse predicts, will be the first to run out of energy, with lives shortening where magnetic flux is weakest. By late 2026 he treats the US East Coast as already under the anomaly's reach — up toward Hudson Bay — while ranking the West Coast relatively better than the East for 2024–2026 because the Pacific thermohaline still runs; California still carries a "double deficit" of high declination and weak steady UV-B, and Texas stays weak. Florida, he says, was "swallowed" in about 14 years. He names the Pacific Northwest as the only US option that is really pinned. The fittest, he says, feel it first: high-demand athletes, whose engines run closest to the limit, are the canaries.

In practice, the track-your-field protocol says to watch declination drift, secular-acceleration ("jerk") charts and Swarm data for your own zip code, comparing the same date across years, along with unusual blocked weather and your own heart-rate variability. If your area shows a jerk or turns toward a vertical field, move before official charts catch up. He also keeps a watch-list for a bigger shift: field loss reaching 30–50% of the Laschamp collapse, sudden changes in the SAA and faster pole drift.

- **sources:** [384286](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384286/), [383962](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383962/), [380918](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380918/), [384118](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384118/), [374212](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374212/), [373833](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373833/), [373835](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373835/), [378213](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378213/), [374211](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374211/), [372042](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372042/), [370220](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370220/)

### Solar storms, the Schumann signal and the "thalamic GPS"
- **id:** solar-flares-space-weather
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says the thalamus works as a position sensor locked to Earth's field and resonance, which solar storms and high declination knock out of alignment, and that each storm is a stress test the body's water passes or fails, with effects on sleep, mood, hearing and even earthquakes.
- **protocols:** space-weather-days, brain-eye-ear-conditions
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says the thalamus works as a "thalamic GPS" that locks onto Earth's field and the 7.83 Hz Schumann resonance.
  - In his model a solar storm jams that signal, thickens spinal fluid and makes the cell's rotary motor lose its magnetic lock.
  - He treats each storm as a stress test: a body low in deuterium rides the wave, while a deuterium-loaded one cannot absorb the jolt.
  - High local declination, he says, does the same thing slowly by denying the thalamus a stable reference.
  - On storm days he says to ground for longer than usual and expect a rough night or two.

Kruse argues that the brain keeps track of where the body is by locking onto Earth's magnetic field and its natural electromagnetic rhythm. Solar storms, and more slowly a skewed local field, knock that internal position sense out of alignment, which in his model explains storm headaches, drained energy on storm days and the unease some people feel in open spaces.

#### The Sun and the thalamic GPS

Kruse treats the Sun as one node on a galactic electrical circuit, with its 11-year cycle an induced response. In the body the receiver is the thalamus, the brain's relay center. He calls it a "thalamic GPS," a three-axis position panel whose axes he places along the sphenoid bone at the base of the skull, the vagus nerve and the heart. It locks onto the Schumann resonance, the 7.83 Hz electromagnetic hum of the space between the ground and the upper atmosphere — the cavity tone made when solar wind and lightning drive the ionosphere — and onto the horizontal and vertical parts of the field. He adds that the thalamus itself emits near that same 7.83 Hz, so every living thing, in his telling, is tuned to Earth's guitar-body rhythm; cut the lock and man-made fields find an open cockpit. The brain's alpha rhythm, around 10 Hz, acts in his model like a strobe that keeps cell membranes aligned.

#### What a storm does

A solar flare or coronal mass ejection (CME, a burst of charged solar material) compresses the magnetosphere, the field's protective bubble, and drives an induced surge through the ground. Kruse says this jams the Schumann signal and uncouples the thalamic GPS, disturbing alpha waves. Cerebrospinal fluid, in his account, loses its charge, thickens and drains poorly, so pressure inside the skull rises; hence storm headaches. He tells members that a bad night can come from a CME rather than from what they ate.

He describes the cell's side of this as a motor slipping. Earth's field is the outer "stator" and ATP synthase, the cell's rotary motor, is the inner rotor; a storm makes the stator turbulent and the rotor loses its magnetic lock. He gauges storms by the K-index, a 0–9 scale of how disturbed the field is: from about 5 upward, he says, the field that normally guides charged particles in the spinal fluid turns chaotic, the fluid's smooth whirl breaks into turbulence and heavy water settles back into the brain's fluid spaces. Storm currents also run through the body's own circuits, not just power lines, pushing against the motor's rotation, which in his account is the drained feeling and "cognitive haze" people report on storm days.

Storms change the light as well. The upper atmosphere sinks during a storm, he says, shifting the mix of ultraviolet that reaches the ground and scattering the photons the skin's pigment system depends on, an effect he calls "atmospheric tanning."

Melatonin takes a hit too. About 95% of it, he says, is made inside mitochondria, and he cites a paper showing levels fall 15–33 hours after a geomagnetic spike, which he calls an "isotopic hangover." Mood shifts with it, he adds, pointing to Robert Becker's 1960s work and Soviet research gathered under the heading of heliogeophysics, both of which tracked human behavior against storms.

The ground answers as well. He calls earthquakes detectors of magnetic anomalies, and cites 2025–2026 statistical studies finding that quakes worldwide rise about 27–28 days after major geomagnetic storms; the storm's currents in the ground, he says, seep into fragile faults and warm them.

He adds that the field also shifts over each ordinary day. Sunlight charges the upper atmosphere, whose currents nudge the ground field by roughly 10–100 nT; after sunset those currents fade and the field settles to a quieter night baseline. As an extreme storm he cites the 1859 Carrington Event, when auroras reached the Caribbean, and ties it to the collapse of European vineyards in the years that followed. He also suspects that storm permanently shifted the background Schumann hum, and that once alternating-current power added its own noise in the early 1900s, the heart's internal positioning lost its lock. Yet he does not see a big storm as purely bad: the Sun, as the parent oscillator, may in his view send a Carrington-scale ejection that works like a defibrillator, jolting the wobbling core back into steady flow.

#### Storms as stress tests

Kruse treats each storm as a test of the body's water. A body low in heavy water, with well-structured water, "rides the wave"; one loaded with deuterium cannot absorb the jolt. In his account a storm induces an electric field in the ground, blood thickened by deuterium cannot soak up the voltage spike, and the shifting field throws off the nervous system that steadies the heart. People, in his view, now struggle with the Sun's periodic "defibrillation" because they carry too much deuterium to tolerate it. Shaking ground may help: one reason he chose El Salvador over Mexico, he says, is that its constant seismic activity may leave residents more resilient to storms.

#### Slow uncoupling from the local field

High declination, Kruse says, does the same thing slowly. A field twisted relative to true north, or changing quickly, keeps the thalamus from locking a stable reference. That lowers tone in the right vagus nerve and makes mitochondria leak DNA, which the immune system treats as an alarm. He pictures the thalamus as "overclocked," hunting for its lost position signal, much as it does for astronauts in the magnetic vacuum of space and for many people on the ground under wireless fields and the field's decline.

#### Open spaces as a navigation load

Kruse reads the unease some people feel in wide open spaces as this navigation system running short of capacity. The brain's grid cells and place cells, which map position, rely in his model on structured water held in order by the field. In ungrounded, sleep-short people living under blue light, that water slides toward ordinary bulk water and its capacity roughly halves, so position signals blur, which he calls "spatial aliasing." Open spaces carry more information than the weakened channel can handle. The answer, he argues, lies in restoring that medium.

In practice, the space-weather days protocol says to ground for longer than your usual 20–60 minutes during solar or geomagnetic storms and to expect possible storm headaches and a rough night or two; he also treats auroras seen over your own home as a sign the field beneath is poor and a reason to move. The brain, eye and ear protocol gives his steps for rebuilding the position sense: remove wireless fields, ground daily, get morning solar infrared, lower deuterium and live where the magnetic field is strong.

### The planet's story: excursions, the magnetic forge and volcanic CO₂
- **id:** geomagnetic-excursions-evolution
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse reads Earth's field history — excursions, ozone, oxygen, oceans, volcanoes and the creatures that survived past collapses — as the large-scale version of the physics that runs the cell.
- **protocols:** none
- **key points:**
  - Kruse reads Earth's magnetic history as cell physics at planetary scale: a steady field allowed complex life, a faltering one culled it.
  - In his model reversals cause extinctions by stripping ozone, while shorter excursions such as Laschamp select survivors and reshaped modern humans.
  - He says today's weakening is a "magnetic forge," a planetary re-tuning not caused by humans, about 9% of the way to a Laschamp-scale collapse.
  - He ties ocean currents, Arctic warming and volcanic CO₂ to the dynamo, and welcomes more CO₂ as a shield during field loss.

Kruse reads Earth's magnetic history as the large-scale version of the physics he sees inside cells. In his model, periods when the field steadied allowed complex life, periods when it faltered reshaped or culled it, and the weakening under way now is another such turning point: a planetary re-tuning rather than an extinction.

#### Excursions and reversals

A reversal is a full swap of the magnetic poles; it takes thousands of years and then holds for 100,000 years or more. An excursion is an aborted reversal: the field weakens and wanders for a few centuries up to about 2,000 years, then recovers, as in the Laschamp excursion about 41,000 years ago. Fast-settling lake and deep-sea sediments record hundreds of them, along with spikes in beryllium-10 and carbon-14 that mark extra cosmic rays. Reversals, in his view, cause extinctions because ozone is stripped for thousands of years; excursions select survivors. The field has collapsed about 500 times with only five mass extinctions, he says, but he believes several human civilizations were lost to such events.
He reads the Permian "Great Dying," which killed about 96% of life, as a magnetic event in which heavy isotopes piled up while the field faltered. As the field weakened, he says, oxygen sank back toward levels of a far older Earth, and he notes researchers' finding that the steadiness of the link between field and oxygen tracks the five great extinctions. In his account the Siberian Traps, the vast lava floods of that time, knocked the dynamo out of step with surface chemistry. The survivors were "hard" life that could keep its internal spin order with its own melanin and DHA, an "internal sun." Animals shrank afterward (the Lilliput effect): a smaller body, he says, lets a smaller internal vortex keep its charge.

#### A steady field made complex life

Kruse argues that complex life arrived only once Earth's dynamo steadied. During the "Boring Billion," the long, quiet middle of Earth's history, a weak and unstable field let the solar wind strip ozone, leaving the surface scorched by UV. In the Ediacaran period just before the Cambrian the field was, he says, 10–30 times weaker than today, hydrogen bled into space, water grew heavier and life stayed simple.

The Cambrian explosion began in his account when the inner core hardened, the field stabilized and oxygen locked into its stable triplet state, letting one-handed molecules such as collagen and melanin act as spin filters. He cites a 2025 *Science Advances* study linking atmospheric oxygen to the strength of Earth's field over 540 million years, with joint peaks between 330 and 220 million years ago, the window in which he says the mammalian blueprint was built. Oxygen, he stresses, did not drive that leap alone — the magnetic dynamo did; without polarized spins there is no coherent light and no complex life beyond archaea and bacteria. Under a later excursion, he says eukaryotes "travel back in time" toward simpler forms, with the circadian system as an electrical-resistance circuit protecting helical DNA while the field swings.

#### Who survives a stall

The asteroid that ended the dinosaurs is, for Kruse, a different kind of event: the field and oxygen stayed strong, so life bounced back. He expects no such rebound from a core-level stall. Asked who would make it through one, he names neither birds nor most mammals: a bird's heart, beating 600–1,000 times a minute, would in his view stall in flight with just 1% more deuterium. His survivors are older designs: crocodilians, with a heart valve they can work like an "isotopic valve" and months of fasting on tail fat; sharks, whose electrosensing organs he says couple them to the core; tardigrades; microbes at deep-sea vents; and deep-sea squid and octopus. If birds disappear, he says, the food web has become an "isotopic dead zone."

#### Laschamp and modern humans

At Laschamp, Kruse says, the field fell below 5% of normal and auroras reached the equator. He links the event to the "chromosome 2 fuse," the spot where two ancestral ape chromosomes fused in humans, which sits beside genes for melanin and metabolism; in his model it tripped and reset human metabolism. Neanderthals, he says, "short-circuited" rather than lost a war, while modern humans emerged with a larger melanin shield and a burst of cognition and cave art.

Modern humans won, in his telling, partly by shrinking. Neanderthals carried about 125 g more brain, which in a weakening field became dead weight, while a smaller brain with relatively larger fluid-filled ventricles spins its spinal fluid faster and clears deuterium better. He says human brains are shrinking again today for the same reason. Large animals fared worse: they need strong "magnetic buoyancy" to push blood against gravity, he says, which is how he explains the loss of North America's megafauna.

#### The magnetic forge today

Kruse frames current changes as planetary rebalancing, not something humans caused. He now traces the start of the decline further out: in his account the solar system has drifted into a cloud of interstellar neutral hydrogen that squeezes the sun's protective bubble, with its strongest edge reached about 1,900 years ago. Earth, he says, is leaving a sunlight-dominated era for a "magnetic forge" like Laschamp's. He puts the present at about 9% of a Laschamp-scale collapse; at 30–50% he expects Earth's core and outer shell to decouple, his "Tau" stage. By his stochastic estimate, a roughly 10% field decline multiplies biologic stress about two-and-a-half-fold — reason enough, in his view, for the scale of modern mismatch. He cites satellite work showing that molten iron under the equatorial Pacific switched from westward to eastward flow in 2010 and has weakened since about 2020.

He also sees the planet keeping a sleep–wake cycle, as Robert Becker found the body's direct current does: today's racing pole is a night-to-day flip he dates to 1859, felt by deuterium-heavy people as a kind of jet lag.

#### Oceans, weather and volcanoes

Moving, salty ocean currents such as the Atlantic overturning circulation (AMOC) make their own magnetic fields and, in his model, stall when the dynamo weakens — the planet's "vagal exhaust." He reads Arctic warming about four times faster than the rest of the world as a "magnetic Arctic flashover" as the north pole races toward Siberia, and calls "climate change" a software name for that hardware failure.

Warming seas release iodine that destroys ozone; volcanoes answer with CO₂ that locks iodine back as bicarbonate. He welcomes more CO₂ during an excursion and reads recent greening as a living shield against field loss. In practice he treats this history as a reason to act: anchor to a stable field, protect internal water, build melanin with sunlight and escape blue-lit indoor life. He rejects "balance" during an excursion, and puts location ahead of every other choice.

- **sources:** [380031](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380031/), [381061](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381061/), [378244](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378244/), [374226](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374226/), [375000](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375000/), [373841](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373841/), [381076](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381076/), [381186](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381186/), [384616](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384616/), [382178](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382178/), [371463](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371463/), [387801](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/387801/)

### nnEMF: wireless, wiring and the "hyper-local excursion"
- **id:** reduce-nnemf-block
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says man-made electromagnetic fields jam cell signalling, scramble electron spins, push heavy-hydrogen fuel into the cell and act like a local magnetic excursion, with low-frequency AC fields the first thing to check and children the most exposed.
- **protocols:** nnemf-reduction, work-space, air-tube-headphones
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says man-made fields create a "hyper-local magnetic excursion," doing around a person what the weakening planetary field does globally.
  - In his model they scramble the electron spins that set the timing of oxygen radicals, draining mitochondrial voltage and NAD⁺.
  - He names low-frequency AC magnetic fields from wiring and appliances as the first thing to check, ahead of wireless.
  - He says pulsed RF can open the blood–brain barrier and that wireless signals jam cell signalling and can fake a "phantom tan."
  - Children take the hardest hit until myelin finishes around age 25; keep devices off the head and measure before mitigating.

nnEMF is Kruse's term for "non-native" electromagnetic fields: the man-made fields from wireless devices, wiring and electronics that did not exist in nature. He argues they do locally what the weakening planetary field does globally, stripping cells of the steady magnetic reference they need, scrambling electron spins and draining cell voltage. Low-frequency fields from household wiring and appliances, he says, are the first thing to check.

#### The same problem as the planet

In Kruse's model Earth's field supplies a constant, gentle "torque" that keeps electron spins aligned inside mitochondria. Wi-Fi, smart meters and Bluetooth, he says, swamp it and create a "hyper-local magnetic excursion" around the person. Cytochrome c oxidase, the enzyme that hands electrons to oxygen, loses its alignment, oxygen flips into its reactive singlet form, and the cell behaves as if starved of oxygen even when oxygen is present, a state called pseudohypoxia. Planetary decline and nnEMF, he says, "cause the same problem," though he now ranks a roughly 10% planetary field drop as far larger than focal wireless — nnEMF is still additive, and phones hurt most near packed west-declination or anomaly lines — which is why copper–rare-earth jewelry on a plane is, for him, mandatory even if jump conduction is a worry. A cell already carrying faulty mitochondrial DNA or a heavy deuterium load is primed for this, so a router or smart meter can tip it over.

He roots this in spin chemistry. Superoxide, a common oxygen radical, forms in radical pairs whose electron spins rock between two states, and Earth's field normally sets the timing of that rocking, which lets cells use these radicals as signals. Radio-frequency fields, he says, jam the hyperfine coupling that keeps the timing, leave pairs stuck in the wrong state and let the radicals slip past the cell's own clean-up enzymes and give off disordered light. He adds that natural and artificial fields alike act through the metal centers of enzymes, whose electron energy levels are split by the protein atoms around them.

#### Low-frequency AC fields first

His first suspect is AC (alternating current) magnetic fields under 100 kHz from power lines, appliances and indoor wiring. He says they knock spin-aligned electrons out of step and drain the charge on the inner mitochondrial membrane. The resulting singlet oxygen damages the iron–sulfur clusters of Complex I, the first step of the energy chain, so NAD⁺ falls; that is the molecule that repair enzymes, sirtuins and the body clock run on. He also cites Liboff and Blackman's work on ion cyclotron resonance: a 60 Hz field layered on Earth's steady field can match the natural frequency of calcium ions and drive calcium out of place, pushing magnesium down.

He sets the grid against the body's own wiring. The vagus nerve, he says, runs coherent rhythms tuned to the Schumann resonance, while 60 Hz is a "heavy" frequency that distorts the shape of the inner mitochondrial membrane and lets deuterium in. A computer chip, he adds, moves charge but ignores electron spin, so it wastes energy as heat; melanin's spin-sorted current, by contrast, carries information with little heat.

Fields also change what the cell burns. Citing a 2011 study by Nora Volkow finding that a phone held to the head raises glucose use in the nearby brain, Kruse argues that such fields break up the thin water shells around proteins and the body responds by flooding the blood with glucose, which he regards as deuterium-heavy fuel. At Complex I, deuterium then takes ordinary hydrogen's place and, he says, slows electron tunneling six- to tenfold.

#### Wireless and the cell's own signals

5G and other wireless signals, Kruse argues, jam the terahertz signals that neighboring cells use to coordinate (ephaptic coupling, meaning communication through fields rather than synapses) and collapse the structured water around membranes. He rejects the idea that non-ionizing radiation is harmless because it cannot break atoms apart, likening it to calling a black mamba safe because it can't throw a punch. He points to a Rockefeller University patent by Jeffrey Friedman and Sarah Stanley on using radio waves to switch heat-sensitive channels in cells, as proof that fields reach the leptin pathway that governs appetite and metabolism.

Wireless signals can also, in his view, fake a sunny day. Responding to a member's paper showing that RF fields activate p53, the cell's repair guardian, he argues that cells mistake RF jitter in their water for UV damage and start making melanin through POMC, the hormone precursor sunlight normally switches on. Because the signal is incoherent, the new pigment never forms a stable lattice and is broken straight back down into dopamine; he calls it a "phantom tan" and sees in it the physics of phone compulsion: phone, repair alarm, pigment, dopamine high, crash, and back to the phone.

Children, he says, take the hardest hit. Their thinner skulls let RF reach deeper, and myelin — the fatty insulation that speeds nerve signals — is not finished until about age 25, so an immature myelin and melanin shield leaves the developing brain more open to pulsed fields. Their plentiful neural stem cells, which RF can slow, are still building the circuits that calm the brain's alarm center.

#### Barriers, pulsed fields and the head

Kruse leans on Allan Frey's work that electromagnetic radiation, especially pulsed microwaves and RF, can open the blood–brain barrier. Once that shield leaks, he says, high-atomic-mass material that does not belong in the brain can enter and ruin the fidelity of the thalamus's Schumann-tuned antenna — the same 7.83 Hz lock he calls the body's GPS. Disconnect people from that natural signal, in his account, and man-made fields have an easier path to scramble sense and timing. The cochlea's melanin sheet and the endolymphatic sac, which also lack a blood–brain barrier, are another doorway: pulsed energy there uncouples the head's antenna and loads the dense petrous bone with deuterium.

Phones and wireless earbuds sit on that doorway. They disturb the nerves that drive saliva in narrow bony canals and cancel sound therapy by adding the very fields it aims to undo. Wearables act from the inside, in his view: a tracking ring worn at night gives off enough radio energy to cut melanin production.

In practice, the nnEMF reduction protocol says to look first for AC magnetic fields and artificial blue light; not to sleep or live near smart meters, routers, 5G arrays or wind turbines; to keep phones and earbuds off the head and neck and devices off the lap; to skip wireless rings and other wearables; to avoid phones in the car, distrust airplane mode and skip smart houses; and to measure before mitigating, ideally with a credentialed assessor, because cheap fixes do little. He suggests judging a shielded home partly by digestion: its occupants should settle into a smooth, well-formed Bristol type 4 stool. The work-space protocol moves wiring, power supplies and appliances away from where you sit and favors daylight and incandescent bulbs, and the air-tube headphones protocol replaces Bluetooth with wired air-tube headphones or a speaker.
