### Drugs and supplements as "atomic impurities"
- **id:** atomic-impurities
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse says foreign compounds add electron traps to the body's semiconductors, that some drugs only seem to work in a deuterium-loaded body, and that pills skip the light-driven steps that make the body's own versions work, leaving "isotopic debt" behind good-looking lab values.
- **protocols:** pills-he-avoids
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says foreign compounds act like stray atoms in a chip, trapping electrons in the mitochondrial membrane instead of passing them on.
  - In his model some drugs only seem to help because the body is already deuterium-loaded, and turn harmful once the water is restored.
  - He argues pills skip the light-driven steps that make the body's own versions work, so good lab values can hide "isotopic debt."
  - He says synthetic molecules scatter heavy isotopes and wrong-handed forms where living enzymes would place them carefully.
  - In place of pills he puts morning sun, grounding, cold, removal of AC fields and a good location.

Kruse argues that most drugs and supplements do more harm than good because of what they do to the cell's electrical wiring. In his model the proteins of the mitochondrial membrane behave like semiconductors, passing electrons along a chain. A foreign compound acts like a stray atom in a computer chip: it traps electrons instead of passing them on. He prefers a chip-fab picture to a music metaphor: a semiconductor clean room fails if any wrong atomic mass lands on the board, and the cell's ubiquitination machinery is that clean room's janitor. When Earth's field is slowing metabolism, he says, peptides that ramp transcription floor a seized engine — worst under the South Atlantic Anomaly or in a desert.

#### Electron traps

Borrowing Ramon Sun's term, he calls such compounds "atomic impurities." His list includes GLP-1 drugs, statins, synthetic peptides, NMN and other NAD⁺ precursors, metformin, rapamycin and Epitalon. Each adds traps to a misaligned network, deepens the blood–brain-barrier leak and speeds energy loss as heat. He labels statins and GLP-1 drugs "magnetic strippers," and says no drug can fix a bad location.

In network terms, each heavy foreign atom raises the barrier between nodes, so an overdriven node still fires but its energy cannot travel on. It turns into heat and leaks out as "alien" ultra-weak light, and the body stops working as a resonant cavity and becomes a "pressure cooker."

NAD⁺, a molecule that shuttles electrons in energy production, is his clearest example. He says the ratio of NAD⁺ to NADH simply reflects the membrane's charge, so it rises without pills when you remove AC fields and add morning sun and cold. Since whole-blood NAD⁺ stays flat with age, he says, NR and NMN push the system rather than repair it.

Injected peptides cause a similar problem in his view. The body cuts its own peptides from a larger precursor on a light-timed schedule set by the brain's master clock. Flooding the blood with synthetic versions reads as a surplus, so the clock shuts down native production and the user ends up dependent on the needle. He calls Melanotan, an injected tanning peptide, a "fake battery": it forces a tan without the voltage or isotope clearance real sun brings.

#### Drugs that only seem to work

Kruse argues that several popular drugs help only because the body is already deuterium-loaded. Metformin works by slowing Complex I, the first step of the energy chain, which he says makes sense only when the cell's NADH carries heavy hydrogen. Once the cell's water is restored and deuterium is kept out of the chain, metformin becomes a poison. He applies the same logic to statins.

Methylene blue is his "ungrounded quantum bypass." It hands electrons from NADH almost straight to the end of the chain, skipping two steps. In a cell whose water is degraded, or a place where the field is unstable, he says this forces current through a system that cannot contain it. He calls its focus boost a brief rush that clears no deuterium.

Creatine, in his image, is coal shoveled into a steam engine whose pipes are clogged. Fluorinated antidepressants he calls "deuterium bombs," since fluorine is the most electron-hungry element; he says they thicken tissue fluid and lower its ability to hold charge.

#### Pills that skip the light-driven steps

Kruse says pills skip steps that sunlight is supposed to run in mitochondria. Melatonin and vitamin D pills bypass the light-driven routes that make the body's own versions work. A vitamin D pill comes without the companions that arrive with sunlight, the faint light emissions and the infrared thinning of the blood, so the body cannot put the vitamin to work in its DNA-guarding systems. Blood levels look ideal in a body that is still "welded shut." Magnesium helps at first, but a cell running at low voltage cannot hold onto it, his "missing dopant" problem.

He likens supplements to photoshopping a bank statement: they give a false sense of security while "isotopic debt," the slow build-up of deuterium, keeps growing until the body goes bankrupt.

He goes on through the list. Chronic vitamin C blocks melanin renewal, supplemental copper damages the body's own melanin, and aspirin is a mitochondrial toxin that lowers melanin. He does accept sulforaphane and allicin as short-term aids. Hair dye and bleach make his list too: he treats hair as a waveguide for light, and says dyes suppress the enzyme that makes melanin, "shaving the antenna."

#### Fever, antioxidants and hospital fluids

For about 25 years Kruse has rejected the antioxidant story. Radicals and oxygen, he says, are fuel for the cell's light-driven engine; the real problem is a weak magnetic field that cannot contain them. Isolated ascorbic acid in a low-oxygen body can turn pro-oxidant and drain glutathione, while whole-food vitamin C comes with flavonoids that let it recycle itself.

Fever, in his model, is a repair program, a "hypothalamic de-frag" that uses infrared heat to speed enzyme repair. Acetaminophen breaks it, because its breakdown product uses up glutathione.

He says MRI and CT contrast agents enter the brain and stay, and calls avoiding gadolinium one of his most important long-term rules, since a contrast scan can pin deuterium back in the brainstem after someone has cleared it. He lists thyroid hormone replacement, hyperbaric oxygen, supplemental oxygen and ozone as risky. By contrast he calls Lactated Ringer's, the IV fluid he used as a surgeon, the hospital's "stealth" deuterium-depleting fluid.

#### The isotope problem

Isotopes add a second layer. Citing Cameron Borg, Kruse says living enzymes place light hydrogen exactly where electrons and protons must tunnel and park deuterium in harmless spots. Chemical synthesis scatters heavy atoms at random, so even a "deuterium-depleted" pill can put a heavy atom on a working hinge. Whole food eaten in sunlight lets the body filter isotopes smoothly; concentrated pills force it to spend energy ejecting mismatched atoms. Handedness matters the same way: a wrong-handed molecule behaves like deuterium, breaking symmetry and making the brain's 40 Hz clock stutter.

Lithium is his sharpest case, a "subatomic monkey wrench." Natural lithium mixes two isotopes with different nuclear spins, and he says the mixed signal jams the thalamus, which he describes as an FM radio locking the body clock to Earth and Sun.

#### Reflexivity

He frames the whole category with "biological reflexivity," borrowed from George Soros's idea that investors move the markets they bet on. Every drug, and every biomarker a person chases, changes the biology it targets. The supplement trade thrives, in his view, because a shortcut is easier to sell than telling people to leave their indoor, wireless-filled routines for the light.

#### In practice

His "pills he avoids" list includes oral vitamin D3, chronic vitamin C, NMN, NR and NAD⁺ injections, metformin, rapamycin, statins and aspirin. It also covers GLP-1 drugs, Epitalon and sleep or repair peptides (he names BPC-157, CJC-1295, Ipamorelin and Pinealon), Melanotan, melatonin pills and chronic magnesium pills. Isolated antioxidants, copper supplements, lithium and modafinil round it out, along with methylene blue, creatine, fluorinated antidepressants, acetaminophen for fever, thyroid hormone replacement, gadolinium contrast where it can be avoided, and hyperbaric or supplemental oxygen. He adds hair dye and bleach. In their place he puts the inputs he says the pills imitate: morning sun, grounding, cold, removal of AC fields and a good location.

- **sources:** [371117](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371117/), [383708](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383708/), [382667](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382667/), [382155](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382155/), [379373](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379373/), [378299](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378299/), [377752](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377752/), [382656](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382656/), [374080](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374080/), [370216](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370216/), [372909](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372909/), [373079](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373079/)

### His critique of "centralized medicine"
- **id:** centralized-medicine
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse argues that modern medicine dropped physics (and even sound) for biochemistry and drugs, recast the sun as a hazard, measures too late to catch light's effects, and urges members to verify for themselves and act on light, field and ground.
- **protocols:** iv-fluids-acute-collapse
- **key points:**
  - Kruse says centralized medicine built itself on biochemistry and drugs, ignoring a deeper layer of light, water, magnetism and membrane voltage.
  - He traces the split to the 1910 Flexner Report, which in his account tied medical training to a chemistry-set model of the body.
  - In his view money flows to patentable molecules and diagnostics, while the high-leverage base of light, field, water and rhythm is neglected.
  - He argues lab values arrive too late, because light reshapes biology millions of times faster than any hormone shift can be measured.
  - He urges people to verify rather than outsource their thinking, and to act first on location, light and grounding.

Kruse uses "centralized medicine" for the mainstream system of drugs, guidelines and institutions, and argues it has built itself on the wrong layer of biology. Beneath biochemistry, in his model, runs a deeper operating system of light, water, magnetism and membrane voltage, and medicine was trained not to look at it. Understanding that history is, for him, the reason to take health into your own hands.

#### How the split happened

He traces it to the 1910 Flexner Report, funded by Carnegie and Rockefeller foundations. He grants that it improved medical training, but says it tied accreditation to a chemistry-set model of the body. That pushed out biophysics, naturopathy and chiropractic and gave rise to a drug industry that barely existed before. Illness was redefined as a local chemical shortage rather than a failure of field, spin and structure, his "pharma over physics." In his image, molecules are only the letters of biology; the light signal is the sentence and the spin of oxygen and metals is the meaning.

Sound was lost in the same shift. Hospitals built before 1900 had bells, and he says taking them out was an economic decision dressed up as science. In his reading their tones helped the nervous system shed deuterium (heavy hydrogen), and the Flexner era swapped that "acoustic medicine" for a chemical monopoly.

Dropping biophysics, he says, is what made drugs that slow the mitochondrial energy chain look necessary. It also hid the fact that the chain's final enzyme, cytochrome c oxidase, makes deuterium-depleted water so the cell can run on light hydrogen.

In physics language, he says medicine manages only the "kinetic" side of the body, calories and pills, and ignores the "potential" side: the membrane's charge, the state of cell water and the magnetic spin of the ATP motor. When that potential has collapsed from too little red and UV-A light or too much deuterium, more food only feeds a shorted circuit.

#### Following the money

Money follows the same split. He describes a health pyramid in which investment flows to drugs and diagnostics at the top, while the base with the most leverage gets little: light, magnetism, deuterium, water and daily rhythm. Medicine targets molecules, he says, because only molecules can be patented, and consensus then forms around what can be sold. Consensus is not science in his view; dissenters such as Ignaz Semmelweis get branded cranks, and prizes reward ideas that sell drugs.

The same incentives run through the wellness world. The longevity industry (David Sinclair, Aubrey de Grey and the Valter Longo fasting school) and diet experts (Ben Bikman, Peter Attia, Andrew Huberman) stay trapped in biochemical models and ignore light. Health influencers who sell subscriptions, supplements and drug or peptide regimens are, to him, "time thieves" who build dependency. He also warns that AI chatbots default to consensus and flatter their users.

#### Blind spots he names

Biochemistry, in his view, assumes Earth's magnetic field never changes, so its textbook diagrams fail once oxygen flips into its reactive state. The same mindset assumes slow, steady change and cannot see magnetic excursions. Doctors themselves, he argues, live in the same indoor, deuterium-loaded state as everyone else, so they cannot perceive the problem and reach for more drugs.

Lab testing is another blind spot. When light reaches the retina, its pigment molecule changes shape in about 200 femtoseconds, which he puts at roughly ten million times faster than a nerve impulse. Light has therefore reshaped your biology long before any hormone or "chemical imbalance" shows up, so a lab value records what he calls a "photonic crime" that is already over. That is why he says hormone panels cannot steer a fix for a light problem.

He sees the same error in wireless safety rules. Heat-based limits, he says, resemble the "ultraviolet catastrophe" of classical physics around 1900, applying smooth reasoning to a system that works in discrete steps: the harm comes through electron spin, not heating. Life divides its activity into day and night, which he calls biology's Planck constant, and an always-on world forces a continuous pattern onto it.

Medicine, in his phrase, sees a clog where there is a stall. His mechanic's analogy is a car with its exhaust welded shut: pressure builds until the head gasket blows, however good the fuel. Wireless exposure and missing UV and infrared, he says, weld shut the vagus nerve's bicarbonate drain, so deuterium backs up into the brain, inner ear and gut.
The mindset also builds human guidelines on nocturnal rodents, animals with inverted daily rhythms and different skulls and eyes, his "nocturnal mouse fallacy." Birds, active by day, are to him the better model: like us they use the morning sun to prime the energy motor and melanin to protect their water. Studying human biology in mice, he says, is like repairing a 747 from a submarine manual.
Drug trials, in his reading, share one uncontrolled variable: every participant lives indoors under wireless fields and drinks ordinary deuterium-rich water, so any drug that slows mitochondria can look protective.

#### How the sun became the enemy

His longest history concerns sunlight. He traces sun worship to the Sumerians, a legal right to sunlight in Justinian's Roman code, and the 1903 Nobel Prize awarded to Niels Finsen for light therapy. From the 1980s the message flipped: block all UV and take a pill instead of the source. To him it is telling that the sun was recast as a hazard in the same century the supplement was patented, and he reads Hawaii's 2026 ban on six sunscreen chemicals as the first official sign that we have been poisoning our own light antenna.

#### Cautionary tales

His stories share one moral: institutions protect shareholders and products before people. The Radium Girls, dial painters of the 1910s and 1920s, were told radium paint was harmless while executives handled it behind screens, and their lawsuit helped create workplace safety law. Harvey Wiley's "Poison Squad," twelve volunteers fed food preservatives for five years, forced the 1906 Pure Food and Drug Act, and with today's constant low-dose additives he says "we are all the twelve men." He traces anti-meat advice to a religious vision rather than a trial, and keeps a timeline of confident advice that aged badly, from industry reassurance about smoking to low-fat guidelines. The lesson he draws is that a doctor's confidence is itself a risk.

#### In practice

Kruse's prescriptions are about mindset rather than a protocol. "Verify, don't outsource your thinking," and start from wherever your own understanding is. Because institutions replaced a moral "should" with a legal "must," and because non-clinicians now dictate what doctors may prescribe, he treats refusing to comply with an ungrounded establishment as an ethical duty. He does leave centralized medicine one role, as an acute toolkit of drugs, fluids and procedures for genuine emergencies, with root-cause work following the physics. And he asks members to accept "Personal Quantum Solvency": food, exercise and drugs cannot fix a changing magnetic environment, so act on location, light and grounding.

- **sources:** [384740](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384740/), [378864](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378864/), [380785](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380785/), [368771](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/368771/), [384445](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384445/), [377915](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377915/), [377244](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377244/), [378126](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378126/), [375652](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375652/), [384551](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384551/), [373674](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373674/), [372807](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372807/)

### Hot pepper, eucalyptus, lavender and sweat
- **id:** heat-scent-sweat-flush
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** other
- **summary:** Kruse uses chili heat, eucalyptus cold, the scent of lavender and old-fashioned sweating as cheap ways to push deuterium out through the glands, skin and nerves, and adds a caution about needling skin that is already loaded.
- **protocols:** scent-heat-flush
- **key points:**
  - Kruse treats chili heat, eucalyptus cold, lavender scent and sweating as cheap levers that push deuterium out through glands, skin and nerves.
  - In his model capsaicin's burn fires the bicarbonate flush and sweating, while eucalyptus imitates cold shock without an ice bath.
  - He calls lavender a "software patch" for the thalamus, grounding it in Luca Turin's vibrational theory of smell.
  - He reads salt lost in sweat as a deliberate isotope purge, trading sodium to tow heavy water out.
  - He would clear the skin's deuterium load with sun and grounding before relying on acupuncture needles.

Kruse treats a few everyday sensations as levers on the body's exhaust system. Chili heat, the cooling bite of eucalyptus, the scent of lavender and a good sweat each, in his model, help push deuterium (heavy hydrogen) out through the glands, the skin and the nerves. None of them needs a prescription.

#### Heat: capsaicin

Capsaicin, the compound that makes chili peppers hot, switches on TRPV1, the heat-and-pain sensor in nerve endings. Kruse points to cultures that eat very hot peppers, in parts of Mexico, Thailand and the Caribbean, and explains the effect in his own terms. The burn sets off a calcium and sodium "voltage spike." It makes the salivary glands and pancreas pour out their secretions, pushing out the roughly two liters of bicarbonate a day that he treats as the gut's isotope exhaust, and it brings on sweating, which in his account dumps deuterium through the skin by way of the cooling system built by the sweat-gland gene EDAR. The heat-shock response it triggers, he says, also switches on MITF, the master switch for making melanin, so pigment gets rebuilt. A very hot dose, in his view, can jolt the brain out of the "cell danger response" (the body's defensive lockdown) and restart a stalled gut through the gastrocolic reflex, the urge to empty the bowel after eating.

#### Cold: eucalyptus

Eucalyptus works on the opposite sensor. Its main compound, cineole, stimulates TRPM8, the cold receptor. Breathed in, or applied over the path of the vagus nerve, it imitates cold shock and the mammalian dive reflex without an ice bath, and Kruse says it quiets the overactive AMPA-receptor "noise" of a light-starved brain. He credits it with thinning the thick secretions of the glands, clearing the sinuses (and so, in his model, the teeth that act as the skull's antenna), and lowering GDF-15, a stress signal from struggling mitochondria. He ties it to work from the Blau lab on the PGE2 and 15-PGDH signaling loop, which he says eucalyptus helps restart.

Paired with capsaicin, it becomes what he calls a "Quantum Contrast Flush." Alternating hot and cold makes the small vessels clamp and relax in turn, and that pumping, he says, squeezes deuterium-laden fluid out of the fine tubules of the teeth and the ducts that carry bicarbonate.

#### Scent: lavender

Kruse calls lavender, through its compounds linalool and linalyl acetate, a "software patch" for the thalamus. In his account it changes how AMPA receptors switch off, lowers background noise in the brainstem, raises the calmer alpha and theta brain rhythms, thins the water at cell surfaces and helps clear deuterium. He grounds this in Luca Turin's vibrational theory of smell: the nose identifies a molecule by how it vibrates, sensed through electron tunneling, which is why, on that theory, a deuterium-swapped version of the same molecule (deuterated acetophenone, for example) smells different. Kruse says Turin, rather than Richard Axel, deserved the Nobel Prize for explaining smell.

#### Sweat and salt

Sweating is his oldest example. He says the temazcal, the traditional sweat lodge of Mesoamerica, was how shamanic cultures depleted deuterium, and he accepts sweating in a geothermal sauna for the same reason.

He reads salt loss through sweat the same way. Losing salt, in his model, is an emergency cooling and isotope purge rather than a defect: the body gives up charge, in the form of sodium, to shed mass, in the form of deuterium, using sodium to tow water out osmotically. Humans, he says, are the only primates that separated sweating from panting. Salt-losing traits most likely arose in the deuterium-rich tropics, where water and the sugars of tropical fruit run heavy, because constant sun recharges the body's "battery" and lets it afford the leak. A film of salt on the skin may also act as a conductive shield against electromagnetic interference, protecting the thalamus's 40 Hz clock.

#### A caution about needles

The skin is also where Kruse places a warning about acupuncture. A needle has to pass through the skin to reach the fascia, the connective sheets beneath it. If the dermis is loaded with deuterium, he says, that mass ruins the needle's gain as an antenna and acupuncture can leave a person worse.

#### In practice

Kruse's suggestions are simple: hot peppers with meals, eucalyptus breathed in or applied along the vagus nerve's path (he likes it with a hummed "M"), alternating hot and cold, lavender in the air for a noisy brain, regular sweating in a sauna or sweat lodge, and real salt to replace what sweat removes. He would clear the skin's deuterium load, with sun and grounding, before relying on needles.

- **sources:** [369049](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369049/), [369052](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369052/), [369038](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369038/), [369039](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369039/), [369044](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369044/), [369090](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369090/), [369370](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369370/)

## Conditions

### Cancer as a voltage failure
- **id:** cancer-voltage
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse says any lasting fall in the voltage of the mitochondria's inner membrane can produce cancer, a protective retreat in which deuterium-laden cell fluid, not the genes, drives the change, and which the environment, not drugs, must reverse.
- **protocols:** cancer-glioma

Kruse sees cancer as an electrical failure rather than mainly a genetic one. In his model the cell's power plants, the mitochondria, hold a strong charge across their inner membrane, and anything that lets that voltage sag for long enough can tip a cell into cancer; he even names the fields around wind turbines, given years of exposure. The tumor, to him, is a retreat the cell makes to survive, so the fix lies in restoring the environment rather than in drugs.

#### How a cell falls into cancer

Kruse describes a healthy cell at about −70 millivolts and a failing one at −15 to −30. At that lower level, he says, a gate in the inner membrane (UCP-2) loses the voltage that keeps it sealed. Water rich in deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen with an extra neutron, then floods the mitochondria's interior.

In his account deuterium's extra mass brakes ATP synthase, the spinning motor that makes the cell's energy currency, ATP. Complex IV, the last step of the energy chain, then stops making the cell's own clean, low-deuterium water. Without that water the field across the inner membrane short-circuits.

He traces the slide one step further back as well. NAD+, the carrier that ferries electrons into the energy chain, can pick up deuterium in place of light hydrogen, and once that buffer is lost oxygen turns reactive, the membrane charge drops, and deuterium floods first the cell's fluid and then the mitochondria's interior. The share of damaged mitochondrial DNA in the cell (its heteroplasmy) climbs as this happens.

The cell then falls back on fermentation, the ancient oxygen-free way of burning sugar, and begins dividing out of control. This switch is called the Warburg shift, after Otto Warburg, who saw cancer as damaged respiration rather than mutation; Kruse says his voltage model completes Warburg's picture, and he calls cancer an atavism, a throwback to an older way of living, rather than a disease. He even dates the pattern to life's beginnings: the merger in which a deuterium-tolerant archaeon swallowed a bacterium to form the first complex cell was, in his telling, Earth's first cancer-causing event, and programmed cell death was later built into Complex IV so that damaged cells could be cleared before it happened again.

#### The cell fluid, not the genes

Kruse leans on nuclear-transfer experiments, pioneered by Robert McKinnell and later championed by Thomas Seyfried, in which the nucleus of one cell is moved into the body of another. A cancer nucleus placed in healthy cytoplasm (the cell's fluid body) behaved normally, while a healthy nucleus placed in cancerous cytoplasm turned cancerous. To him this shows that DNA is the parts list, not the engine, and that what drives cancer is the water around it.

In a cancer cell, he says, the body's exhaust routes have failed and the cytoplasm is saturated with deuterium "silt." A healthy nucleus dropped into it locks up: the chemical tags on its DNA-packaging proteins freeze, and p53, the protein that patrols DNA for errors, can no longer slide along the strand because the surrounding water has stiffened. He credits Seyfried with calling cancer a mitochondrial disease but says the problem is mass, not fuel. Fermentation is a low-torque "safe mode" the cell drops into once deuterium has silted its mitochondria so badly that ATP synthase can no longer spin at speed.

Methylation, the tagging of DNA with small carbon groups that switches genes on and off, is where he sees the weight doing its worst damage. When the body's bicarbonate tide fails, deuterium enters the pool of methyl groups, and a carbon–deuterium bond grips harder and vibrates more slowly than a carbon–hydrogen one. The tags turn "sticky," the enzymes that erase them can't keep up, and p53 can no longer read the pattern well enough to order repair or cell death; he calls this the "CD3 brake." Wireless signals, in his account, cause cancer through exactly this kind of deuterium-driven over-tagging.

He adds a second route into DNA itself. When the blood's vortex stalls, deuterium floods the pentose phosphate pathway, the side road where cells build the sugars for new DNA, and heavy building blocks end up in the genome. DNA built that way is too massive to vibrate in step, and that, to him, is what genomic instability means.

#### The "matrix fire" and its causes

Kruse calls the stalled interior a "matrix fire." Oxygen that can't be used cleanly flips into singlet oxygen, a reactive form that attacks membranes and DNA, and the faint light cells naturally give off (ultra-weak photon emission) turns into chaotic noise. The growth pathways cancer drugs target (PI3K, Akt, mTOR) are, in his reading, readouts of that fire, not its cause.

He names four things that uncouple Complex IV: missing near-infrared light; nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless devices and wiring); deuterium; and the wrong copper isotope, since he says Complex IV is built to run on heavy copper-65. Like weight gain, he suggests, cancer can be a "safe mode": a lower-energy state that staves off sudden organ failure and buys time, which can be won back once the interference is removed.

#### A body-wide electrical circuit

Kruse draws support from Björn Nordenström, a radiologist at Sweden's Karolinska Institute who built on Robert O. Becker's work. Nordenström described a second circulation in the body, an electrical one running through blood vessels and the fluid between cells, which he called "biologically closed electric circuits." Between 1978 and 1983 he treated lung and breast tumors by inserting electrodes and passing a weak direct current, which drew white blood cells to the tumor and shut down its blood supply.

Chinese doctors later used this "electrochemical therapy" on more than 10,000 patients, Kruse notes, reporting success above 70% in some solid tumors, while Western medicine looked away. He tells a similar story about Georges Lakhovsky, who in 1925 at the Salpêtrière hospital in Paris infected geraniums with a tumor-causing bacterium; the one plant circled by an open copper ring shed its tumor while the rest died. Work like this was buried, he says, because a copper ring is free and chemotherapy is not. To him it confirms cancer as a bioelectric short circuit.

#### Magnetism, the brain and the night

Magnetism sits at the root of his model. Kruse says oxygen keeps its safe, stable form (triplet oxygen) only when a person is "pinned": grounded, sunlit and living under a stable local magnetic field. His shortest chain for glioma, a brain tumor, is no pinning, then singlet oxygen, then glioma. Glioblastoma, he argues, starts when foreign magnetic fields break the junctions that link mitochondria into one network, and restoring that connection can extend survival beyond what doctors expect.

Because Earth's field is weakening, he expects a magnetic excursion (a large, rapid drop and shift in the field) to drive many cancers. Without spin pinning, chromosomes misalign as cells divide, and the mutations oncology tracks are debris left by that failure.

He reads Australia's melanoma epidemic as an early example. It began on the west of the continent and has spread to the Sydney coast, which he ties to the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), a vast and growing weak patch in Earth's field now reaching into the Indian Ocean. The skin's layered network of small arteries works, in his model, as a "distal refinery" that sheds heat and deuterium through cold and sweat. Once those vessels lose their melanin, the light-absorbing pigment, they can no longer shield the skin's mitochondria from the combined load of light and nnEMF, and melanoma follows.

Submariners make the same point from the other side. They carry about twice the usual melanoma risk, he notes, despite spending months without any UV. To him a submarine is indoor life at its extreme: no UV-A or infrared to keep body water ordered, and a steel hull full of electronics and shielding that drives deuterium out of the blood into the skin's base layer. When the inner exhausts through the brain and gut are blocked, the skin becomes the body's largest vent for deuterium, so in his view light matters more than food or fitness for melanoma.

Night is part of the picture. Citing studies from Russel Reiter's group, Kruse says cancer cells drift back toward normal oxygen-burning at night, when mitochondria release melatonin, and return to fermentation by day. He calls this nightly repair "magnetic chemotherapy," and says light at night switches it off.

#### Breast cancer and darkness

Breast cancer he reads as a disease of darkness. He cites a population-based case-control study in *Cancer Epidemiology, Biomarkers & Prevention* in which every additional daily hour of sun during the teenage years went with roughly 16% lower odds of breast cancer for winter sun and 11% for summer sun, with more sun giving more protection and no link at all for sun after 30. Without light, he says, Complex IV backs up with protons, the breast's melanin shield fails and local DNA is damaged, so a breast tumor is evidence of too little working melanin. Long-term hair dye belongs in the same story: hair and scalp pigment filter the brain's own ultraviolet glow, and stripping it is, in his account, why dye use is linked to breast, ovarian and brain cancers.

#### Glioblastoma as a deuterium sink

Glioblastoma (GBM), the most aggressive brain tumor, is in his model a sink where deuterium collects. He cites a paper finding that glioblastoma cells lack a working DFF40/CAD enzyme, the one that chops DNA into fragments when a cell dies on schedule. With melanin wiped from the brain, he says, the cell gets the order to die but can't finish; it lingers in a half-dead stall that breeds instability, aggression and resistance to radiation and chemotherapy, and he calls such cells "geometric zombies."

He reads a 2024 *New England Journal of Medicine* report from Massachusetts General Hospital, in which engineered immune cells (CAR-T) shrank glioblastomas dramatically within days, as a short-lived deuterium purge: the immune flood sped up water turnover, Complex IV briefly made clean water again and the tumor lost its fermenting edge. The tumors returned, he says, because nothing in the hospital restored light, ground or field. He points to studies reporting longer median survival when deuterium-depleted water (DDW) was added to standard care, says he has used DDW with glioblastoma patients for 20 years, and reports that his patients average close to four years against about one under usual care. Most who die, in his view, die because they don't relocate.

#### In practice

His priorities for cancer and glioma: get magnetically pinned first; ground; take natural UV-A and infrared sunlight and don't fear UV-B; remove nnEMF; and for glioma, sleep in a cold bedroom and move to a better environment. Women, he says, should get sun on their breasts. At high latitudes, he adds, naturally low-deuterium water won't protect someone who lives indoors under artificial light. He believes UV-A light, life on a zero-declination line and tree resins such as mastic gum, which he says are naturally low in deuterium, can help turn cancer cells back from fermentation toward normal respiration. He calls a ketogenic diet unwise as a cancer strategy because many aggressive tumors (prostate, glioblastoma, triple-negative breast) burn fat too, so cutting glucose alone can select fat-burning clones. He warns against hyperbaric oxygen in cancer, treating extra oxygen as a toxin for a stalled cell, and says anything that removes excess mass from the body tends to help. Nor does he see drugs as the main answer, pointing to a 15-year Oxford trial in the *New England Journal of Medicine* in which surgery or radiation for localized prostate cancer did not lengthen survival compared with monitoring.

- **sources:** [385641](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385641/), [378783](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378783/), [379984](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379984/), [382301](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382301/), [370282](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370282/), [385873](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385873/), [378207](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378207/), [382394](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382394/), [374830](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374830/), [383346](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383346/), [373641](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373641/), [369924](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369924/)

### Diabetes and blood sugar
- **id:** diabetes-light-field
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse treats insulin resistance as a protective circuit breaker, and type 1 and type 2 diabetes and the dawn sugar rise as light, deuterium and magnetic problems in which deuterium escaping the blood into tissue, not food, drives sugar up.
- **protocols:** type-1-diabetes, type-2-dawn-sugar

Kruse treats diabetes as a problem of light, deuterium and magnetism, not of food. In his model the pancreas is one of the body's main exits for deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen that jams the cell's energy machinery, and diabetes follows when indoor light and an unstable magnetic environment block that exit. He holds that type 2 diabetes is reversible and that counting carbohydrates and insulin units treats only the surface.

#### Insulin resistance as a circuit breaker

Kruse calls insulin resistance a protective shield, not a defect: a circuit breaker that trips when a cell is offered more fuel than it can safely burn. Indoor spaces, he says, are dominated by blue light and magnetically "dead," and running the cell's energy chain under that light upsets the electrons' spin, which drives insulin spikes. He puts high blood sugar and insulin down mainly to light and to a lack of "pinning" (being grounded under a stable local field), and says meal timing under LEDs drives type 2 diabetes more than food does.

In a low-field room full of nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields), he says, burning glucose is a "dirty burn" that throws off flares of the faint light cells emit and leaves deuterium behind. Extra fuel would stall ATP synthase, the cell's energy motor, and could tip the cell toward cancer, so refusing it is self-protection. Forcing glucose in with injected insulin is, in his phrase, a "thermodynamic crime," like pushing more current through a transformer that is already shorting; he says it damages DNA and the melanin lining the blood vessels.

The shield works, in his account, through the glycerol-3-phosphate shuttle, a back-door route that carries hydrogen into the mitochondria. He says it runs only during insulin resistance and favors light hydrogen, so deuterium is left behind in the cell fluid instead of jamming the energy motor. He points to animals that use this on purpose: the bar-tailed godwit flying 11,000 km without food, and the naked mole-rat surviving without oxygen. High-dose insulin, by contrast, forces deuterium in; he calls it a "biophysical degausser" because it strips cell membranes of their charge.

The godwit, he adds, makes the flight with high blood sugar and full insulin resistance, burning its own fat and gut, yet performs at its peak. What protects it is the pecten oculi, a comb of blood vessels packed with melanin inside the bird's eye, which he calls a "photonic heat sink": it turns the metabolic noise of the flight into usable current and shields the bird's magnetic compass. Humans do the same, in his account, with the choroid plexus (the tissue that makes spinal fluid) and the retina's pigment layer. High sugar is survivable when that melanin shielding is intact, and diabetics, he says, have stripped theirs with nnEMF and blue light. Research on night-active mice misses this, because day-living birds and humans share a melanin-led clock.

Birds as a group, Kruse says, are insulin resistant by design. They lack GLUT4, the transporter that lets insulin pull sugar into muscle and fat; their liver's glucagon receptor is permanently switched on; they carry several times more alpha cells (the pancreas cells that make glucagon) than mammals; and they run blood sugar in the hundreds of mg/dL with no fat under the skin, so lifelong insulin resistance costs them nothing. They clear the deuterium that comes with all that glucose through the pecten. Mammals took another road after the dinosaur extinction, relying on leptin and body fat to turn sunlight into energy and dumping deuterium through the pancreas, which is why the pancreas sits at the center of human diabetes. To him the birds show that insulin and leptin are less central to disease than medicine assumes.

Fat burning gives the cell another way to set sugar aside. Burning saturated fat, Kruse says, pushes electrons backward through Complex I, the energy chain's entry point, and an iron-sulfur sensor there reads this reverse electron flow as a signal to skip glucose and fructose. That is why, to him, high blood sugar in the cold can be healthy.

#### Where the deuterium sits

Kruse points out that blood serum carries deuterium at about 12–14 millimoles per liter (roughly 150 ppm), far above any of the trace elements listed on a standard lab sheet. In his model that load is safe while it stays in the blood: the heart's vortex and the brain's fluid-sorting system keep it there, away from the mitochondria and their energy cycles. Once it moves into tissue, the energy cost of each reaction soars and blood sugar and insulin climb with it. He says nothing raises blood sugar more than deuterium, and that deuterium lodged in beta cells is the reason diabetes exists. Blue-rich light at night adds to it by confusing the eye's daylight sensor and the body clock, which blunts insulin release.

He reads a 2026 Gladstone Institutes paper in *Cell Metabolism* the same way. In it, low oxygen turned red blood cells into glucose sinks, and a drug that makes hemoglobin grip oxygen more tightly (HypoxyStat) reversed high blood sugar in mouse models of both type 1 and type 2 diabetes. To Kruse, red cells, which carry no mitochondria, act as a body-wide sink that pulls glucose and deuterium back into the blood and out of tissue, and low oxygen mimics the nitric oxide that UV-A light releases from skin. That, he says, is why people living at altitude have less diabetes.

#### The beta cell's jammed motor

In type 2, Kruse says, the beta cell (the pancreas cell that makes insulin) is usually full of insulin but can't release it. Release needs a burst of energy from ATP synthase, the cell's rotary motor. Table sugar and corn syrup, which he says plants load with deuterium, eaten under blue light without balancing red light, break up the cell's structured water and let deuterium jam that motor. "Beta-cell fatigue" is therefore, to him, an isotope jam; left long enough, singlet oxygen (a reactive form of oxygen) damages the cell until the body removes it.

The pancreas normally clears deuterium through what he calls the vagal "tailpipe." The vagus nerve drives an enzyme, carbonic anhydrase, to make bicarbonate, which binds deuterium and carries it out through the pancreatic ducts into the gut. Sugar under blue light, or a magnetic excursion, knocks the enzyme out of alignment and "welds the tailpipe shut," so deuterium backs up into the beta cells.

Clearing that exhaust, Kruse argues, lets the cell resolve its own diabetes whatever the carbohydrate intake. Only once the flush runs, shown by a well-formed (Bristol type 4) stool, can beta cells make insulin again, so the key is clearing deuterium from the vagal nerves that serve them. He says sunlight lowers blood glucose by about 27%, that advanced type 2 patients have regained insulin secretion with deuterium depletion, and that beta-cell stem cells can recover through the light-driven repair Robert O. Becker described. Type 2 diabetics also lose less weight on GLP-1 drugs, he says, because their energy chain is already stalled ("Marcus-inverted," pushed past the point where electrons can flow), so the drug strips muscle rather than fat.

Diabetics carry more cancer, in his account, for the same reason. The chain runs from weak stomach acid to too little bicarbonate, to deuterium that sits instead of leaving, to deuterium-laden methyl tags on DNA and finally to failure of p53, the cell's DNA guardian. The longer someone has been diabetic, the more of this builds up, and the more nerve, eye and kidney damage, dementia and cancer follow.

#### The dawn rise and a shifting field

The morning rise in blood sugar, the "dawn phenomenon," is in his reading a failure to catch the morning magnetic shift, not a hormone surge. At daybreak the upper atmosphere ionizes and the local field changes, and healthy cells use that wave to wake. A diabetic's damaged membranes can't capture it, so voltage drops and the liver dumps glucose to keep the cell's battery alive.

Kruse calls diabetics "magnetic messes" who all need an agonic line, a place where compass north and true north coincide, which he treats as the zone of most coherent field. As the poles move, these lines drift across continents, leaving people under a field their ancestry wasn't adapted to, and he expects a weakening field to "mint" millions of new diabetics.

India is his main example. He has tracked India's diabetes alongside Australia's melanoma for about ten years and treats them as one disease of the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), the vast weak patch in Earth's field, which he believes has pushed into the southern Indian Ocean and reached the southern tip of India. A population that ate deuterium-rich staples for 5,000 years without diabetes now has about 170 million diabetics, and he notes new cases of both types among sun-rich, darkly pigmented people on the volcanic island of Réunion.

The damage, he says, travels through the pancreas's blood supply. Beta cells draw up to a fifth of the pancreas's blood flow from a dense capillary bed, much like the brain's, fed from the superior mesenteric artery, the main artery to the gut. A shifting field and nnEMF load that artery with deuterium and block the body's own melanin-making, "bleaching" the arterial tree even while the skin stays dark. He says surgeon friends in India found more disease in the aorta and this artery in diabetics, and he reads the rise in pancreatic cancer since 2015 as the same process with more deuterium.

#### Type 1

Type 1 he frames as a deuterium and magnetic problem too, worse at high latitudes and mildest near the equator. Blue light without sunlight's near-infrared can't be used, so the body stores the energy as deuterium in tissue and fat ("mammals who turn blue light into fat"), and the pancreas, a midline isotope regulator, stops filtering. Oxygen must stay in its stable triplet state at all times for beta cells to rebuild, he says, and mouth breathing undermines this by cutting nasal nitric oxide. Rising insulin needs despite sun, or a sunburn, mean too little pinning.

Each insulin dose given without other routes forces more deuterium into cells, he says, which is why doctors now meet "insulin-resistant type 1s," whose cells are so packed with heavy water that insulin can no longer open them. Type 1 children are often hyperactive, he adds, because their bodies are trying to make the movement-driven vibration a godwit uses in flight.

Numb toes in type 1 are, in his reading, deuterium silt settling in the body's longest nerves rather than sugar damage. The brain's fluid vortex lacks the lift to draw the heavy water back up the vagal drain, and a weakening field no longer helps. So the number of years someone has had type 1, a measure of isotope piling up, predicts this damage better than HbA1c, the standard three-month sugar average, and a person with one kidney has half the filter to work with. Sunspots on the face alongside numb toes fit the same pattern: the far end of the exhaust has shut and heat backs up into the head. Type 1s, he adds, carry more cancer risk than type 2s because their bicarbonate loop fails earliest, and diabetes shares a latitude gradient with MS and ALS.

#### The back door to glycogen

For those other routes Kruse points type 1 families to Dr. G. Moody's book *The Back Door to Glycogen* (the "Moody Method"), which he treats as a record of thinking from before insulin became the only answer. Moody held that movement, heat and cold can carry sugar into storage as glycogen without insulin's "hammer." Kruse reads the back door as chewing (his "mastication pump"), sunlight and cold, and he redefines type 1 as a lost flow of fluid through the brain's ventricles more than a missing hormone. Banting and Best's insulin won, in his telling, because a chemical path could be sold, in the same era that he ties the first modern wave of type 1 to the field shift after the Carrington Event.

Whether and when beta cells restart can't be predicted, he says, even though the physics they need (unbroken light, water and magnetism) is known; childhood is the best window, before the pancreas becomes locked by deuterium. In children he reads red eyes as a good sign, deuterium leaving through the tear and eyelid glands, and a deep tan as beta cells making insulin again.

He accepts that light and field alone may not regrow beta cells the immune system has destroyed. For those cases he points to stem-cell-derived beta cells and plasmapheresis (filtering antibodies out of the blood plasma), each protected by sun and pinning.

#### In practice

For type 2 and the dawn rise, Kruse's protocol is to ground, strip out nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields) and take in solar infrared; to go out barefoot facing the sunrise during the morning magnetic shift, ideally on an agonic line; to use incandescent rather than LED light and a short burst of red light while grounded to blunt a spike; and to avoid sugar eaten under screens and LEDs. To clear the tailpipe he names copper resonance rings over the neck and solar plexus, or deuterium-depleted water (DDW) with 3% saline. For type 1 his protocol adds incandescent or red bulbs, bare-eyed sunrise, a red and near-infrared panel over the pancreas and brainstem, DDW, seasonal high-fat food with nothing after dark, cold water, and at least 90 minutes a day of barefoot grounding on an agonic line; he also recommends Moody's book and its back-door routes of chewing, sun and cold, and notes that talking loudly works the vagus nerve and helps the clearance. Someone with a single kidney, he says, has to lean even harder on sun and ground. He judges progress by a well-formed (Bristol type 4) stool more than by glucose readings or HbA1c, by years of disease more than by any single number, and in children by a deep tan.

### Heart, cholesterol and arteries
- **id:** heart-and-cholesterol
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse calls cholesterol the membrane's insulator and light sensor, treats ApoB, Lp(a) and homocysteine as signs of repair and sludge, reads a heart attack as a "magnetic stall" rather than a blocked pipe, and traces heart and artery disease to lost melanin, lost field, lost sleep and missing sun.
- **protocols:** heart-protocols

Kruse rejects the idea that cholesterol causes heart disease. In his model cholesterol is the insulation and light sensor of cell membranes, and heart and artery disease come from broken-down melanin, a weakening magnetic field and too little sunlight. Forcing cholesterol down, to him, removes one of the body's defenders.

#### Cholesterol as insulator and light sensor

Kruse calls cholesterol the main stabilizer of the inner mitochondrial membrane: it sets how stiff and well insulated the membrane is, which lets it hold its strong electric field. Medicine pictures arteries as pipes clogged with grease; he pictures membranes as capacitors that store charge. When an ungrounded person under wireless signals and blue light loses the structure of their cell water, cells make more cholesterol to patch leaking membranes, so cholesterol arrives like a fireman, not an arsonist.

It is also, in his account, a light sensor. Sulfated cholesterol, carried mainly in HDL, is a "summer antenna" tuned to UV-B: when UV-B strikes it in the skin it makes vitamin D3 and releases charge. LDL is the sulfur-poor winter or indoor form that can't catch UV-B, so a high LDL signals light stress, with the liver making ever more LDL to catch energy it can't see.

He calls cholesterol a "second magnetic fat" alongside DHA (the omega-3 fat in seafood), the two working as a spin filter in membranes and nerves. He reads a Korean study of 12.8 million adults, and a review in *BMJ Open*, as showing that low cholesterol is the real danger, with the highest-cholesterol elderly living longest.

Familial hypercholesterolemia (FH), an inherited tendency to very high LDL, is to Kruse an adaptation rather than a defect. Because cholesterol and DHA both insulate well, he says, a body facing a failing outside field floods itself with them to build a local shield, a kind of Faraday cage, around heart and brain: FH families lean on cholesterol to guard the heart, while carriers of APOE4 (a gene variant linked to Alzheimer's) need DHA to guard the brain. He also suggests FH began as a northern lineage's way to stock extra raw material for vitamin D and hormones under weak UV, and expects strong tropical light to bring such a person's cholesterol down quickly. While the planet's shield weakens, he tells people with FH they are lucky to have it.

#### Statins, ApoB and Lp(a)

Statins, Kruse says, block the pathway that makes both cholesterol and CoQ10, an electron carrier in the energy chain. The membrane then loosens, its insulation leaks and its voltage collapses; he calls statins "quantum de-magnetizers" and links them to muscle pain and breakdown, memory loss and heart-rhythm problems. They also keep Complex IV, the energy chain's last enzyme, from reaching full efficiency, so a patient may dodge a heart attack now but, held in a low-energy fermenting state, later slide into amyloidosis (protein deposits in organs), ataxia (loss of coordination) or dementia.

The brain pays too. The thalamus, the relay hub that sits against the brain's fluid chambers, must be stocked with cholesterol and DHA, whose electrons respond to Earth's field, and in his model the pair forms a magnetic sheath that keeps the spinal fluid's vortex steady. Strip it with a statin and the flow turns turbulent, much as it does in a strong geomagnetic storm, giving what he calls "cognitive haze." He describes lowering LDL during a field decline as stripping a house's wiring in a lightning storm.

Lp(a), a cholesterol particle cardiology now targets, he sees as an emergency patch the body sends to insulate a failing vessel wall. He cites a large trial in which a drug cut Lp(a) by 80–90% without reducing heart attacks or strokes, and sees no point in testing for it.

ApoB, the main protein on LDL particles, plays the same role. Stalled mitochondria give off flares of faint light that tell the nucleus to make more ApoB and Lp(a), which he calls "dielectric patch kits" for leaking water at the vessel wall; the sulfur bridges in Lp(a), he says, hold charge like a capacitor. Stripping these particles out with drugs is, to him, shooting the repair crew while the power plant burns.

#### How arteries fail

Heart attacks were barely recognized before about 1911, Kruse notes, and he ties the rise of heart disease to Earth's weakening field since the 1859 Carrington Event and to electrification. A healthy vessel lining, in his model, carries a strong negative charge that repels LDL, built from collagen, sulfated cholesterol and melanin (the dark pigment he treats as a light-to-electricity converter). A weakening field, wireless signals and blue LEDs strip that charge, the lining's nitric oxide output falls, and LDL slips under it.

Elastin, the springy protein that lets arteries recoil with each beat, works alongside melanin. Kruse notes that the same embryonic cells (the neural crest) give rise to both the nerves of the great vessels and the pigment cells that put melanin in them. Elastin acts as a spring tuned to the sun's infrared and melanin as a broad light-catcher, and together they carry the current that keeps the lining negative. Deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen, lodged in elastin turns the spring into a brittle stick, so each heartbeat cracks the wall a little; rising ApoB is the body grouting the cracks, and this is how he believes peripheral artery disease (PAD), the narrowing of arteries outside the heart, begins.

PAD, to him, is the far end of a central failure. When the carotid artery where it passes Meckel's cave silts up, the blood's capacity to hold charge drops, its smooth vortex breaks down and deuterium scours the linings of the high-resistance arteries in the legs. Heart disease and neurodegeneration are, in his phrase, two blowouts on one circuit.

A high coronary calcium score despite good deuterium numbers, he says, means melanin loss, and the scan maps where melanin is missing from the arterial tree. Charged by light, melanin holds metals and calcium in solution, and light-activated vitamin K2 keeps calcium bound to its proteins. When nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields) and blue light make melanin drop its metals, calcium is left in the artery wall like a scab. A deuterium-loaded aorta, he adds, "freezes" into calcified scar to cut its energy costs, choosing what he calls "the symmetry of the stone," and the organs it feeds inherit that stiffness. So calcified arteries and valves need the field restored, not calcium-channel blockers or peptides.

He stresses that K2 does not steer calcium on its own. Matrix Gla protein, the K2-activated protein that holds calcium in the vessel wall, can only grab it when the wall's water is well ordered; in a light-starved "electronic winter" the calcium has already dropped out of solution where arterial melanin is missing. K2, to him, is a shuttle that needs sun-charged vessel walls, and the real steering comes from light, piezoelectric pressure and the field.

High blood pressure is, to Kruse, the loss of a "second pump": blood moving through Earth's field is pushed along by a magnetic (Lorentz) force, and when that assist fades the heart has to push harder. Low nitric oxide means high pressure, he says, and pressure that stays high means a person is not tanned enough. Sunlight also shifts the iron in red cells toward its ferrous form, which he says raises blood's magnetic responsiveness and thins it electrically rather than with drugs. Light at night, he adds, remodels the heart. In heart failure he points to a protocol pairing hypertonic (extra-salty) saline with the diuretic furosemide, credited with better urine output and kidney numbers and fewer deaths and readmissions; to him the salt works by raising the dielectric constant of blood water, its capacity to hold charge, toward the healthy value of 160, which matters more than removing fluid.

#### Heart attack as a magnetic stall

Kruse rejects the blocked-pipe picture of a heart attack. The heart, in his model, is a vortex engine that lets blood spiral with little friction, and a heart attack (myocardial infarction) is a "magnetic stall": the moment the vortex stops turning and the blood, which he treats as a liquid crystal, begins to tumble instead of spiral. Plaque is a scab where the vessel's shield was stripped, and the clot is debris that settles out after the stall rather than its cause.

He reads the electrocardiogram (EKG) as a meter of the heart's direct current, and ST elevation, the classic heart-attack trace, as current arcing out where insulation has broken down. A geomagnetic storm can supply the last push: a jump in the K-index, the standard gauge of storm strength, makes blood stickier, and in someone already loaded with deuterium and rusted (ferric) iron it becomes the final lock. He gives the order of failure as the energy motor first, then the nerves, then heart muscle, and says people recover when they are grounded or move to a high-intensity volcanic zone such as El Salvador.

#### Sleep, marrow and plaque

Kruse reads a 2019 *Nature* paper on sleep and arteries through his model. Plaque-prone mice whose sleep was broken up made less hypocretin (also called orexin, a brain hormone that keeps us awake), released more inflammatory white cells from their bone marrow and grew bigger plaques. To him the root cause is deuterium in the marrow. Building blood cells takes clean, light water, and sleep is when the brain and the vagal exhaust pull deuterium out of the marrow; without sleep the marrow becomes a deuterium sink that turns out "heavy" inflammatory cells, which travel to the aorta and shred its elastin. Sleep, in his phrase, is a nightly isotopic refinery.

He ties this to a cascade he says he sees in people with degenerating spinal discs. Ungrounded and loaded with deuterium, the brain's fluid sorting stalls, its ventricles fill with heavy water, hypocretin falls, the marrow sends out heavy inflammatory cells, and arteries and discs break down together. Degenerative disc disease, PAD, hardening of the arteries, sleep apnea and neurodegeneration are, in his view, one disease.

#### Homocysteine and the blood's vortex

Kruse says the four-chambered heart spins blood into a vortex that pushes deuterium out toward the vessel wall, helped by the lift Earth's field gives moving blood. When that field fails, homocysteine, an amino-acid by-product, pools as "isotopic sludge" and scrapes the lining like "endothelial sandpaper," setting off the light flares that summon ApoB and Lp(a).

He treats raised homocysteine as a gauge both of a place's danger to mammals and of heart-attack risk, a "Rosetta Stone" for the heart. To him it means that chewing and the field's lift are not keeping blood moving well enough.

#### The heart's own brain

The heart has a "little brain" of its own. Citing Armour's mapping of the roughly 40,000 neurons of the intrinsic cardiac nervous system, Kruse says these cells sense how thick and deuterium-laden the blood entering the right atrium is and set the force and rate of each beat to keep the blood vortex fast enough. He calls the heart's magnetic field 60 times stronger than the brain's, and points to transplanted hearts, which beat without any vagus nerve input, as proof of its independence.

Heart-rate variability (HRV), the beat-to-beat change in rhythm, is in his reading this mini-brain's direct readout, and it is the first thing to turn chaotic as the field weakens. Deuterium backing up from the gut can then load the heart's own nerves, and he ties sudden deaths of fit young people in Brazil, under the South Atlantic Anomaly (the large weak patch in Earth's field), to such a stall.

Most rhythm problems, Kruse says, are the heart managing mass rather than faulty wiring. Deuterium backing up from a brain that drains poorly, through too little sun and grounding, loads the heart, and its rhythm shifts to cope: a fast compensating rate that can end in heart failure with preserved pumping (HFpEF); slow rates and pauses when the load gets too heavy; and atrial fibrillation and extra beats where the ordered water around ion channels breaks down, especially in the left atrium near the aortic root, which takes the most shear from heavy blood. Each pattern, he says, cuts the heart's information traffic to avoid overheating, and all of them worsen with lost sleep.

#### One root with the brain

Heart and brain disease share one root in his view: failure of the body's non-visual light sensing, the light sensors in skin and tissue outside the eye's vision. Strong sun on the chest lets skin melanin release adenosine, the same molecule emergency doctors use to reset a racing heart, which steadies rhythm. He says sunlight lowers Lp(a), ApoE and calcium scores. Atrial fibrillation set off through the vagus nerve he traces to a deuterium-loaded cardiac nerve plexus. Deuterium in the spinal fluid, he adds, is read by a sensor on the floor of the brainstem as a weaker signal, which turns the vagus down and primes the heart for atrial flutter.

#### In practice

His heart protocol: get full sun on the chest, and for a high calcium score combine UV light with grounding. Skip Lp(a) testing, don't push cholesterol below about 200 mg/dL, and take no statins during the field decline, even with familial hypercholesterolemia, which he treats as protection. He watches homocysteine as a better warning sign than cholesterol, reads persistent high blood pressure as a sign of too little tan, and treats deep, unbroken sleep as the nightly clean-out of the marrow that keeps plaque from forming. For vagally triggered atrial fibrillation he suggests self-hypnosis to raise CO₂ and calm the cardiac plexus, or simple tricks such as rubbing the eyes, dunking the face in cold water and chewing mastic gum. For calcified arteries or valves, he says, restore the field and cut wireless and night LED light rather than adding drugs or leaning on vitamin K2 alone.

### The heart's vortex: bicuspid valve, Lasix and rescue drips
- **id:** aortic-vortex
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse treats the heart as a vortex generator and the aortic valve as its nozzle; he says a two-leaflet valve, salt-stripping diuretics, lost sunlight or a heavy deuterium load break the vortex, calcify or balloon the aorta and starve the brain, while catecholamines, hypertonic saline and Lactated Ringer's can restart a stalled system.
- **protocols:** heart-protocols, pregnancy-birth-children, iv-fluids-acute-collapse

Kruse argues that the heart's job is not just to push blood but to spin it. The aortic valve, in his model, shapes each beat into a centred vortex that carries the pulse's energy up the aorta, sorts deuterium (heavy hydrogen) toward the vessel wall and drives the spinal fluid's flow through the brain. When that vortex breaks, whether from a malformed valve, a drug that strips the blood's salts or a deuterium-loaded body, he says the heart, brain, hair and thyroid fail together. He calls this map of linked failures a "failing stator" and expects to see more of it as Earth's field moves through an excursion.

#### A heart built to spin

Kruse draws on the Spanish anatomist Francisco Torrent-Guasp, who described the ventricles as a single band of muscle wound into a helix. Wringing like a towel, that band makes suction as well as pressure, so to Kruse the heart is a vortex generator first and a pump second. Blood carries about 150 ppm deuterium, and while the vortex spins this is harmless: the spiral throws the heavy isotope toward the outer layers of flow and keeps it parked in the bloodstream, away from the light water inside cells. When nnEMF or a weak field stalls the vortex, the same blood becomes, in his phrase, a "deuterium bomb," and the heavy isotope leaks into heart muscle and artery walls.

He reads this design into evolution. Each chamber added, from the two of fish to the three of amphibians and reptiles to the four of mammals and birds, was to him another stage of isotope sorting, and the four-chambered heart is a two-stage centrifuge needed to power a large upright brain. He traces the idea back to archaea, the deuterium-tolerant partner in the merger that made complex cells, whose stiff membranes squeezed deuterium out by rigidity; complex life, he says, turned that static armor into the spinning flows of the heart and the brain's ventricles.

#### A circle versus a slit

A normal aortic valve has three leaflets. Kruse says they open symmetrically into a round opening that preserves the left ventricle's own twist, so blood rises up the ascending aorta as an organised, centre-aligned laminar flow (smooth layers that do not mix). Because the stream stays centred, wall shear stress, the frictional drag of blood on the vessel lining, stays at a minimum and the pulse's kinetic energy is conserved. He adds that the aortic wall carries POMC and melanin in its elastin and fibrillin, and that the smooth vortex lets them sort deuterium.

Some people are born with a bicuspid aortic valve (BAV), which has only two leaflets. He calls it a "vortex destruction event." A two-leaflet valve opens as an ellipse or slit and fires blood off-centre. He cites 4D-flow MRI, a scan that tracks blood motion in three dimensions over time, showing an abnormally tight right-handed helical jet striking the outer curve of the ascending aorta, like a firehose aimed into a bend in a pipe.

The consequences follow in his account. The off-centre jet wastes the pulse's energy as heat instead of delivering it to the brain and organs. Its shear damages the lining and the POMC cells in the wall, which lose their thin, deuterium-poor boundary layer, so the wall "browns" (calcifies and degenerates) and stretches into the aneurysm typical of BAV. The heart, in his phrase, is fighting a gear mismatch from day one: it thickens to overcome its own internal friction until it collapses hydraulically, which he gives as the reason BAV so often ends in early heart failure.

#### Why a valve forms with two leaflets

Medicine calls BAV a congenital anomaly; Kruse calls it an energy problem in the germ line. Methylation, the tagging of DNA that times the steps of development, needs a steady supply of ATP and well-structured water. A female fetus forming her eggs keeps them in what he calls "satellite mode," with Complex I (the energy chain's entry point) switched down to keep deuterium out. If the mother drinks water at 155–200 parts per million (ppm) deuterium and lacks the sun and field to sort it, he says, deuterium floods the germ line.

The heavy atom then jams methylation. A carbon–deuterium bond is shorter and tighter than a carbon–hydrogen bond, so enzymes struggle to move methyl groups onto DNA, and the result is hypomethylation (too few tags). The precise timing needed to build a three-leaflet valve is lost, and the embryo falls back on an older two-leaflet design. He points to reptiles such as snakes, turtles and lizards, whose bodies do not need strong vortex power, and calls BAV a "Pre-Cambrian" throwback. Congenital anomalies in general are, to him, "epigenetic scars" of a deuterium-heavy environment.

He says the literature already points his way without knowing it. He cites links between variants in the folate pathway (such as MTHFR) and obstruction of the heart's outflow tract, including aortic stenosis. He also cites studies finding abnormal DNA methylation in BAV aortas, global hypomethylation alongside heavily tagged cardiac control genes, while the vessel is still normal in size, before blood-flow damage could explain it.

#### Frozen or burst: calcification and aneurysm

Kruse treats the aorta as a light-powered organ. Hormones cut from POMC, the precursor that also yields the tanning hormone alpha-MSH, act on receptors in the vessel lining to calm inflammation and keep its surface charge high, which he calls the vessel's "neutral cloak." Sunlight on skin primes that system, and copper is needed both for the pigment-making enzyme and for Complex IV, so a copper shortage drops the shield. The organ of Zuckerkandl, a cluster of hormone-making cells beside the abdominal aorta, comes from the same embryonic cells as pigment cells, and in vitiligo, where skin loses its pigment, he notes that skin elastin falls as deuterium rises. He pictures melanin migrating along the elastin of the heart and great vessels as the heart's "fulcrum," the pivot that balances its energy budget, so when it is lost the first signs are electrical, shifts in rhythm and in heart-rate variability, well before calcium appears on a scan.

When the cloak fails, an enzyme he calls the "inflammatory phospholipase" (sPLA2-IIA) starts shredding membranes, and the wall goes one of two ways. In calcification the elastin spring locks and its exposed charged sites bind calcium into a bone-like template; in an aneurysm, protein-cutting enzymes and sPLA2-IIA shred the wall until it balloons outward. He calls both the same shortage of sun. Calcium moved out of bone into the artery wall, he adds, is why calcification where the aorta forks travels with thinning bones.

#### Brain, hair and thyroid

The brain feels a broken vortex first, Kruse says, because it is about 2% of body mass yet draws about a fifth of the heart's power. An off-centre pulse makes the spinal fluid's own vortex chaotic, the beating cilia and the brain's glymphatic (waste-clearing) flow lose their driving force, and deuterium silt builds up at the obex, the lower tip of the fourth ventricle. He links this to the early fatigue, dizziness and racing heart many people with BAV report later in life.

Heavy water that settles in the third ventricle, he says, causes a "dielectric flashover" in the hypothalamus, the brain's hormone hub, disrupting the stress-hormone axis (HPA), sleep and hormone production. Hair follicles are among the body's fastest-cycling tissues and very sensitive to methylation. With a deuterium-loaded germ line and ordinary 155 ppm water, their mitochondria hit what he calls the "4.3-second stutter," his figure for how often a deuterium atom strikes at that level, and the follicles miniaturize. That, he says, is why large meta-analyses tie baldness at the crown to coronary heart disease. More broadly he reads hair loss as the brain's matrices and central retinal pathways being "cooked" by disorder, a sign that foretells dementia.

The thyroid is drawn in last. Ordered to make more hormone so stalled mitochondria spin faster against heavy water, it burns itself out, which he gives as the reason Hashimoto's thyroiditis and alopecia so often appear together.

#### Salt, potassium and Lasix

Kruse treats the blood's salts as "dopants," the impurities engineers add to tune a semiconductor. Sodium keeps blood and spinal fluid able to hold charge. A loop diuretic such as furosemide (Lasix) strips that sodium, the dielectric constant (the water's capacity to hold charge) falls, and the vortex loses its grip. He compares it to the ocean's overturning circulation (AMOC): when seawater freshens, the sinking current that carries oxygen to the depths stalls. In the heart, the left ventricle's natural cycloid vortex weakens, oxygen delivery drops, and the heart's pacemaker nodes fire fast or erratically, producing atrial flutter in a structurally normal heart. Lasix also flushes potassium, which he says collapses the structured water inside cells.

Flutter and fibrillation are, in this framework, mechanical events rather than primary wiring faults: the vortex loses its magnetic grip, the blood cavitates and the atrial walls shudder as they try to keep the flow smooth. If a heart attack is a stall, flutter is the wobble before the engine seizes, so he treats it as a genuine warning. The heart borrows background lift from Earth's field, he says, so where declination (the gap between compass north and true north) is shifting fast, as in Canada or under the South Atlantic Anomaly, the outside timing signal wobbles too; poor grounding and too little cholesterol and DHA add to the risk.

That is how he reads the heart-failure protocol that pairs hypertonic saline with furosemide: giving the salt back keeps the charge high while the volume comes off. Lasix alone, in his phrase, is a volume fix for a dielectric problem, like lightening a struggling conveyor belt while cutting power to its motor. Albumin, the main blood protein, plays a similar part in his model: it holds water inside the vessels and keeps the blood thin, so a rising albumin means a stronger vortex, the vessels' counterpart of rising NAD+ in the mitochondria.

#### Restarting a stalled vortex

Kruse says he saw sudden "acute lattice lock," his term for body water abruptly stiffening with deuterium, in intensive-care patients during COVID while working in a Missouri hospital. In organ-donor protocols, he notes, high-dose dopamine drips are used to keep organs perfused, and at that dose dopamine acts much like racemic epinephrine. He says he watched several apparently dead patients revive on Levophed (norepinephrine). In his reading dopamine, a highly conductive and paramagnetic catecholamine, restores conduction in the brain's striatum and the heart, offers a bypass around the drag of deuterium's kinetic isotope effect (the slowing of reactions by heavier atoms), restarts the heart's vortex and "thaws" a lock that had been diagnosed as brain death. Absent brainstem reflexes, he argues, are often a hydraulic "magnetic stall" rather than death.

He calls every epi-pen, dopamine drip and 3% saline bolus a "magnetic de-fragger," says other drugs can do the same only if they are deuterium-depleted first, and says he built his own protocol for this in Missouri. His complaint is that these tools are used at the harvest end of the timeline instead of as first-line rescue.

He gives Lactated Ringer's, a standard IV fluid, a similar role. Its lactate, he proposes, feeds the glycerol-3-phosphate (G3P) shuttle, which hands electrons straight to CoQ10 and Complex III and so bypasses the deuterium-jammed Complex I step he calls the "NADD+" stall; its pH shift, he suggests, also helps eject deuterium by changing how fast protons tunnel in the mitochondrial matrix. He says he used the G3P shunt, among other measures, to de-frag lattice-locked clients.

#### In practice

Before conception, Kruse wants the mother on 92.5 ppm deuterium-depleted water and a methylation-rich diet (choline, methionine and B vitamins from pasture-raised animal foods), not synthetic folate. Anyone born with BAV, he says, should drink 92.5 ppm water for life so the heart's motor is not wrecking the aortic wall under a heavy load. To protect the aorta from calcifying or ballooning he relies on sun on the skin, grounding and enough cholesterol and DHA, and he reads new flutter as an early warning to fix the environment. He never wants Lasix used alone and gives salt back with the diuretic. In hospital he asks for Lactated Ringer's rather than glucose or plain saline drips, and in a sudden collapse he wants clinicians to consider catecholamines and hypertonic saline early; intravenous saline is a clinician's tool.
