### Neurodegeneration and MS
- **id:** neurodegeneration
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse traces Alzheimer's, Parkinson's, ALS, MS, depression, anxiety and addiction to lost light, field and vagal tone, with deuterium backing up into the brain's fluid, misplaced copper and melanin breakdown doing the damage; he reads sundowning, sleep apnea and hydrocephalus as stalls of the same fluid system.
- **protocols:** ms-agonic-therapy, brain-eye-ear-conditions

Kruse traces Alzheimer's, Parkinson's, ALS and multiple sclerosis to lost sunlight, a weak or unstable magnetic field and a failing vagus nerve. In each, he says, deuterium (heavy hydrogen), misplaced copper and broken-down melanin do the damage. The misfolded proteins medicine targets are, to him, the body's defenses rather than the cause.

#### Alzheimer's starts outside the brain

Citing an 86,000-patient study, Kruse argues Alzheimer's is a body-wide disease. In his model the vagus nerve is a cable whose steady pacing keeps the linings of the gut, lungs and blood vessels charged so they can keep deuterium out. Being unpinned (not grounded under a stable field), nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields) and blue light drain that tone.

In the lungs, he says, the structured water then collapses and incoming oxygen flips into singlet oxygen, a reactive form. In the gut, singlet oxygen damages fats and the lining can no longer sort out heavy hydrogen, so deuterium leaks into the blood. The blood carries both up to the thalamus and hippocampus, where deuterium jams the brain's navigation cells and memory and sense of place fail. He also describes a back route: when the gut's morning bicarbonate flush stalls, heavy water climbs the vagus to the floor of the brainstem.

#### The brain's isotope centrifuge

Kruse asks why the brain, about 2% of body weight, takes 15–25% of the heart's output. His answer is that blood flow powers a vortex of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF, the clear fluid bathing brain and spinal cord) between the third and fourth ventricles, the brain's central fluid chambers. Driven by each heartbeat and steered by tiny hairs lining the ventricles, the vortex spins light hydrogen away from deuterium and vents the heavy isotope through the vagus.

He describes the refinery in stages. Blood sits at about 150 ppm deuterium; the aorta's vortex is the first centrifuge, and the choroid plexus, the pigmented, blood-rich tissue that makes CSF, is the second, returning heavy water to the veins so the fluid bathing the brain can stay below about 130 ppm. The CSF then speeds through the narrow cerebral aqueduct between the two ventricles, and the pressure drop there separates heavy from light like wine settling in a decanter.

The system keeps time with the sun. In his model the flow runs fast by day, when the brain's melanin and water are charged by light, and reverses at night for the glymphatic flush, the brain's sleep-time waste clearance. Small patches without a blood–brain barrier, the circumventricular organs, sample the blood's deuterium and tell the choroid plexus how hard to work. Chewing helps as well: its pressure waves break up films of heavy water that settle around the skull base, one reason he links gum disease to both brain and heart disease.

Under the South Atlantic Anomaly, a large weak patch in Earth's field, he says life pays a constant "Landauer tax": with little outside field to lift the vortex, each thought costs more heat. He calls dementia the biological version of the planet losing its magnetic memory, and expects people carrying heavy deuterium to fare worst as the old field fades.

#### APOE4, light starvation and sundowning

Kruse calls Alzheimer's the most sensitive sign of a brain mismatched with its light and field. APOE4, the gene variant that raises Alzheimer's risk, is to him not a broken gene but a high-performance design built for the strong UV of Africa, a "Formula 1 engine" that rushes low-deuterium fat and cholesterol to the brain. Under flickering LEDs, nnEMF and too little sun it short-circuits: deuterium settles, the fourth ventricle's vortex stalls and amyloid forms as insulation against the noise. APOE4 carriers are the canary, he says, and fail first where the local field drops. More broadly he calls dementia a frequency mismatch, a brain built for one solar environment living in another, and his answer is to restore the missing frequencies of sunlight through the skin.

Sundowning, the evening confusion and agitation of dementia, he reads as a nightly stall of the same vortex. As infrared and UV-A light disappear at sunset, brain water loses its order, deuterium kept in suspension all day settles out, and the vortex loses torque. It is worst in APOE4 carriers and in people whose DHA and cholesterol shields are thin, statin users among them. The hallucinations of that hour are, to him, crosstalk from leaking visual pathways, and melatonin pills can't stand in for a calcified pineal gland.

#### Plaques as "deuterium trash cans"

Citing Stephanie Seneff and Anthony Kyriakopoulos, Kruse says that once the vagal drain and melanin fail, cells deliberately fold amyloid and alpha-synuclein into rigid fibrils, using cardiolipin (an inner-membrane fat) and copper to do it. These fibrils are vaults that lock deuterium away from the energy chain, which is why he calls plaques "deuterium trash cans." He also blames misplaced copper: the energy chain's last enzyme should run on heavy copper-65, and the wrong isotope there, he says, always goes with neurodegeneration.

Melanin, in his account, normally makes a tiny electric current that sweeps deuterium out of tissue. When it can't, amyloid, tau and alpha-synuclein are left behind like the chalk outline at a crime scene, evidence rather than culprit, which is why drugs that clear plaque fail while the deuterium and wireless load stays. Building on Robert O. Becker, who showed that anesthesia drops the body's direct-current nerve voltage to zero, he calls advanced ALS and Alzheimer's a permanent anesthesia from isotopic mass.

He reads a Yokohama City University study in *Brain Communications*, which used a new PET tracer to show abnormally dense AMPA receptors (glutamate receptors that excite neurons) in people with cognitive impairment after COVID, as the cell danger response caught on camera. Receptor density tracked how severe the symptoms were and separated patients from healthy people with high accuracy. In his model, a mitochondrion losing its voltage sends out GDF15, its distress signal, and the stalled energy supply lets AMPA receptors pile up as "synaptic noise," a failing melanin battery trying to jump-start itself.

He tells of a 97-year-old woman with Alzheimer's who "woke up" after a single LSD experience, which he takes as proof that her self was drowned in heavy water, not lost. He cites a 2023 *Nature Neuroscience* paper by Moliner and colleagues showing that psychedelics bind TrkB, the receptor for the brain growth factor BDNF, directly; in his reading the drug speeds the cell's energy cycle enough to spin deuterium out and reopens the vagal drain to the gut. He even links hidradenitis suppurativa, painful tunnels under the skin of the armpit and groin, to Alzheimer's: the same enzyme, gamma-secretase, processes amyloid's parent protein in the brain and a growth signal in hair follicles, so deuterium-driven misfolding gives plaques at one end and blocked follicles at the other.

#### Thick fluid: depression, anxiety and SSRIs

Kruse proposes renaming major depression "Isotopic Viscosity Syndrome": a brain whose fluid has thickened as the field declines, which he says is why depression is surging. He cites a 2025 *JAMA Psychiatry* study by Kochunov and colleagues finding that depression is marked by local drops in brain blood flow that pull neighboring neurons out of sync, a functional change that tracks how severe the symptoms are.

Anxiety, he says, often has the same source: heavy CSF pressing on the third and fourth ventricles and on the amygdala, the brain's alarm center. His chain runs from nnEMF to deuterium in tissue, failing mitochondrial motors, failing sodium pumps and more AMPA receptors, leaving a hyperexcitable brain. He cites work showing that chronically low sodium disturbs serotonin and dopamine in the amygdala and produces anxious behavior. Depression and anxiety are, to him, the first signs of a downshift from a bright serotonin-and-melatonin state to a noisy survival state on the kynurenine pathway, the route that breaks down tryptophan when light is wrong, and he wants the deuterium lowered rather than relying on talk therapy alone.

SSRIs, the most common antidepressants, can make this worse in his view. Sertraline carries two chlorine atoms that grip electrons and make nearby water denser and more viscous (thicker and slower-flowing), so the spinal fluid turns to "jello." On scans this shows as enlarged ventricles and a shrinking brain; the hairs lining the ventricles can't beat through the sludge, and heavy water presses on the pituitary and brainstem. The brain, using a fifth of the body's power, drowns first.

#### Addiction

Kruse defines addiction as deuterium overload in the brain and CSF that destroys melanin and lowers dopamine. The brain's reward and aversion centers sit right against the ventricles, with the hypothalamus forming the floor of the third ventricle, so poor drainage hits them first. Heavy fluid slows the dopamine machinery of the substantia nigra, and once its neuromelanin is bleached, dopamine output shuts down to save power.

Craving, in his reading, is the hypothalamus demanding a dopamine spike to push through the drag, and withdrawal is the thalamus sensing the lag of heavy fluid. Without sunlight to drive the gut's exhaust, he says, a "heavy" brain reaches for alcohol, opiates, food, supplements or screens for a crude electrical kick, and humans, built to be the most electrically clean of mammals, suffer most when skin and stomach are cut off from the sun.

#### Parkinson's

In Kruse's model the substantia nigra's neuromelanin, a dark pigment bound to iron, is a magnetic battery that keeps the spins of incoming oxygen aligned. nnEMF, artificial light or dehydration warp it, oxygen goes singlet, and Lewy bodies (clumps of misfolded alpha-synuclein) build up as corrupted proteins the cell can't clear. Skin cells facing the same voltage drop can escape into cancer; dopamine neurons can't, so they die.

Parkinson's strikes where the field is weakest, he says, so patients who learn to pin themselves magnetically will win. Cutting the vagus nerve lowers risk, in his reading, because it blocks deuterium flowing from gut to brain. From his years at Charity Hospital, moving from general surgery to neurosurgery, he recalls that patients who had a truncal vagotomy (cutting the main vagus trunks, once common for ulcers) developed less Parkinson's. With the stomach and pancreas cut off, deuterium could no longer back up into the brain, an accidental "isotopic firewall." This, he says, gives the Braak hypothesis, the idea that Parkinson's begins in the gut, its missing mechanism: the gut is the body's main deuterium sink.

The entry point, he says, is the area postrema, a small patch on the floor of the fourth ventricle with no blood–brain barrier. Deuterium backing up from the gut gets in there, gathers in the lateral ventricles and reaches the basal ganglia and substantia nigra beside them, where it jams ATP synthase and uncouples the neuromelanin shield; medicine sees the result as Lewy bodies and tremor. Under blue light and nnEMF, he adds, the kynurenine pathway's by-products char that neuromelanin further.

Eyelid spasm (blepharospasm) is, to him, an early warning. It means deuterium and heavy metals have reached critical density in the basal ganglia, where the substantia nigra, the most pigmented and iron-rich node, clogs first. Each twitch is an attempt to shake the mass loose, and it worsens under blue LED light as the brain "shuts the blinds."

Every Parkinson's patient is heavy with deuterium, Kruse says, which makes light-driven repair very hard, and L-DOPA, the standard drug, only treats symptoms. He reads Sardinia's historically low Parkinson's rate as low deuterium rather than the L-DOPA in its fava beans: a traditional diet rich in folate and fiber, strong UV-A light and local field conditions. He reports a man with severe Parkinson's who had not walked or talked in three years talking and walking 50 yards on the beach after four weeks at Costa del Sol in El Salvador.

#### Why the adult brain degenerates

Kruse links neurodegeneration to infancy. Babies are unusually fat because the brain uses most of their energy, and body fat feeds it until the solar clock matures. An adult brain stripped of its light shielding runs that program in reverse, its cells turning senescent and storing fat droplets, but the skull has no fat reserve, so the brain degenerates instead. Complex brains, he adds, appeared only after Earth's field strengthened, and degeneration is life's retreat when it weakens again.

#### Sleep apnea, CPAP and hydrocephalus

The thalamus, in his model, weighs the brain's load by feeling the basilar artery pulse against the clivus, the sloping bone at the base of the skull. Heavy water damps the flow of protons and drops the brain into a high-resistance state that shows up as brain fog, sleep apnea and neurodegeneration.

CPAP, the mask that blows air at constant pressure to hold the airway open, has a hidden cost in his view. The pressure travels back through the veins and the cavernous sinus, a venous channel beside the pituitary, and weighs on the fourth ventricle's vortex, so users wake up oxygenated but, in his phrase, "magnetically braindead." For someone whose heart-rate variability is already low from a poor field, he says, CPAP can be the last straw that shuts the vagal exhaust, and he blames this for the death of the football Hall of Famer Reggie White.

Hydrocephalus, a build-up of fluid in the ventricles, he calls the ultimate clog of the brain's exhaust pipes and a salt-wasting state. Heavier fluid presses on the hypothalamus, which governs sodium and water, producing SIADH (a hormone state in which the body holds water and blood sodium falls) and swings in blood pressure, while weight on the cerebellum explains the poor balance. Low sodium in turn makes neurons misfire and leads to falls in the elderly. In one man with a shunt, the tube that drains the extra fluid, Kruse judged the shunt sound and blamed deuterium overflow, noting that he woke up more as more deuterium cleared. A shunt relieves pressure, he says, but the deeper fix is full-spectrum light and deuterium depletion, and he reads the confusion that can follow surgery as the cell danger response to a deuterium surge, with GDF15 as its marker.

#### ALS and MS

ALS he calls a waking form of dream-sleep paralysis. With the body clock destroyed, the brainstem can't tell day fields from night fields and sends the paralysis commands of REM sleep while the person is awake. He says it is rare in tanned tropical people and worsened by long flights across time zones, especially over the weakening South Atlantic field.

He also sees ALS as the Parkinson's backflow aimed downward. Instead of rising into the brain, deuterium drains through the obex, the narrow point where the fourth ventricle opens into the spinal canal, and settles around the nerve roots, "lattice-locking" the spinal fluid until the motor neurons leaving there burn out. Motor neurons go first because they have the highest energy demand and the most complex mitochondrial networks. He maps the fourth ventricle's outlets as isotope gates: the central opening should send the lightest fluid down to the spinal cord, the body's information highway, and when the vortex fails, heavy fluid reaches the cord's wiring instead, which he ties to both ALS and MS.

He reads the genetic finding behind a 2026 Breakthrough Prize, a repeated stretch of DNA in the C9ORF72 gene shared by ALS and frontotemporal dementia, as a weak point rather than the cause. The repeats fold into knots that are sensitive to hydrogen's mass; when the chemical tags around them carry deuterium, the knots stiffen and the gene can't be read properly, so its proteins misfold. ALS is 1.2–1.5 times more common in men and starts later in women, which he credits to periodic pelvic flushing in women that keeps the bottom of the spinal fluid "highway" clear; the advantage fades after menopause, when the pelvic water stagnates.

MS, in his account, is spin failure at the nodes of Ranvier, the gaps in a nerve's insulation where signals jump. Its rise with latitude reflects collapsing water structure and melanin, not low vitamin D, and the misfolded proteins in MS are readouts of a poor local field. An MS flare, he says, is a deuterium flood that arrives when the bicarbonate exhaust is already near capacity, which is why immune-suppressing drugs disappoint: they don't touch the mass. The pallor and thinning hair common in MS are the body shutting down costly melanin and hair to send its electrons to the brainstem. Hence his "agonic line therapy": living where compass north and true north coincide, which he says matters more than deuterium-depleted water.

#### In practice

For MS his protocol puts relocation first: move to a low-latitude spot with a flat, stable field on a good agonic line, then use grounded Mu-metal shielding (a magnetic-shield alloy), block non-native frequencies and use targeted coherent light. Off a good line he adds deuterium-depleted water, a seafood diet rich in DHA and iodine, and red and infrared light; he favors relocation over sleeping pills and says thin MS patients shouldn't dry fast. For Parkinson's he says to get magnetically pinned by living where the field is strong, points to sunlit beach relocation like the El Salvador case, and rates the Neuropod the best vagus nerve stimulator for neurodegenerative disease. For dementia, and above all for APOE4 carriers, he puts sunlight on skin and eyes first, keeps evenings dark and screen-free, chews real food and avoids statins. He is wary of CPAP for people already low in heart-rate variability, wants light and deuterium depletion alongside any shunt for hydrocephalus, and treats anxiety and addiction by restoring sun and the gut's exhaust rather than with talk or pills alone.

### Immunity, inflammation and the body's barriers
- **id:** immunity-barriers
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse describes the body's barriers as isotope filters that fail together, inflammation as a containment breach carried by positive charge, immune cells as melanin-powered, and infections, tick bites and post-viral fatigue as signs of a deuterated terrain.
- **protocols:** mold-inflammation

Kruse sees the body's barriers (blood–brain, gut, retina, spinal cord) as filters built to keep deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen, away from the mitochondria, and says they fail together. Inflammation, in his model, starts when mitochondria lose their electrical containment, and infections reveal the state of the body's terrain more than the strength of the germ.

#### Barriers as isotope filters

The blood–brain barrier's main job, Kruse says, is to keep deuterium out of brain mitochondria, whose strong membrane field can't tolerate its drag. The barrier's lining cells sort light hydrogen from heavy using one-handed (chiral) molecules, tuned by morning UV-A light. Wireless signals and blue LEDs make calcium channels in those cells leak, the tight seals between cells loosen, and deuterium floods the spinal fluid.

The brainstem senses the breach and releases GDF15, a stress signal from struggling mitochondria; he calls the surge a "mitokine storm." It shows up first as fatigue, poor focus and psychiatric distress and can eventually push brain tissue into fermentation and brain cancer.

Because mitochondria link into one network across the body, he says, barriers never fail alone. The retinal barrier (macular degeneration), the gut barrier (fatty liver) and the spinal barrier (MS) go down together, and treating them as separate organ diseases misses the shared cause.

#### Inflammation as a containment breach

Mitochondria descend from bacteria and still carry bacterial markers: circular DNA, cardiolipin (an inner-membrane fat) and bacterial-style proteins. Inside the mitochondrion these are harmless; once they leak, the immune system's sensors read them as infection. What decides whether inflammation fires, in Kruse's account, is whether the inner membrane keeps these contents electrically and magnetically contained. Autoimmunity is then a clean-up of a damaged grid rather than a mistake, with antibodies tagging broken mitochondrial parts.

Inflammation itself, he says, is a build-up of positive charge. That charge was once carried mostly by spare protons (ordinary hydrogen nuclei) but, as the field weakens, increasingly by deuterons, the heavier nuclei of deuterium. That is why, to him, minor problems now progress faster than textbooks predict and worsen with each generation.

Severe allergic reactions fit the same picture. When a deuterium-loaded person exercises, the blood grows thicker and hotter; if the body's electrical brake fails, inflammation surges, and anaphylaxis is the body blowing a fuse, a desperate vagal reset that stops the engine before a total burnout.

#### Melanin arms the immune cells

Immune cells, Kruse says, run on melanin too. B and T cells kill germs and then wind inflammation down using bursts of reactive oxygen and nitrogen, and melanin powers and steadies those bursts. When blue light and nnEMF, together his "Silicon Valley Syndrome," drain that melanin battery, the immune system has no ammunition against either germs or stress, and its firepower turns into heat that damages the body itself.

That is how he explains the low alpha-MSH he says is found in nearly all patients with Lyme disease, chronic fatigue, fibromyalgia and mold illness. Deuterium distorts POMC, the precursor protein, so it can't be cut cleanly into alpha-MSH, and the signal linking skin and nerve tissue to the immune system fades. A tick bite or a biotoxin then works like a crowbar on the mitochondrial membrane: its voltage collapses, and deuterium held in the blood and the fluid between cells rushes in and seizes the energy motor. Screens, he argues, are the main risk factor, because they open the gut and brain barriers before any tick arrives, so the bite tips an already loaded person into chronic fatigue or fibromyalgia.

Women, he proposes, carry more autoimmunity and mental illness because they are built for more melanin and more exposed to its loss. Estrogen raises melanin capacity to support pregnancy, longer hair makes a larger antenna, and cosmetics and hair dyes suppress MITF, the master switch for pigment. When a cell's internal skeleton collapses for lack of light, the immune system no longer recognizes it as self.

#### Viruses as scouts

He frames viral illness the same way. Letting a virus replicate is a cell's emergency shutdown when its field fails, and a cytokine storm (a runaway immune flare) is containment failure, with the cell dying because its containment dissolved rather than because the virus killed it. The spike protein, which he describes as strongly positively charged, short-circuits membrane charge along the same final path as chronic disease. He is blunter about the mRNA shots: once the injection has "integrated," he says, no one recovers. The damage waits until a person stops doing the right things, then catches up with the inner mitochondrial membrane, and the spike protein problem never goes away.

Post-viral fatigue, in his model, is a collapsed energy gap. A virus diverts the cell's proton flow into fermentation and into building its own proteins, which come out heavy because viruses lack the machinery that keeps deuterium out, so a person with a high viral load becomes a "deuterium bomb." The cell stops making its own high-charge water (dielectric 160) and fills with ordinary water at 78, and the brain's microtubules grow too heavy to vibrate as consciousness needs. He points to NASA's finding that dormant Epstein–Barr, shingles and cytomegalovirus wake up again in astronauts as the same collapse in space, and says wireless-heavy cities do the same on Earth.

Fast-replicating viruses, Kruse argues, are the biosphere's scouts, adapting to environmental shifts before complex life does. He reads the unusual person-to-person spread of the Andes strain of hantavirus as a way of skipping a degraded outer environment by jumping straight between sheltered human bodies. In the noisy water of a weak-field region, ubiquitin, the cell's tag marking proteins for destruction, starts tagging the host's own respiratory proteins, leaving an energy gap the virus exploits in the vessel lining and lungs.

The large cortisol surge of acute hantavirus is, to him, the brain sensing the energy chain stalling and dumping more glucose, and with it more deuterium, to force current through. The fluid that floods the lungs in fatal cases is the vagal exhaust collapsing and the lung's air–water interface shorting out.

#### Tolerance needs light and timing

Immune tolerance, Kruse says, depends on mitochondria signaling "self." Citing Zhang and colleagues in *Nature* (2025), he says steady EPO signaling to dendritic cells (the immune system's scouts) switches on calming regulatory T cells; magnetically stable mitochondria keep that signal steady, while wireless and blue light scramble it so the immune system reads chaos as invasion. Eating indoors after dark puts the gut's clock out of step with the brain's, which he says tips the thymus toward inflammatory T cells. Vitamin A, in his account, is a pigment whose light- and field-driven shape changes program immunity in the gut, airways and urinary tract.

In one family he read positive antibodies against voltage-gated calcium channels as proof that their "melanin–water battery" had run down. Calcium then floods cells and either "petrifies" them, as in Lambert–Eaton myasthenic syndrome (a muscle-weakness disorder), or "shreds" them, as in PANS/PANDAS; he calls Lambert–Eaton the "astronaut on Earth" syndrome. He likewise ties a Toronto child's primary immunodeficiency to too little magnetic flux in that city, and in a girl born without a working FCHO1 gene, which T cells need to pull their receptors inside, he sees the extreme case: her T cells can spot a germ but can't act on it, a defect he traces to deuterium in both parents' germ lines.

#### Infections read the terrain

To Kruse more deuterium means more bacterial overgrowth, and candida means a deuterated gut. In PANS/PANDAS, the sudden childhood tics and OCD linked to strep, he sees the strep as a clean-up crew rather than the cause. Mold illness and Lyme lower alpha-MSH, a skin and immune hormone made alongside melanin, and he says mold troubles only people who can't make melanin.

Chronic infections such as Epstein–Barr virus, Lyme and filarial worms, he says, thrive in heavy, low-oxygen water once the body reverts to a primitive "Archean" state; they mislead doctors but are scavengers of deuterium decay, not its cause. In a child stuck in a "magnetic stall," even pinworms may be tolerated as crude backup immune regulators, though their presence still signals a child too heavy to clear its own waste.

The same logic applies to Protomyxzoa rheumatica, a blood parasite some practitioners target directly. Kruse calls it a marker of a collapsed water table: its sugary biofilms (protective slime layers) trap deuterium, thicken the blood and lower its dielectric constant, the water's capacity to hold charge, and deuterated dietary fats harden them. In one member he blamed an indoor, tech-heavy life in the Arizona desert, whose groundwater carries fluoride, which he calls the most damaging atom for water's dielectric.

#### The skin as the last exit

When the inner exhausts through the gut and brain are shut, Kruse says, the skin becomes the outlet. He reads a burning butterfly rash across the cheeks, the classic pattern of lupus, as deuterium leaving through the face once the vagal and facial-nerve routes have closed, and calls lupus a whole-body hydraulic failure in which skin, brain and heart short out together. Stevens–Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis, in which the skin peels away in sheets, are to him the extreme case of the body venting through its largest exhaust port; he ties a rise in these cases reported by a burns unit after the COVID shots to heavy elements he believes the injections carried.

He says thousands have reversed mold illness on clean, low-EMF land with equatorial sun. Infrared sunlight expands the body's structured water and squeezes toxins out of membranes, and barefoot grounding speeds blood and spinal-fluid flow to flush them. Binders, charcoal and glutathione fail, he says, when cell voltage has collapsed, because detox needs energy.

#### In practice

Kruse's mold and inflammation protocol: move to a clean, low-nnEMF property (low in wireless and wiring fields) with clean indoor and outdoor air; maximize equatorial sun; and ground barefoot at an equatorial shoreline. For inflammation and viral illness he says to ground, get sunlight, re-pin to a good field and clear deuterium, and to eat by daylight. For Lyme, tick-borne and post-viral illness he puts cutting screens and wireless first, so the barriers close before anything else is tried. He advises against relying on binders, charcoal, glutathione or antifungals while cell voltage is collapsed. For parasites and biofilms he treats the medium rather than the bug: UV-A to build melanin and 3% saline to clear deuterium, until the water's dielectric climbs back toward a healthy 160 and the immune system can clear the biofilm itself.

- **sources:** [382727](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382727/), [373627](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373627/), [382733](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382733/), [381869](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381869/), [370104](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370104/), [382234](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382234/), [378819](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378819/), [385785](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385785/), [384272](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384272/), [382546](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382546/), [373347](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373347/), [373451](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373451/)

### Gut and liver: the light-and-deuterium axis
- **id:** gut-liver-axis
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse treats the gut as a light-and-deuterium organ whose exhaust starts with very acidic stomach acid and a morning bicarbonate flush, reads stool and reflux as gauges of that exhaust, and sees the liver as the first organ to fail, linked to brain and pelvis by one fluid axis.
- **protocols:** morning-bowel-movement

In Kruse's model the liver is the first organ to fail and the gut is an organ run by light and deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen. One shared fluid route links liver, pelvis, gut and brain, so he expects their diseases to travel together. The gut's job, as he sees it, is to flush deuterium out each morning, and most gut disease follows when that flush backs up.

#### Why the liver goes first

The liver carries the body's largest daily load of fluid, electrons and deuterium and serves as its deuterium sink, so Kruse says its mitochondria fail first. He reads high liver enzymes with a clean scan as deuterium overload, and points to antibodies against mitochondria in about 95% of primary biliary cholangitis. Fatty liver, to him, is a pressure-relief valve: when nerve noise breaks the gut barrier, the liver packs its cells with fat to shield them from singlet oxygen, a reactive form of oxygen.

Albumin, the main protein the liver makes, is to him the liver's light-fueled sentinel. Low albumin signals an inflamed, deuterium-loaded liver rather than too little dietary protein, and a rising albumin shows the load clearing. He cites studies in which power-line and microwave-band wireless fields raised oxidative stress and shifted fat markers in the liver, and attributes this to the fields changing the state of iron in the liver's heme enzymes, the ones that process drugs and hormones.

The portal vein, liver and pelvic organs share one fluid conveyor that runs on up to the brain. That, he says, is why ovarian failure, sperm DNA damage, gut disease, brain fog, abnormal lipids and early artery disease cluster in the same people, rather than because of family history.

#### Stomach acid as the first exhaust

Kruse reads human stomach acid as a clue to our past. Comparing species, he notes that sheep and horses keep a stomach pH around 5, gorillas about 4.5, wolves and lions about 2, and humans 1.5, as acidic as hyenas and close to vultures. To him that marks humans as scavengers who ate old seafood and carcasses, and it means our pancreas must pour out unusually large amounts of bicarbonate to neutralize the acid, which gives us one of the strongest deuterium exhausts among mammals.

The link is the "alkaline tide": each proton the stomach pumps out to make acid releases a bicarbonate into the blood, and glucagon, the fasting hormone, drives the pancreas to send bicarbonate into the duodenum, the first stretch of small intestine. The enzyme running this loop, carbonic anhydrase, prefers light hydrogen, so deuterium is trapped in gastric juice or leaves in breath and urine. Taking antacids for heartburn, in his view, clogs the engine. Glucagon peaks at the shift from dark to light, which is why sunrise times the whole exhaust; without it, the liver's clock loses its timing and deuterium builds up instead of leaving.

#### The morning bicarbonate flush

Morning sunlight's infrared, in Kruse's account, sets off the cortisol awakening response, the natural hormone rise after waking. That switches on the glucagon gene network on chromosome 2 and releases about 2 liters of bicarbonate fluid from the pancreas. This downward flush, guided by the vagus nerve, sweeps deuterium out through the gut, and a well-formed (Bristol type 4) stool shows it is working. Skipping food at the cortisol peak, he says, stalls it.

The mouth starts the sequence. Chewing sends the bite's pressure map along the trigeminal nerve to the same brainstem centers the vagus uses, warning the gut that mass is on the way and priming the flush, while yawning before sleep sends pressure waves along the brainstem to begin night-time clearance. Overnight the gut collects deuterium, and the reflex that empties the colon after sunrise and breakfast (the gastrocolic reflex) dumps it, best done upright so gravity helps. Teeth show the result, he says: blue-white, translucent teeth go with high voltage and a fast gut, yellow or grey ones with low voltage and a slow gut.

Diet can help or hinder the flush. He points to the Hunza people, whose roughly 100 grams a day of fiber from apricots and whole grains acts as a "deuterium sponge" in the gut, since plant cellulose is naturally low in deuterium; it shields the vagus and frees the bicarbonate loop to do its work. Plain baking soda, he adds, can stand in for missing pancreatic bicarbonate: it flushes deuterium and raises acetylcholine, the vagus nerve's chemical messenger, which tells the spleen and immune system the crisis is over and ends what he calls the cell danger response, the cell's defensive lockdown.

He cites a study in which rats and healthy people drank baking soda in water: cells lining the abdomen signaled the spleen through acetylcholine, and within about two weeks immune cells in the spleen, blood and kidney had shifted from inflammatory to calming types. Taking baking soda, he adds, makes the pancreas work better rather than lazier, because it eases acid stress and leaves the ducts free to respond to their own signals.

When the vagus fails in an ungrounded person under blue LEDs and nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields), the flush stalls and reverses. He calls this an "isotopic backwash": heavy water is pushed into the stomach lining, the valve at the bottom of the esophagus and the crypts of the colon. Reflux, hiatal hernia, colon polyps and colon cancer are, to him, one "hydraulic failure," with polyps a primitive low-voltage shelter that comes before cancer.

As the gut lining loses its alignment from too little sun or too much blue light, stomach pH drifts from 1.5 toward 3 or 4 and the exhaust stops. Reflux, to him, is not too much acid but failed vagal clearance, a kind of cough reflex in which acid rises so the voice box can shake deuterium out. H. pylori and similar microbes are bystanders that thrive in the stagnant, heavy lining, and Barrett's esophagus is a retreat to a heavier, older state. Deuterium backing into the gut's immune tissue creates what looks like food allergy; he says true food allergies don't exist, and that the backed-up load can reach the brain.

Cystic fibrosis he treats as the extreme case of a stalled exhaust. Its faulty gene, CFTR, is in his model the master valve for the bicarbonate flush; when it fails, secretions thicken, deuterium backs up until it destroys the insulin-making islets (cystic-fibrosis-related diabetes), and salty sweat shows the skin's battery shorting out.

Kruse saw the same backup as a surgeon. After truncal vagotomy, the old ulcer operation that cuts the main vagus trunks, patients developed gallstones, dumping syndrome and gastroparesis (a stalled stomach), and his first rupture of a spleen without injury came two weeks after one. He reads all of these as gut organs filling with deuterium once the exhaust was cut.

#### The gut as a light organ

Gut bacteria, Kruse says, give off far more faint light (ultra-weak photon emission) than human cells, and that light organizes the gut's water. The gut lining sheds its cells every day or two, carrying deuterium away with them. The nerve route from the brainstem to the abdomen's nerve hubs (the celiac ganglion and the organ of Zuckerkandl) depends on melanin, the light-absorbing pigment, and blue light and nnEMF crash it.

A broken body clock shows up here first. Citing Li Cheng and colleagues (2026, *Microbiological Research*), Kruse says circadian disruption raises sebacic acid in the gut and shifts the microbiome away from Akkermansia, a mucus-protecting species; the mucus thins and immune B cells inflame the lining. The leaky barrier then speeds deuterium into the mitochondria, which he says is why shift workers have more leaky gut, inflammatory bowel disease and metabolic disease.

The microbiome then turns inflammatory, producing SIBO, Crohn's, colitis and leaky gut; deuterium favors the wrong bacteria, he says, and medicine then blames the bacteria. Crohn's lesions form where heavy water settles unevenly in the gut wall. Cutting out a Crohn's segment, he adds, only detunes the gut, which behaves like a tuned antenna. Intussusception, the bowel telescoping into itself, he calls a motor stall rather than a mechanical accident: deuterium clogs the energy motors of the gut's muscle and nerves, which lack insulating myelin and so are especially exposed.

#### Signs and gauges

When the abdominal melanin network fails, Kruse says, pigment gathers at boundaries as an emergency shield: melasma on the face and a darkened colon wall (melanosis coli) seen at colonoscopy. Polycythemia vera, a condition of thickened blood, traps heat and deuterium in small vessels like a stalled ocean current.

First-morning urine should be dark, he says, as proof of an overnight deuterium dump; clear urine means heavy isotopes were held back. He also reads tests as gauges of the interior. High homocysteine and TMAO (a by-product of gut bacteria) are "silt" markers showing the vagus and the bicarbonate flush aren't clearing deuterium, while white-matter changes on MRI and slow flow on thermography show body fluids thickened like "ketchup."

Stool form is his most direct gauge. A type 4 stool is the finished pellet of the flush. Loose stools (types 5–7) mean a pancreas too jammed to sort water, so heavy water stays in the gut and drags sodium out with it; hard stools (types 1–2) let deuterium seep back up the vagal route toward the brainstem, which he says some patients notice as a metallic taste. Floating stools are good news: citing Levitt and Duane's 1972 *New England Journal of Medicine* report that floaters sink once the gas is removed, their buoyancy coming mainly from methane, and a 2022 mouse study in which germ-free animals' stools sank, he proposes that archaea in the gut bind deuterium into methane and vent it as gas. He calls these methane-makers "isotopic janitors" and links their loss to hard stools and cognitive decline.

#### In practice

No separate protocol is linked here, but Kruse's advice follows from the model. Stand barefoot on wet sand, surf or dewy grass at dawn with sun on skin and eyes, and make the bedroom a "magnetic sanctuary" with all wireless off so the liver can clear deuterium in sleep. Eat a morning meal near the cortisol peak so the bicarbonate flush can run, chew it well, and empty the bowel upright. He would rather restore stomach acid than suppress it with antacids. Avoid ungrounded high-rise living, and avoid surgery for Crohn's where possible. Instead of waiting for structural disease, he tracks four markers: morning glucose, LDL, vitamin B12 and pigment changes such as melasma, and adds homocysteine, TMAO, albumin, tooth color, a Bristol type 4 stool and dark first-morning urine as signs of whether the exhaust is clearing.

### Graves', thyroid and the eyes
- **id:** graves-light-circuit
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse traces Graves' disease, thyroid and adrenal flares and several eye diseases to a broken eye-to-brain light circuit plus night light and wireless, and reads an underactive thyroid as a protective slowdown under a deuterium load.
- **protocols:** graves-and-eyes, brain-eye-ear-conditions

Kruse traces Graves' disease, thyroid flares and several eye diseases to a broken light circuit running from the eye to the brain's master clock, made worse by night light and wireless signals. Fix the eye's light and magnetic inputs, he argues, and the thyroid loses its upstream drive.

#### The broken circuit

Graves' disease, an overactive thyroid often with bulging eyes, is to Kruse a downstream symptom of a broken retina-to-hypothalamus pathway, the circuit that sets the body's clock and temperature. Mistimed blue light bleaches melanopsin, the retina's daylight sensor, so the master clock in the brain can't read the time. This is worse in an "unpinned" location, one where a person isn't grounded under a stable magnetic field.

The hypothalamus then loses its metabolic set point, and the thyroid overdrives to cover what it reads as an energy shortfall. Meanwhile nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields) opens calcium channels in the eye muscles, raising their metabolic rate and loading them with deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen. A higher metabolic rate is exactly the wrong direction in Graves', he says.

The same failure reaches the adrenal glands. A third ventricle (the fluid chamber beside the hypothalamus) heavy with deuterium stretches the window in which thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) can do its repair work, and a stalled thyroid forces the adrenals to over-rev on adrenaline to keep the cell's energy motor spinning. That ends in the adrenal burnout of Addison's disease, which he says doctors treat as permanent.

#### The underactive thyroid as a governor

An underactive thyroid he reads the other way round, as protection. The thyroid, in his model, is the body's most sensitive flow meter, and when nnEMF and blue light let deuterium thicken the water cells make, so that protons can no longer hop freely along it, the gland throttles metabolism to keep the engine from seizing. He sees six decades of rising hypothyroidism, obesity and falling body temperature, at a time when warming seas are speeding up marine animals' metabolism, as this "isotopic drowning" playing out in people. A mildly raised TSH, such as one reading of 4.9 he discussed, is in that light deliberate cooling to avoid taking on more deuterium, not a failing gland.

#### Heme, histamine and lost tolerance

Heme, the iron-containing molecule in blood and many enzymes, is the body's magnetic antenna in Kruse's model, and immune tolerance depends on it. Citing Zhang and colleagues in *Nature* (2025), he says stable mitochondria send a steady "self" signal to the immune system's dendritic cells; spin chaos from wireless and blue light breaks that signal, so antibodies attack the eye muscles and thyroid.

When heme collapses, histidine, the amino acid that anchors its iron, is diverted into histamine, causing itching and thyroid flares. He ties the rise in thyroid disease since the 1980s to the weakening magnetic field and calls all thyroid disease "altered topology."

He names drug effects too: tetracycline antibiotics blacken the thyroid, and lithium concentrates there and disrupts hormone-making, pushing people toward an underactive thyroid. He calls Hashimoto's thyroiditis pre-cancerous. Eyelid (meibomian) gland trouble is part of the same picture: light and fields thicken the oil to wax and plug the ducts, bulging eyes that don't close in sleep dry the glands out, and the overactive thyroid burns them out. Dry eye in general he calls a signal failure rather than a water shortage, so eye drops only add chemical noise.

#### Other eye diseases

The retinal barrier fails along with the body's other barriers, Kruse says. Without a stable field, neuropsin (a UV-A sensor in the eye) can no longer check oxygen's state, the pigment layer's melanin breaks down and macular degeneration speeds up; he reports that a member's father fully reversed his. For glaucoma in the tropics, he says, the driver to remove is artificial light and wireless at night, and a red-light device adds nothing for someone already outdoors all day.

The eye is also the one place where the body's melanin shield and its energy engines can be seen directly. Drusen, the yellow deposits beneath the retina seen on eye scans, and thinning of the retina's pigment layer are to him the ash of a burnt-out melanin battery, and they imply that the choroid plexus in the brain is failing in the same way, letting deuterium across the blood–brain barrier.

He describes the failing retina as a "frosted lens." Heavy water in the space beneath the retina changes how light bends there, so light scatters off deuterium before melanin can convert it. The light-sensing cells aren't broken; sight fails because the medium does, its dielectric constant (water's capacity to hold charge) falling from a healthy 160 to 78.

Retinitis pigmentosa he calls epigenetic, not genetic. nnEMF and blue light damage the melanin and heme proteins of the retina's pigment layer, the retina's charge falls, and that loss alters the genes blamed for the disease. Phones, Wi-Fi, no grounding and contact lenses worn around the clock, he says, speed it up.

Presbyopia, the age-related loss of near focus, he places in the lens itself. Pupils that still constrict and eyes that still converge show the brain's focusing signal is firing, so the lock lies in the eye rather than the brainstem.

#### In practice

His Graves' and eye protocol: plenty of dawn sun into bare eyes while grounded in a well-pinned field; a bedroom free of wireless; red light or fully blue- and green-blocking glasses after sunset; and nothing that raises metabolic rate, including supplements, peptides and stimulating exercise. For an underactive thyroid he would rather lift the deuterium load than force the gland. For eyelid gland problems and dry eye he uses sun on the eyes and lids rather than drops. For glaucoma, sun rather than devices, no eyewear outdoors, and total darkness without wireless at night. For retinitis pigmentosa, cut nnEMF, ground or pin, build tolerance to strong sun gradually, and never wear contact lenses around the clock.

- **sources:** [382234](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382234/), [382239](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382239/), [382237](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382237/), [384734](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384734/), [378299](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378299/), [375722](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375722/), [382730](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382730/), [382384](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382384/), [377239](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377239/), [373170](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373170/), [371637](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371637/), [370213](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370213/)

### Nerves, head and pain
- **id:** nerve-pain-head-injury
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse explains CRPS, head and facial trauma, salt wasting after brain injury, Bell's palsy, voice and hearing loss, tinnitus and teeth-grinding, migraine, trigeminal neuralgia, spinal-cord softening, degenerative discs and osteoporosis as local failures of light, field and fluid.
- **protocols:** nerve-injury, brain-eye-ear-conditions

Kruse explains complex regional pain syndrome, head and facial injuries, Bell's palsy, tinnitus, headaches and osteoporosis as local failures of light, magnetic field and body fluid. In each case he puts grounding and a good field ahead of drugs.

#### Complex regional pain syndrome

CRPS, a severe burning pain that persists after an injury, is in Kruse's model a local crash of melanin and magnetism rather than a mysterious nerve misfire. Melanin, the dark pigment he treats as the tissue's light-to-electricity converter, loses its link to Earth's field in the affected limb. Without "pinning" (being grounded under a stable field), he says light isn't strong enough to settle it, and sunlight or light therapy on an ungrounded limb can speed the damage. So grounding and a good field come first, ahead of nerve blocks or painkillers.

#### Head and facial trauma

Any injury to the brain, physical or emotional, is in his model a floodgate. Trauma, or grief deep enough to cause the "broken-heart" syndrome, stops the fluid vortex, the barrier gives way and deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen, pours into the nerves. What gets called brain damage is to him heavy hydrogen invading the mitochondria, which is why he says head injury from any cause raises the risk of disease later.

Discussing a member hit in the face by a baseball, Kruse read the injury as a "de-pinning" of the sphenoid, the bone at the center of the skull that he treats as the brain's switchboard. The blow, he says, cavitated the spinal fluid and broke up the structured water around the brain's balance centers. Deuterium then seeped in over the following year, which is why vertigo appeared a year after the injury.

The flattened nose also cut nasal nitric oxide, a gas he says shields incoming oxygen from flipping into a reactive form. His approach is to ground, use a 3% saline method with structural decompression, and get sunlight to restart nasal nitric oxide, rather than relying on anti-vertigo drugs. After a brain bleed with new symptoms, he tells members to get examined and imaged by their surgeon.

Two salt disorders that can follow brain injury he reads as survival gears. In cerebral salt wasting the kidneys dump sodium, and in his account that dump drags deuterium out of the spinal fluid. In SIADH the body holds on to water to buffer heat, but under artificial light at high latitudes the retained water is itself deuterium-rich, so the brain swells and stalls. The sensitivity to blue light that so often follows brain injury, he says, comes from the same deuterium surge.

#### Bell's palsy, voice and the inner ear

Bell's palsy, a sudden one-sided facial weakness, is today often the first sign that someone is in a "Marcus inversion well," a place whose field stalls the cell's energy chain. Kruse got it himself within a day of arriving in South America; he had standard treatment, but judged the location the cause and cut the trip short.

A voice that gets "stuck" he traces to the far end of the left vagus nerve. In one member he blamed CT scanning, which he says "de-gausses" tissue, plus a compressive "vise" under the arch of the aorta, where the left recurrent laryngeal nerve (the branch that runs the voice box) loops back up. The nerve then acts like a heat-losing resistor instead of a clean conductor, and he compares it to astronauts losing function in the brain's speech area once they leave Earth's field.

He reads the January 2026 case of NASA astronaut Mike Fincke, who abruptly lost his speech over dinner on the space station and prompted the first evacuation of a whole ISS crew for medical reasons, the same way. To him it was deuterium flooding Broca's area, the mitochondria-dense speech-planning region of the left frontal lobe. In orbit there is no ground to bleed off the heat of the brain's information processing; weightlessness stalls the spinning flow of spinal fluid at the skull base, so the aqueduct between the third and fourth ventricles stops sorting heavy from light water; and cosmic rays, without the sun's 1878 nm short-wave infrared to offset them, let heavy isotopes build where they don't belong. Deuterium then blocks proton tunneling along the ancient iron–sulfur (ferredoxin) wires of the energy chain, the faint light of the cells loses its coherence and the energy cost of processing information soars. Speech, the newest human addition, goes first. Rather than more scans or drugs, he would have the astronaut do breath-hold free-diving runs to restart the skull-base vortex and draw deuterium out of the spinal fluid.

Tinnitus, he says, is the inner ear's fluid losing its structure, so the hair cells generate noise. Astronauts develop it within days in orbit, and on Earth it means something in the environment must be removed. For Ménière's disease he warns that a diuretic makes it worse. Hearing loss carried through bone, he adds, can be repaired with light, but where someone lives sets a ceiling: a place with a decent field but poor UV and near-infrared light limits how far repair can go.

#### Teeth-grinding and tinnitus as one disease

Kruse treats tinnitus and bruxism (clenching or grinding the teeth) as one disease. The nerves for the jaw, the face and hearing are packed together in a brainstem heavy with deuterium, so clenching triggers a surge of excitatory signals in the hearing pathway; with neuromelanin drained, nothing buffers it, and the brain hears the light leaking from overworked mitochondria in the inner ear as ringing. Clenching itself, he says, sends vibration through bone to the inner ear as the nervous system tries to drown out that static. He cites a study in which treating the jaw alone improved tinnitus in only 44% of people, and says lowering the load on the trigeminal nerve's hub does far better; dentists, he adds, aren't taught the link.

The endolymphatic sac, the inner ear's own drain, is another exhaust in his model. When the vagal loop through the stomach fails, back pressure thickens the inner-ear fluid with deuterium and shifts the tuning of the hair cells, which is why astronauts, whose drain stops at once, develop tinnitus within days. Sound sensitivity (hyperacusis), which many Bell's palsy patients develop, he reads as a warning that the system is close to collapse.

#### Headaches and facial pain

Headache, brain swelling and seizures come in his model from spinal fluid losing its charge. The fluid thickens, drains poorly and pressure rises, and solar storms can tip a vulnerable brain into this state. Migraines he attributes to deuterium loading in the trigeminal (Gasserian) ganglion, a nerve hub beside the brain.

Migraine and visual snow (a constant TV-static overlay on vision) are, to him, the brain perceiving light that leaks from its mitochondria once the melanin shield can no longer contain it, which he calls "Alien UPE." He also ties migraine to anxiety: about half of people with chronic migraine have anxiety, the hypothalamus both governs sodium and water and fires before the pain starts, and the spinal fluid of migraine sufferers runs high in sodium even when blood sodium is normal.

Right-sided facial pain follows the drainage. The right lymphatic duct and the right-side veins carry most of the brain's waste fluid, Kruse says; when that fluid is deuterium-heavy and slow, it presses on the first branch of the trigeminal nerve in Meckel's cave, a fluid pocket beside the brainstem, causing a pressure-type trigeminal neuralgia felt in the right eye.

In trigeminal neuralgia more generally, he notes that an artery always lies close to the nerve, so what varies from person to person is the fluid around it in Meckel's cave. When that fluid's capacity to hold charge drops from 160 toward 78, it stops insulating and current arcs across the nerve fibers, producing the notoriously severe pain. Many cases, he believes, involve a variant in SCN9A, the gene for a pain-signaling sodium channel (Nav1.7), primed by deuterium in the womb; it suits the tropics but fails by mid-life in high-latitude, wireless-heavy places. Surgery that cushions the nerve with a sponge (microvascular decompression) works, in his reading, by restoring smooth fluid flow so deuterium stops leaking there.

Meckel's cave also gathers pressure signals from the teeth, jaw and face, he says, so jaw-joint, tooth and facial pain are warning lights for a clogged skull base, and a phone held against the ear loads it directly. When its exhaust is blocked by earbuds, phones, too little chewing and the weakening field, the insulating cells of the hearing nerve can multiply into an acoustic neuroma, a benign tumor he reads as a wall built to keep deuterium and electronic noise away from the brainstem.

#### The spinal cord and discs

Meckel's cave matters for the spinal cord too. He says it supplies the mechanical "dither" that keeps water in the fourth ventricle moving, so carbon dioxide from the blood is stirred in, deuterium can be separated out, and the vagal region can clear it from a damaged stretch of cord. That is why he holds that myelomalacia, a softening of the spinal cord usually seen as permanent, can reverse, and why he looks for a blockage at Meckel's cave, often alongside sleep problems.

Degenerative disc disease is, in his view, a brain disease caused by deuterium in the spinal fluid. The jelly-like cores of the discs and the long ligaments running down the spine are the route by which the brain sends its waste heat down to the Earth; when a person is ungrounded and the fluid is heavy, that route overloads and the discs break down along with the arteries.

#### Bone and osteoporosis

Bone is not a calcium rock, Kruse says, but a crystal-and-collagen semiconductor held together at its junctions by copper-65. Under Earth's field, walking and other loading generate a steady current that charges bone and tells bone-building cells to keep working. In space, or under nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields) and night light on Earth, the copper loses its alignment and is pushed out, the bone's charge flips and osteoporosis follows. With the field weakening, he says, Earth now behaves increasingly like space.

Bone also loses its piezoelectric charge, the current it makes under load, when deuterium floods in from too little grounding or too little sun on the skin. Much of the bone lost from the skeleton, he adds, turns up in the arteries. Blood thinners such as warfarin block vitamin K2, the activator of the bone protein osteocalcin, so calcium spills into the artery lining and the pineal gland, giving soft bones and hard arteries at the same time, a single "polarity flip" in his phrase.

Citing Becker's work on bone growing in response to magnetic pressure, he reads a bone spur (osteophyte) on the spine as a calcified shadow of magnetic turbulence, laid down at the level where deuterium stalled, and calls such spurs "magnetic tafoni," after the honeycomb hollows wind carves in rock.

#### In practice

His nerve-injury protocol: for CRPS, secure grounding and a good field before putting sun or light on the limb; for head or face trauma with vertigo, ground, use his 3% saline approach with structural decompression, and get sunlight for nasal nitric oxide; after a brain bleed with new symptoms, get examined and imaged; and if Bell's palsy appears soon after arriving somewhere, suspect the place. For Ménière's, avoid diuretics. For tinnitus, teeth-grinding and facial pain he takes earbuds and phones away from the head, restores chewing and clears the skull base rather than treating the jaw alone. For hearing loss, spinal-cord softening and degenerating discs he relies on light, grounding and clearing the fluid routes rather than writing them off. He cautions against making nerve blocks, painkillers or anti-vertigo drugs the main strategy.

- **sources:** [381930](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381930/), [381468](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381468/), [369111](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/369111/), [385704](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385704/), [384185](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384185/), [377363](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377363/), [385877](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385877/), [376041](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/376041/), [382943](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382943/), [373261](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373261/), [373274](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373274/), [370592](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370592/)

### Pregnancy and birth
- **id:** pregnancy-and-birth
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse says a pregnant woman needs grounded sun more than extra calories, treats labor and nausea as functional signals, sees the fetus as a second deuterium sink for its mother (as in gestational diabetes), and links parents' deuterium load to birth defects and to the child's later insulin resistance.
- **protocols:** pregnancy-sun, pregnancy-birth-children

Kruse says a pregnant woman needs grounded sunlight more than extra calories. He reads severe pregnancy nausea and labor pain as purposeful signals rather than problems to suppress, and links the parents' load of deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen, at conception to birth defects.

#### Sun before calories

Standard prenatal advice, in Kruse's view, sets women up for gestational diabetes and preeclampsia: avoid the sun, wear chemical sunscreen, eat a calorie surplus and take synthetic prenatal pills. In a sun-starved mother surrounded by wireless signals, he says, extra food only piles up unused electrons and loads deuterium into the liver.

Grounded, full-spectrum sun does the opposite in his account. Near-infrared light hydrates the mother's melanin (the light-absorbing pigment), UV light anchors her organ clocks through light sensors in the skin and eyes, and her blood carries the sun's energy to the placenta, so she doesn't need to overeat.

The placenta, he says, is the fetus's stand-in ground, which he calls a "placental stator." Because the fetus can't touch the Earth, the placenta's twisted, one-handed villi act as a giant spin filter, creating a small magnetic field under the fetus that keeps its energy chain working during rapid growth.

The mother is also the baby's filter, or "fab plant." Nature, in his account, meant her to turn plant vitamin K1 into MK-4, the animal form of vitamin K, filtering out deuterium on the way, and to pass it on slowly through breast milk. The placenta likewise meters vitamin K on purpose, so babies are not "born deficient."

#### The fetus as a second sink

Kruse believes a mother can use her fetus as a second deuterium sink. Blood volume rises by about half in pregnancy, diluting the deuterium in her brainstem, which he gives as the reason multiple sclerosis often quiets during pregnancy; meanwhile she shunts heavy water and other heavy elements into the placenta. After the birth she loses that sink and the postpartum flare follows, while the child arrives preloaded, which he links to a narrow midface, extra teeth and autism or ADHD. He suggests preeclampsia may be an extreme salt dump meant to protect the fetus.

Gestational diabetes, in his account, is the same valve opened wider. A mother whose mitochondria can no longer deplete her own water pushes deuterium-heavy glucose across the placenta, and the fetal brain becomes the dumping ground. Babies normally lay down fat in the last trimester so the brain can insulate and pigment itself after birth, but a newborn lacks the melanin to sort deuterium and falls back on insulin-driven fat storage, so he rejects the usual idea of "fetal overnutrition." The fetus's own high insulin then programs later metabolic syndrome, obesity, heart disease, fatty liver, kidney disease, autism and childhood cancers.

The risk climbs with the mother's age, as her ability to clear her own water declines. At 47, he notes, gestational diabetes is several times likelier than in younger mothers, and miscarriage, chromosome errors, preeclampsia and heavy bleeding after birth all rise sharply. Pregnancy on or soon after GLP-1 weight-loss drugs is to him a "double strip": pregnancy already draws down the mother's DHA and fat to build the baby's nervous system, the drug has already dumped them, and the fetus is left without insulation.

#### Nausea, the heart and early warnings

Severe pregnancy nausea (hyperemesis gravidarum) is a protective brain alarm in Kruse's model. When deuterium floods the mother's blood, a stress hormone called GDF15 acts on the vomiting center at the floor of the brain. It stops her eating so she burns fat, which makes the clean, low-deuterium water the embryo needs to copy its mitochondria cleanly. Anti-nausea drugs silence the alarm without fixing its cause.

Pregnancy is a huge draw on the mother's mitochondria, he says. If her body water is loaded with deuterium, her heart muscle can tear itself apart trying to spin its energy motors "against concrete," as happens to astronauts. He sees Takotsubo or "broken-heart" cardiomyopathy, a sudden ballooning of the heart, as more than a grief reaction: anything that suddenly destroys melanin and the hormones made along its pathway (dopamine, noradrenaline, adrenaline) can dilate the heart, and nnEMF and a weakening field both qualify.

If the stress continues, he says, the fetus's liver raises alpha-fetoprotein (AFP), the protein measured in maternal blood tests, as a distress flare and shield. Morning sickness and newborn jaundice are, to him, early warnings of an oxygen problem before birth that later shows up as eye and skin disease.

#### Labor as an ignition switch

Labor pain is a "spintronic ignition switch," Kruse says. Contractions squeeze tissue the way pressure squeezes a crystal, sending an electrical surge up the spine that switches on oxytocin, endorphins and anti-inflammatory signals. Epidurals cut that signal, and he links them to worse tearing, breastfeeding trouble and postpartum fog. The squeeze of the birth canal and the mother's vaginal microbes, he adds, set the newborn's immune baseline.

Skin-to-skin contact after birth is physics in his model. He points to a 1995 case of premature twins in which the weaker sister, failing in her own incubator, stabilized as soon as nurses laid her beside her healthy twin: the stable twin, he says, acted as a steady rhythm that pulled her heart into step, while skin contact passed infrared warmth and drained the static charge that builds in an isolated incubator. Kangaroo care makes the mother, or a twin, the baby's "wireless charger." Breastfeeding works the same way: he proposes that the pigment of the nipple and areola turns morning light into a signal passed to the infant that tunes its body clock, which he values even more than the milk.

#### Conception and after

Parents who conceive under light stress, wireless exposure and no grounding, Kruse says, can't make low-deuterium water, so their sperm and eggs fill with deuterium. The embryo's spine needs very fluid water to lengthen; under the heavy load it protects itself with scar-like collagen that tethers the spinal cord to the base of the spine. He links the same germline load to heart defects.

He reads rarer conditions the same way. Tuberous sclerosis, a genetic disorder with benign growths in the brain, is in his view partly fetal triage: built amid the mother's deuterium, the fetus crystallizes heavy mass into these "tubers" to protect its brainstem. A child born with complete albinism (oculocutaneous albinism type 1A, with no melanin at all) is to him a readout of both parents' germ lines, and in one family he took the father's low overnight oxygen, without any sleep apnea, as a sign that his own shield was failing before conception.

The load carries into later life. Skin oils and the fat under the skin should act as deuterium sinks, but a mother with heavy body water, processed carbohydrates, little sun and a weak field forces the fetus to build its machinery with heavy hydrogen. Cells that can't make energy then cut back their insulin receptors to avoid damage, which he calls the true origin of insulin resistance, type 2 diabetes and obesity. For the child, he adds, a healthy mother of modest intellect is a better start than a brilliant one who is heavily deuterated, eats from a deuterium-heavy region and waits until 40 to conceive.

Postpartum depression, like sleep apnea, is in his view a disease of lost magnetism. The mother must become magnetically "pinned" (grounded under a stable, strong field) to let old depressive memories go and start fresh, and he says moving to Florida is not enough.

#### In practice

His pregnancy protocols: plenty of grounded sun with UV and infrared, including bare-eyed morning light; no chemical sunscreen or sun avoidance; no forced calorie surplus and no synthetic prenatal pills; and a wireless-free home, fixing the environment first in a high-risk pregnancy. He wants both parents' deuterium load dealt with before conception, wants the mother tanned before she conceives, and advises against conceiving on or soon after GLP-1 drugs. He favors unmedicated vaginal birth without an epidural where possible, skin-to-skin contact, and breastfeeding in morning light. For severe nausea he says to fix light, grounding and field rather than taking anti-nausea drugs, and for postpartum depression, light, water, magnetism and pinning rather than SSRIs.
