### Children, infants and autism
- **id:** children-and-autism
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse calls autism a "magnetic decline disease," reads tics, childhood anxiety, facial and dental crowding and SIDS as signs of an early deuterium load, and warns that indoor childhood, blue-light phototherapy and the newborn vitamin K shot set children up for lifelong problems.
- **protocols:** pregnancy-birth-children

Kruse calls autism a "magnetic decline disease" and says an indoor childhood, blue-light treatment for newborn jaundice and an early overload of deuterium (a heavy form of hydrogen) set children up for lifelong problems. Children have less buffering than adults, in his view, so their environment matters even more.

#### Autism as magnetic decline

Building on Douglas Wallace's work linking certain mitochondrial haplogroups (maternal lineages) to higher odds of autism, Kruse says a vulnerable lineage living in a mismatched place crosses an energy threshold. The brain uses about 20% of the body's energy at 2% of its mass, so it is the first to fail. A high-declination city, where compass north strays far from true north, combined with heavy nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields), pushes the brain's energy chain into a stall.

He adds that the fusion site on chromosome 2 trips under field loss and nnEMF, that autism rates track declination, and that a mother's vitamin D deficiency adds risk. He predicts autism should be rarest where agonic lines (zero-declination lines) cross the tropics or the equator.

Autistic sensory reactions, Kruse says, are protective triage: a brain that can't process everything sheds costly input, and a vacuum cleaner's noise sets off an alarm in unbuffered tissue. Autistic children sleep badly because a weak planetary field, LED light and indoor EMF disrupt their body clock.

Social connection, he argues, is a high-bandwidth signal that needs a quiet body clock. Without melanin to ground out deuterium and metal noise, the brain drops into a primitive "safe mode" that limits bandwidth, so the child is overloaded rather than antisocial, and a noisy brain steers POMC toward the stress hormone ACTH instead of beta-endorphin, the bonding signal. In autism the system turns its gain up and floods the brain with raw input (the "intense world"); ADHD, in his reading, is a jittering clock, with hyperactivity a physical search for a magnetically quiet place to reset. He describes the six-layered cortex as a filter built to screen out symmetrical man-made signals such as blue light and nnEMF, and says flooding a young brain with them keeps the cortex from unfolding properly.

#### Infants and the unfinished vagus

An infant's vagus nerve is not yet myelinated (insulated), so in Kruse's model it can't cope with a deuterium overload. Deuterium jams the baby's energy motors, and the 2 Hz rhythm he believes keeps tissues in sync breaks down. The baby becomes a "foreign astronaut" in its own crib, unable to lock its clocks to the sun and planet, and is set up for developmental delay, sensory problems, gut imbalance, broken sleep and early metabolic trouble. How badly a child is hit, he says, depends on the mother's condition before and during pregnancy.

Sudden infant death syndrome (SIDS) he calls a "Lagrangian stall." A baby born preloaded with deuterium and heavy elements, with too little melanin to bind them, must switch from fetal to adult hemoglobin, a step steered by the BCL11A gene that he says needs a surge of voltage the infant may not be able to make. Fetal hemoglobin holds on to oxygen tightly, starving the brainstem's mitochondria, so a small extra stressor such as artificial light or sleeping face down can tip the breathing centers into permanent apnea. He reads the low butyrylcholinesterase, a blood enzyme, reported in babies who died of SIDS as a sign that their arousal system crashed under a too-heavy brainstem.

Blue-light phototherapy for newborn jaundice, which he says replaced full-spectrum light over the past 35 years, permanently disrupts the clock run by heme, the iron-containing molecule in blood. Citing Oláh and colleagues (2013), he notes more atypical moles, a melanoma risk marker, in children who had it.

He groups cradle cap (infant seborrheic dermatitis) with jaundice as an early sign of mitochondrial trouble rather than a passing effect of the mother's hormones, since children who have it show more metabolic problems later. Skin, to him, is the outward map of how efficiently mitochondria run, and in adults the same rash is a recognized marker of metabolic syndrome.

#### The newborn vitamin K shot

Kruse questions the routine vitamin K injection at birth. Turning the injected K1 into MK-4, the form mammals use to manage calcium, has to pass through menadione, a step that uses up glutathione, which he calls the cell's main defense against deuterium and oxidative stress; he likens the drain to what acetaminophen does in adults. Nature delivers vitamin K as a "slow leak" through the placenta and breast milk, and a large dose is, to him, a high-voltage event in a low-voltage infant whose detox systems aren't yet running.

In a hospital full of nnEMF he calls the shot an "nnEMF multiplier." With glutathione drained, the inner mitochondrial membrane fails, ATP synthase slows on deuterium-laden fuel, and the brain fails to prune its connections and lay down myelin. He calls this "isotopic ossification" and links it to developmental delay and autism.

#### Tics, faces and teeth

Tics are surging in children, Kruse says, because earbuds, blue light and wireless now land on top of a weakening field, with little chewing and little sun to help. In Tourette's he sees deuterium soaking the brainstem once the fourth ventricle can't sort it, swelling and thinning the myelin so signals jump between neighboring nerve centers: crosstalk at the facial nerve's center gives facial tics, and at the vagus and its neighbor, vocal ones. The tics themselves, he suggests, may be an involuntary attempt to shake the heavy fluid loose, a violent version of chewing.

He traces a narrow midface, extra or missing teeth, crowded bites and scoliosis to the same early load. A mother's sluggish bicarbonate exhaust floods the embryo with deuterium, which slows the neural-crest cells that build the face (he was born with extra teeth himself, in New York City), and scoliosis is the spine twisting in search of magnetic north when the brainstem has been blinded. Such children often breathe through the mouth and struggle to focus or sleep. A child raised on strong morning and infrared light without nnEMF can, he says, keep unfolding the midface over time.

#### Indoor childhood

Children kept indoors under blue light and away from the ground are, to Kruse, low-capacitance "astronauts" with no outside field to guide their electrons. PANS/PANDAS, the sudden onset of tics, rage and OCD often blamed on strep, he calls a disorder of a deuterated terrain, set up by desert living, wireless-heavy classrooms, blue light and deuterium-rich tap water. Strep, in his account, arrives as a clean-up crew, so antibiotics kill the helpers.

Pinworms, likewise, settle in the still, heavy-water "ponds" of a child whose drainage routes aren't flushing, and chronic infections in children mean both child and parents are carrying deuterium. The worms' night itching, he adds, blocks the brain's deep-sleep clean-up.

He says GLP-1 weight-loss drugs leave about 1 in 8 children with vitamin D deficiency within a year. Bedwetting is often a deuterium-loaded skull base acting on the pituitary's water-balance signal, not a behavior problem or a missing hormone.

#### Childhood anxiety

Anxiety is rising fastest in the youngest groups, Kruse says. He cites Global Burden of Disease and CDC data placing most first episodes between ages 3 and 11, a 27% rise in diagnoses among 3- to 17-year-olds between 2016 and 2019, and the child mental-health emergency pediatricians declared in 2021. Children are the most exposed, in his view, because neuromelanin builds up only with age and most myelin is laid down before age two, so they have the thinnest insulation just when they face the heaviest frequency load.

Spinal fluid is filtered from blood, so the deuterium that nnEMF and blue light push into a child's tissues ends up in the fluid around the brain too. Sleep suffers, excitatory signaling climbs, and anxiety follows; low sodium, which he says unsettles the amygdala's serotonin and dopamine, adds to it. He calls a child's anxiety or ADHD a predictable distortion from a noisy environment rather than a random genetic error, and wants a biophysical fix rather than talk therapy alone.

#### In practice

His pregnancy, birth and childhood protocol: no blue-light phototherapy for newborn jaundice; no heavy deuterium load for an infant before its nerves are insulated, which for him includes a skeptical look at the high-dose vitamin K shot; and children kept grounded. For autistic children who sleep badly, cut night-time LED light and indoor EMF. For PANS/PANDAS, take the child out of wireless-heavy rooms, put bare feet on the earth, use full sun including near-infrared, and favor deuterium depletion over repeated antibiotics. For tics and anxiety he removes earbuds and screens, adds real chewing, morning UV-A on eyes and skin, midday infrared, total darkness at night and barefoot time. He gives parents simple signs to watch: a lag between instruction and action, pupils that stay wide or react sluggishly, a child who is "tired but wired" at night, frequent static shocks, frizzy hair or dry, ashy skin, and a child who calms down barefoot. No GLP-1 drugs for children. For autism generally he suggests living on or near an agonic line, ideally in the tropics.

### Fertility, cycles and pelvic voltage
- **id:** fertility-cycles-voltage
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse ties menstrual rhythm, egg and sperm quality, erections, orgasm and menopause to lunar ground currents, sunlight, fat and DHA stores and pelvic voltage, which devices and indoor life collapse.
- **protocols:** moon-cycle-routine, male-fertility

Kruse ties menstrual rhythm, erections and sperm quality to sunlight, moon-driven currents in the ground and the electrical charge of pelvic tissue. Devices, high-rise living and indoor life collapse that charge, he says, and fertility shows it first because one pathway governs both fertility and longevity.

#### Sperm as the purest test

Sperm gave up their repair machinery to travel light, Kruse says, so they can't compensate when the environment fails, which makes them the clearest readout of where a man lives. If DNA damage in sperm climbs from about 20% to 35% within a few months, he reads that as a new, ongoing stressor acting across two or three of the 74–90-day sperm production cycles.

In his example the stressor was a move to a 24th-floor apartment. High up, he says, the body sits thousands of volts above the ground, unable to draw electrons from the Earth, and acts like an ungrounded antenna. Deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen, then adds mass to the sperm's DNA so that its copying machinery stalls and breaks. Place matters at a larger scale too: he calls San Diego, home to a major wireless-chip company, a poor place to start a family, saying it has among the highest rates of autism and infertility anywhere.

#### Eggs kept offline

Eggs take the opposite strategy. Citing a *Nature* paper showing that egg cells suppress Complex I, the energy chain's entry point and its main source of reactive oxygen, Kruse says the female germline is kept largely offline from before birth, like a satellite powering down to cross a radiation zone. That lets an egg stay a quiet "quantum seed" for decades.

Egg quality is falling, he says, because night light, nnEMF and the weakening field force Complex I back on, so the egg burns deuterium-heavy fuel and "browns" from within. Citing a 2026 paper in which long days (18 hours of light, 6 of dark) disrupted the ovary's NAD+ metabolism (a key electron carrier) and impaired its follicles, he calls a long light day a trap that mimics indoor "magnetic darkness."

A broken body clock also stalls the vagus-driven bicarbonate flush that clears deuterium from the gut, sending it into the pelvis and the follicular fluid around the egg. The follicle then shifts to fermentation and fails to ovulate, and he expects ovarian failure to cluster where the field is weakest.

Fertilization itself, he proposes, is magnetic reconnection, the snapping and rejoining of field lines that also drives solar flares. The resting egg is like Earth's core before a reversal, its field repeatedly "trying" to flip but held in place until the sperm supplies the chaotic trigger; he likens a weakening field such as the South Atlantic Anomaly to an unfertilized egg.

#### Cycles and the moon

Citing Harold Saxton Burr's recordings of tree voltage and the work of Georges Lakhovsky, Kruse says living tissue tracks the moon, the sun and magnetic storms. Moon-driven ground currents (telluric currents) once synchronized women's cycles to a place's "electromagnetic terroir," the way they time coral spawning a few days after the late-spring full moon. Women living together in nature, he says, synchronize like pendulum clocks on a shared wall, and city light and a weakened field break both rhythms.

#### Hormones as stand-ins for charge

Hormones, in Kruse's model, are stand-ins for the body's local electrical charge. Night light and nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields) damage melanin and the eye's daylight sensor, and lack of grounding drains charge from the mitochondria.

Sex hormones start as cholesterol turned into pregnenolone inside the mitochondria by iron-containing enzymes that depend on a stable field. In a weak field, he says, the cell diverts pregnenolone to the stress hormone cortisol to survive, which he calls the "pregnenolone steal." Progesterone then crashes and the monthly cycle fades; he warns of widespread infertility, endometriosis and early menopause. Heavy menstrual bleeding signals a missing "Zeeman handshake," his term for the field keeping electron spins aligned.

Leptin, the hormone made by body fat, acts as a gatekeeper. When the hypothalamus senses that DHA is depleted and fat stripped away, whether by a culture of extreme leanness or by GLP-1 drugs, leptin falls, the pulses of GnRH (the brain hormone that starts each reproductive cycle) shut down, and the body declines a pregnancy that would build a heavy, "noisy" child. Without DHA to hold the sun's charge, he says, even a fertilized egg can't be sustained, which he offers as the cause of much "unexplained infertility."

Menopausal hot flashes are, to him, an exhaust vent rather than a fault in the brain's thermostat. A woman who no longer sheds deuterium with a monthly period has to dump its heat another way, and as melanin falls, the faint light her mitochondria give off can no longer be recycled, so the skin flushes to release it.

#### Erectile dysfunction and orgasm

Kruse calls erectile dysfunction the male version of a lost cycle: a whole-body voltage collapse, not a plumbing problem. An erection needs nitric oxide from an iron-containing enzyme that depends on precise electron spins. A phone in the pocket or a laptop on the lap exposes the groin to alternating fields that, citing Liboff and Blackman's work on ion cyclotron resonance, pull calcium out of cells and drop pelvic voltage, while the pregnenolone steal sends cholesterol to cortisol instead of testosterone. Ancestral men, he says, stayed virile because full sun and bare feet kept their pelvic tissue charged.

Orgasm, in his model, is a fluid surge that pushes the brain into an urgent "de-frag," which is why sleep afterward is often the deepest dream sleep. Female ejaculation, fluid from the Skene's glands around the urethra (the female counterpart of the prostate), is to him a normal, healthy flush of the pelvic nerve network, not incontinence. Inability to climax, dementia and thyroid disease share one culprit in his view: a clogged deuterium exhaust.

He sees the lower spine and pelvis as a sump below the brainstem where heavy water settles, and female ejaculation as its "lumbo-sacral de-frag": strong pelvic-floor contractions spin deuterium out of the Skene's glands. Most women, he notes, don't know these glands make PSA (prostate-specific antigen). PSA is a protease, an enzyme that cuts proteins, and he suggests it acts as a local cleaner for the protein "sludge" that ubiquitin has tagged for removal once deuterium disturbs it, so the fluid is the isotopic and protein exhaust of a cellular reboot. He links this periodic flush to women's lower rate of ALS. A light-starved perineum, in his account, lets pelvic melanin slide back into its precursors and weakens the flush.

Climax gets harder, he says, because dopamine can't drive the pelvic muscles against a thicker, deuterium-heavy base. Intravenous 3% saline, given by a clinician, pulls that heavy fluid out of the pelvic tissue into the blood to be cleared, lowering viscosity so dopamine can work again; he suggests timing it three to five days before a period so the body sheds the load with it.

#### In practice

For men his protocol is to take phones and laptops out of pockets and off the lap and clear alternating-current sources from the workspace; use groin cold (ice packs or cold plunges); and sleep on a grounded sheet with the bed aligned north–south. For sperm DNA damage, live at ground level (or shield and ground the bed) and drink certified deuterium-depleted water under 105 ppm for 90 days, one full production cycle. Before conceiving, he wants both partners' deuterium load addressed and wants enough body fat and DHA on board that leptin keeps the cycle running, which rules out GLP-1 drugs and extreme leanness. For women with difficult climax he points to UV-A sunlight on the perineum and, with a clinician, the pre-period 3% saline flush. His moon-cycle routine, written to restore the menstrual cycle, calls for strict darkness or full blue-blocking after sunset (strictest around the full moon), morning sun, 20–60 minutes of barefoot grounding a day with more near full and new moons, time in stronger-field places, cool dark sleep and cold, and tracking cycle, mood and heart-rate variability over two to three lunar months.

- **sources:** [378967](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378967/), [378968](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378968/), [370246](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370246/), [378974](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378974/), [378976](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378976/), [378971](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378971/), [384732](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384732/), [373379](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373379/), [373384](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373384/), [372385](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372385/), [372386](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372386/), [373079](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373079/)

### GLP-1 drugs, anorexia and the lost fat capacitor
- **id:** glp1-drug-damage
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse says GLP-1 weight-loss drugs force a crude deuterium flush that ends in lost pleasure, lost muscle and collagen, stripped nerve insulation, a hijacked vagus, vision loss and a "welded-shut" exhaust, and that anorexia shares the damage without the fat buffer.
- **protocols:** glp1-damage-offset, anorexia-recovery

Kruse considers GLP-1 weight-loss drugs (semaglutide, tirzepatide, retatrutide) harmful overall. In his model they force a crude, off-schedule flush of deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen. The flush brings short-term benefits but leaves people unable to feel pleasure, losing muscle and collagen, and with the body's own deuterium exhaust shut. He groups anorexia with them because it shares the damage but lacks the body fat that would buffer it.

#### The exhaust the drugs hijack

Kruse's starting point is the body's natural deuterium exit. Morning sunlight's infrared triggers the cortisol awakening response, the hormone rise after waking. That switches on the glucagon gene on chromosome 2, the same gene that makes GLP-1 in the gut, and releases about 2 liters of bicarbonate fluid from the pancreas. Guided by the vagus nerve, the flush carries deuterium out through the gut.

Much modern hunger, he says, is a false famine. Deuterium-heavy food eaten indoors under LEDs jams ATP synthase, the cell's energy motor, and the brain reads the resulting electron shortage as a lack of calories; he calls this the "hunger mirror." GLP-1 drugs silence that signal without fixing what sends it.

#### A crude flush

The injected drug, Kruse says, bypasses sunlight's timing and forces a continuous, crude deuterium purge, a "quantum centrifuge." For a while this clears deuterium from blood-vessel linings and kidney filters. That, he argues, explains the roughly 20% drop in heart and kidney events in the SELECT and FLOW trials, which did not track weight loss. He calls it a "cardioprotective illusion": a real effect, but not a cure, and paid for later in the ways below.

#### Loss of pleasure

The first cost is anhedonia, a loss of pleasure that he distinguishes from depression because there is no guilt or worthlessness. The drugs act in the brainstem and reward centers and damp the pulsed dopamine surges behind wanting. He adds that the forced clamp on mitochondrial energy leaves none of the loose fluctuation dopamine needs. Users lose interest not only in food but in sex, work, hobbies and friends, and he wants this screened with the SHAPS pleasure scale rather than depression questionnaires.

#### Muscle, collagen and hair

Citing Helen Blau's lab at Stanford, Kruse says semaglutide raises 15-PGDH, an aging enzyme he calls a "gerozyme." It breaks down prostaglandin E2, a repair signal, and so locks muscle stem cells in dormancy. With muscle unable to repair, even moderate exercise acts like overtraining. He cites figures from a 2026 Blau lab preprint in which, on semaglutide, muscle stem-cell function fell to about a fifth of normal, muscle mass to just over half, and regeneration after injury to under a tenth. Muscle, he adds, is the body's main store of the water used to make energy, so losing it drains the battery.

Any drug that drives up metabolic rate, he says, forces ATP synthase to turn against a deuterium-heavy, "concrete" medium and damages the junctions of the inner mitochondrial membrane. GLP-1 drugs do the same, which he compares to the muscle astronauts lose in space, and he sees an underactive thyroid as the body's adaptive slowdown under deuterium.

In his model the drugs plus nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields) stall the energy chain and flip oxygen into its reactive singlet form. Mitochondrial DNA then leaks out, sets off the cell's virus alarms and jams the 2 Hz rhythm he says stem cells listen to. The stem-cell niche is left overheated and dried out, a state he calls "focal lattice lock."

The multi-agonists that also hit the glucagon receptor (tirzepatide and retatrutide) push tissue further, he says. The chromosome 2 "fuse" blows, and muscle and collagen revert to loose, primitive tissue, so he expects hypermobile, Ehlers-Danlos-like connective tissue and 35-year-olds with the bones of 80-year-olds. In type 2 diabetics, whose energy chain is already stalled, the calorie cut comes out of muscle rather than fat.

Hair loss is triage. A sudden shock and a reduced supply of nutrients push many follicles into their shedding phase about two to four months in, and losing weight faster than 3–4 lb a week is the strongest predictor. He watches ferritin, zinc, vitamin D3, B12 and protein, notes that about 1 in 8 children on these drugs develop vitamin D deficiency within a year, and estimates the drugs cost ten to twenty years of life.

#### Stripped insulation

The drugs also strip fat that insulates the nerves. "Ozempic face," the hollowed look of rapid weight loss, is to him the breakdown of facial fat pads that shield the visual pathways and the trigeminal nerve, and losing the fat cushion around the brain's fluid vortex leaves it more exposed to a shifting field. Pair a GLP-1 drug, which removes the fat shield, with a statin, which removes the cholesterol shield, and he says the nervous system is left "naked," which he links to reports of suicidal thoughts and anhedonia on the drugs.

#### The welded-shut exhaust

While the drug forces its flush, Kruse says, the natural route idles and fails. GLP-1 drugs cut the pancreas's bicarbonate output and slow the gut, and GLP-1 receptors on the vagus nerve's sensory hub (the nodose ganglion) overload with deuterium; gastroparesis, bowel problems and fatty liver follow. The liver's P450 enzymes, which process steroids and fat-soluble vitamins, are scrambled. In his view users move from a stalled small intestine to Crohn's disease and then gut cancer, and the deuterium backing up from the gut can reach the brain and set up Parkinson's.

Handing a body function to a drug, he says, lets the organ waste away. The drugs are a borrowed copy of the vagus nerve's own fullness loop, so the circuits that sense and dump deuterium go dormant and users lose what he calls "deuterium interoception," the felt sense of their own isotope load. A stomach that empties slowly lets deuterium back up into the gut's immune tissue, producing SIBO and apparent sensitivities to food and protein.

He points to 3,636 active lawsuits gathered in one federal case (MDL 3094), mostly over gastroparesis, a paralyzed stomach, as evidence that the drugs hijack the vagus. Without the nerve's electrical instructions the stomach lining stalls, building up deuterium and low oxygen. Low oxygen also blocks DHA, the omega-3 fat that needs high oxygen pressure to enter the brain, cutting the brain off from light-driven melanin repair.

The same lawsuits now include permanent vision loss from NAION, a stroke of the optic nerve, which he calls the eye syndrome of spaceflight produced on Earth. The optic nerve is so dense with mitochondria that deuterium easily locks it up, and its UV-A light sensor can no longer turn light into electricity. In astronauts, he says, the headward fluid shift of weightlessness pushes heavy spinal fluid against the back of the eye, flattening the globe and swelling the optic nerve head (SANS), and GLP-1 users are losing sight through the same blocked fluid exhaust.

He compares the drugs to Pervitin, the methamphetamine given to soldiers in the Second World War: both flood the body with CLIP, a misprocessed fragment of the hormone precursor POMC, and "weld shut" the vagal bicarbonate exhaust. When the drug stops, deuterium backs into the insulin-making beta cells, POMC keeps yielding CLIP instead of fat-burning alpha-MSH, and hunger rebounds frantically, with weight returning as fat alongside inflammation and insulin resistance. Grey-market retatrutide is worse still, he says, and he predicts a rise in insulin-secreting tumors (insulinomas).

He goes as far as calling the drugs "glioblastoma in a pen." Like a glioblastoma cell that gets the order to die but cannot finish, users are held in a sublethal stall: deuterium backs up instead of leaving, which in his account is why the rebound is so aggressive. Breaking down muscle while the heavy debris stays inside raises heteroplasmy, the share of damaged mitochondrial DNA, and the backflow reaches the thalamus and substantia nigra first. Users end up looking thin while, in his phrase, being "inertially massive," dense with heavy isotopes and unusually sensitive to solar flares and 5G.

#### Anorexia: the same damage without the buffer

Anorexia, in Kruse's account, shares the GLP-1 damage but lacks the "fat capacitor," body fat acting as an electrical buffer and energy store. Leptin, the fat hormone he calls the master photoelectric transducer because it reports stored energy to the brain, approaches zero. Its "carrier wave" vanishes and the brain reads endless starvation, producing distorted body image and obsessive loops. The starved gut has also wasted, so it can't extract enough energy from food.

He reads the loss of appetite at the start of anorexia as a sign that electron flow is overloading the cell. Leptin, in his model, has a food side and a sun side, and a body that can no longer handle food mass is pushed toward the sun side, shedding mass to keep its mitochondria from overheating.

#### In practice

Kruse's GLP-1 damage protocol has four core steps: morning sun where the field is clean, ideally at zero declination and away from all wireless; deuterium-depleted water at 90–105 ppm with frequent cold immersion at 45–55 °F; unshielded early-morning infrared and UV-A on skin and eyes; and pure, fresh (2S)-hesperidin timed to sun exposure. For gut damage he adds the "bicarb hack," oral baking soda to restart the flush, and he rules the drugs out for children and warns against combining them with statins. He frames coming off the drugs as the basics of his leptin reset, and has also described a clinician-run "Crater Flush" built around intravenous methylene blue and vitamin C to restart a stalled stomach. His anorexia recovery base keeps the same foundation but replaces ice baths with short, gentle dips in 80–85 °F water, going colder only as fat returns. It maximizes morning UV-A and infrared so melanin can supply charge directly, bypassing the weakened gut, and adds liquid ionic magnesium and trace copper-histidine timed with the morning sun.

### Signs of a clogged exhaust: skin, spine, ventricles and blood
- **id:** exhaust-failure-signs
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse reads vitiligo, melasma, neurocutaneous spots, spinal and white-matter changes, abnormal ventricles, central sleep apnea, clumsy children and abnormal red cells as visible readouts of one failure, a clogged deuterium exhaust, and even recasts several modern diagnoses as nature's experiments.
- **protocols:** morning-bowel-movement

Kruse reads many separate diagnoses as readouts of a single failure: the body's system for clearing deuterium (heavy hydrogen) is clogging. In his model the skin, scans of the spine and brain, the shape of the brain's fluid chambers, breathing during sleep and even the red blood cells each show where the clog sits. He builds this on his "Human GPS," the three axes of the skull and trunk: the sphenoid bone as the clock-setting switchboard (the X-axis) and the notochord line from skull base to sacrum as the drain (the Y-axis).

#### Skin as a thermal map

Kruse believes vitiligo, the loss of pigment in white patches, follows the blood supply. A patch on a dermatome (the strip of skin served by one spinal nerve) tracks how thick the blood is in the artery feeding it, an "infrared shadow" he says thermography (heat imaging) would confirm. He says people with vitiligo all have low vitamin D because indoor life and heavy technology use thicken their blood, and that the answer is to purify the water in the arteries rather than apply creams.

He does not see it as autoimmunity but as a controlled shutdown. When the proton tunneling powered by the sun's 0.66 electron-volt infrared stalls for lack of IR-A light, cell water drops into what he calls "78-sludge," its low charge-holding state. Keeping melanin in that skin would then act like a lightning rod on a shorted circuit and burn out the mitochondria beneath, so the skin–brain pigment circuit (the MITF–AMPAR loop) ejects it; he calls this "Electronic Winter" and the result "rhino skin." Vitiligo on the face, he says, points to a noisy, clogged trigeminal ganglion, the relay of the face's main nerve, with thermal failure across its three branches (V1–V3). He says he pieced this together from facial-pain and Tourette's patients over a 30-year neurosurgical career and confirms it with facial thermography.

Melasma, the brown facial patches, and actinic keratoses, the rough sun spots dermatologists treat as precancers, are the opposite in his model: too much pigment over a leaking wire. Where an internal conductor such as the internal carotid artery or a trigeminal branch silts up with deuterium, faint light leaks from it and the pigment circuit thickens the melanin shield over that dermatome. Melasma on the forehead and cheeks flags a stalling vortex in the fourth ventricle and an overheating brain. He calls actinic keratoses isotopic "clinkers" rather than sun damage, and says reading the skin can locate neurodegeneration more cheaply than a PET scan. People with these spots sleep poorly, he adds, because drainage of the brain during dream sleep is too weak to clear its deuterium.

#### Neurocutaneous signs

Kruse claims to unite dermatology and neurology here. The skin findings that travel with nervous-system disease, in Turcot and Peutz-Jeghers syndromes, neurofibromatosis types 1 and 2, ash-leaf spots, café-au-lait spots, iris changes, melasma, vitiligo and acanthosis nigricans (dark velvety skin in body folds), are to him the skin displaying an inner failure to sort deuterium. Café-au-lait spots are overcharged melanin "sinks," vitiligo is a tripped breaker, acanthosis is a "velvet clog" of insulin resistance, and ash-leaf spots and iris changes are "dead pixels" where the brain's 40 Hz timing lost resolution. He says he understood this only after operating on 45 tumors of peripheral nerves, and apologizes to those patients.

He separates hardware from software. Skin lesions show hardware damage; a metallic taste is the software alarm. Acanthosis together with a metallic taste means, in his estimate, an exhaust about 90% clogged, and giving that person nicotine or a gadolinium contrast scan, he says, amounts to tossing a grenade into an engine that has already stalled. His route out is tropical sun, a hummed low "M" and the body's bicarbonate loop, after which, he says, the taste clears, the acanthosis thins and the iris regains its clarity.

#### What a nerve block flushes

Kruse explains local anesthetic blocks through the same plumbing. Lidocaine stiffens the fatty membrane and the water beside it so ions cannot flow. When it wears off, the relaxing water releases a hydraulic surge that washes built-up deuterium toward Meckel's cave (the fluid pouch of the trigeminal relay) or the vagal exhaust, a section-by-section power flush.

#### Spine and white matter

On brain scans, Kruse reads white-matter hyperintensities (bright spots on MRI) as trouble along the notochord axis, matched by Modic changes, signal changes in the vertebral bone beside a disc. Degenerative disc disease, to him, warns that the notochord line is about to fail, because blue light and nnEMF (man-made wireless and electrical fields) make that axis leak biophotons. A misaligned sphenoid, he adds, kinks the cranial nerves, the vagus among them, and the structural collapse that follows can show up as scoliosis or a syrinx (a fluid cavity in the spinal cord).

He ties failure of the sphenoid axis to autism, Alzheimer's, heart-rhythm problems and mental illness. Because spinal fluid is filtered from blood, he says, brain and circulatory disease, peripheral artery disease included, tend to arrive together.

#### Ventricle shape

The brain's fluid chambers, in his model, are shaped to work as a cyclone separator for heavy isotopes. Slit ventricles, too narrow to form a vortex, are what he sees in pseudotumor cerebri, in the eye-and-brain syndrome of astronauts (SANS) and in PANDAS: deuterium settles in still fluid and pressure rises with no tumor present. Normal-pressure hydrocephalus is the reverse, ventricles swelling like a backed-up sewer.

#### Breathing at night

Central sleep apnea, in which the brain stops sending the signal to breathe, is to Kruse a stalled respiratory motor. The breathing centers of the lower brainstem sit where deuterium and heavy metals collect when the pancreas's bicarbonate system fails, and with too little voltage the brain "forgets" to breathe. He says this explains the sudden sleep problems of children with PANS or PANDAS, whose systems can no longer hold the rhythmic 40 Hz "M" tone he treats as essential to autonomous life.

#### Clumsy children and the bath test

Deuterium, he says, makes the fluid of the inner ear's balance canals heavy and sluggish, so it lags behind the head and brings motion sickness, poor balance and dyspraxia (clumsiness in planning movement). His "Buoyancy Check" is simple: if a child's sensory overload disappears in a bath or pool, water pressure is compensating, which he calls the clearest sign of deuterium toxicity and a reason to bring in the pressure of diving.

#### Red cells and salt

Kruse explains why mature red blood cells drop their mitochondria and nucleus. Without them they do not burn the oxygen they carry and run instead on sugar fermentation; they can fold through narrow capillaries, since their flexible disc shape sets how thick blood is in the smallest vessels; they keep glutathione reduced through the pentose phosphate pathway; and they devote themselves to carbonic anhydrase, turning carbon dioxide into the bicarbonate that feeds the vagus-linked exhaust. He calls the red cell a mobile semiconductor. In sickle cell disease and lupus, he says, red cells that keep mitochondria thicken the blood and start tissue breaking down.

He reads cystic fibrosis, with its salty sweat, as a tropical adaptation for shedding deuterium rather than a disease, and says that is why people with it die young at high latitudes, where the sun no longer subsidizes the leak.

#### Diseases as experiments

Kruse goes furthest in proposing that since the Carrington storm of 1859 broke the magnetic "stator" of the human engine, many diagnoses are nature's experiments in regaining lift without magnetic help. Hypothyroidism supercools cell water by lowering metabolism. Osteopenia lightens bone so vibration reaches the fourth ventricle. Craniosynostosis, the early fusing of skull sutures, tries out new skull shapes, and brain arteriovenous malformations act as radiators dumping heat. Brain injury, PTSD, CTE, autism, ADHD, OCD and the dementias are, to him, experiments in isotope sorting, with autistic and ADHD brains working as "high-pass filters" under a heavy load of light. Humans, he concludes, are shifting from a magnetic-based to a "dielectric-based" species.

#### In practice

Kruse uses the skin, a heat camera and the patient's sense of taste as cheap gauges before expensive scans. His fixes aim at the water in the arteries and spinal fluid: full-spectrum sun including infrared, grounding, a tropical location where possible, humming and the bicarbonate loop, with diving pressure for children who pass the bath test. He would avoid nicotine and gadolinium contrast in someone whose skin and taste say the exhaust is badly clogged.

## Glossary

| Term | Plain meaning (in his usage) |
|---|---|
| 0.66 eV / 1878 nm (ignition switch) | The short-wave infrared band of sunlight whose photon energy he says cytochrome c oxidase needs to finish turning oxygen into water; LEDs lack it. |
| 100th meridian | John Wesley Powell's humid-East/arid-West line, which he treats as a moving boundary of good water. |
| 110 Hz (chamber resonance) | The pitch at which Neolithic stone chambers ring; he says it amplified chanting and calms the brain's language and planning areas. |
| 128 Hz fork | A tuning fork used on stiff joints, nerves and, in the evening, the sternum or neck as a calming vagal brake. |
| 160 vs 78 | His numbers for the dielectric constant of structured, charge-holding cell water (160) versus ordinary bulk water (78). |
| 2 Hz rhythm (carrier) | A slow, universal rhythm he says keeps tissues and stem cells in sync; falling water and chanting "OM" match it. |
| 25-hydroxyvitamin D (25-OH-D) | The vitamin D blood marker doctors test; to him a gauge a pill can paint over without real light. |
| 30 million volts per meter (30 MV/m) | The electric field he says a healthy inner mitochondrial membrane holds, likened to a lightning bolt. |
| 380 nm light | Violet/UV-A dawn light that neuropsin reads to set the body clock; he calls it the "mTOR switch.". |
| 4.3-second stutter | His figure for how often a deuterium atom strikes the system on ordinary 155 ppm water. |
| 40 Hz fork | A tuning fork at the brain's gamma frequency, used mornings, on the spine and on the hard palate. |
| 43% red light engine | Red and infrared (about 43% of sunlight) spinning the liver's ATP synthase on light hydrogen, so it needs less food fuel. |
| 4D-flow MRI | A scan that tracks blood motion in three dimensions over time; he cites it showing a bicuspid valve's tight helical jet. |
| 51% attack | A cryptocurrency term for seizing a network by controlling most of it; his image for technology overpowering the cell's deuterium clearance. |
| 71-terahertz vibration | A mitochondrial vibration (Song et al., 2026) he reads as a timing signal pacing the nucleus and telomeres while the membrane holds charge. |
| Acanthosis nigricans | Dark, velvety skin in body folds linked to insulin resistance; he calls it a "velvet clog.". |
| Acetylcholine | The vagus nerve's chemical messenger; raised by baking soda in his account. |
| Acoustic medicine | His name for the healing use of sound, such as pre-1900 hospital bells, which he says the Flexner era replaced with chemistry. |
| Acoustic neuroma | A benign tumor of the hearing nerve's insulating cells; he reads it as a wall against deuterium and electronic noise. |
| ACTH | The pituitary hormone cut from POMC that drives the adrenal glands; he says sunrise light helps make it. |
| Action (physics) | The running total of a system's kinetic minus potential energy over time, which nature tends to minimize; he uses it as an organism's lifetime energy bill. |
| Acute lattice lock | A sudden lock in an already loaded person when demand spikes, as in exercise or infection. |
| Adaptive healing | Robert Becker's idea, as he uses it, that tissue must rebuild for a new environment, so people may feel worse before better after moving. |
| Adipose capacitor (fat capacitor) | Body fat acting as an electrical buffer and energy store that also holds deuterium away from vital organs. |
| Agonic line | A line where declination is zero, so compass north equals true north; he treats living near one as protective. |
| Akkermansia | A mucus-protecting gut bacterium that declines when the body clock breaks. |
| Albumin | The main blood protein that holds water inside vessels; he says morning sun powers its production. |
| Alien UPE | Disordered faint light leaking from an overdriven cell node that cannot pass its energy on; to him the distress signal of a disconnected node. |
| Alkaline tide | The bicarbonate released into the blood each time the stomach pumps out acid. |
| Alpha cells | The pancreas cells that make glucagon; birds carry many more than mammals. |
| Amacrine cells | Nerve cells in the retina; he notes that in birds they make glucagon locally. |
| AMOC | The Atlantic's great overturning current; he says its slowdown leaves deuterium in East Coast surface water. |
| AMOC / SMOC | The Atlantic and Southern ocean overturning currents, which he says generate their own magnetic fields tied to the dynamo. |
| AMPA receptors | Fast receivers for glutamate on nerve and pigment cells; he says the brain adds more of them when its internal light signal fails. |
| AMPK | The cell's fuel gauge, which he says infrared from the sun switches on. |
| Amyloidosis | Abnormal protein deposits in organs; one of the later costs he attributes to statins. |
| Anaphylaxis | A severe allergic reaction, which he frames as the body blowing a fuse. |
| Angular momentum | The quantity of spin or rotation, which cannot simply appear or vanish; one of the two pillars of his thesis. |
| Antenna gain | How sensitively the skin picks up light and field signals; he says humans shed fur to raise it. |
| Antinutrients | Plant compounds said to block mineral uptake, which he recasts as largely a deuterium problem. |
| ApoB | The main protein on LDL particles; to him a repair patch, not a villain. |
| APOE4 | A gene variant linked to Alzheimer's; to him its carriers depend on DHA rather than cholesterol to guard the brain. |
| Arachidonic acid | An omega-6 fat that makes inflammatory signals, which he says keep running when DHA signals are jammed. |
| Archaea | Ancient single-celled microbes, one of the two partners he says merged to form complex cells, which tolerated deuterium. |
| Area postrema | A patch on the floor of the fourth ventricle with no blood–brain barrier, where he says gut deuterium enters the brain. |
| Ash-leaf spots | Pale, leaf-shaped skin patches seen in tuberous sclerosis; he calls them "dead pixels.". |
| Astaxanthin | The red pigment of salmon and krill; he accepts it only as food and describes it as membrane "rebar" and an internal blue-blocker. |
| Astronauts on Earth (foreign astronaut) | A person living indoors, ungrounded, high up or flying often, cut off from sun, ground and field as if in orbit. |
| Atavism | A throwback to an older, more primitive way of living; how he frames a cell falling back on fermentation. |
| Atavistic retreat | Tissues reverting to older, simpler, looser designs after the chromosome 2 fuse blows. |
| Ataxia | Loss of coordination; another later cost he attributes to statins. |
| Atmospheric tanning | His term for a storm lowering the upper atmosphere and scattering the ultraviolet the skin's pigment system needs. |
| Atomic impurity / rogue dopant | A foreign compound (drug, supplement or toxin) acting like a stray atom in a chip that traps electrons. |
| ATP / ATP synthase | The cell's energy currency / the rotary motor in mitochondria that makes it, spinning near 9,000 RPM in his figure. |
| Atrial flutter | A fast, regular rhythm in the heart's upper chambers that he ties to a deuterium-dulled vagus. |
| Back door to glycogen (Moody Method) | Dr. G. Moody's pre-insulin idea that movement, heat and cold can move sugar into storage without insulin; Kruse reads it as chewing, sun and cold. |
| Ballast (deuterium) | The small amount of heavy hydrogen he says a vortex needs to hold its spin, which is why he does not aim for zero. |
| Bandgap | The energy step that lets a protein act as a semiconductor; he says it narrows when structured water collapses. |
| Barrett's esophagus | A change in the lining of the lower gullet after long reflux; he reads it as retreat to a heavier, older state. |
| Basal ganglia | Deep brain structures near the lateral ventricles that he says collect backwashed deuterium when the brain's drain fails. |
| Basalt | Dark, magnetite-rich volcanic rock that he says holds a flat field, insulates against electrical noise and is ideal to ground on. |
| Base chain / layer two | His Bitcoin analogy: DNA is the base chain; daily clock-timed chemical tagging is a layer two that adjusts genes without rewriting them. |
| BCL11A | The gene that steers the switch from fetal to adult hemoglobin. |
| BDNF | The brain's growth and repair signal, which he says needs light and a burst of reactive oxygen to switch on. |
| Beeswax candle test | Burning a rated pure-beeswax candle at home; burning faster or slower than rated is his readout of local field quality. |
| Beta-endorphin | The body's own opioid, cut from POMC in sunlit skin; he says it makes sun feel rewarding and eases pain. |
| Bh / Bz | The horizontal part of the local field (Bh, parallel to the ground) / the vertical part (Bz, diving into it). |
| Bicarb hack | Oral baking soda used to copy and restart the bicarbonate flush and draw deuterium out. |
| Bicarbonate blanket | Volcanic CO₂ dissolving into seawater during a field decline, changing the water's electrical properties. |
| Bicuspid aortic valve (BAV) | An aortic valve born with two leaflets instead of three; he calls it a "vortex destruction event" that fires blood off-centre. |
| Biofilm | A protective slime layer microbes build; he says it traps deuterium. |
| Biological Nazaré | Crista tips focusing a proton wave the way Portugal's Nazaré canyon focuses ocean swells, driving ATP synthase. |
| Biological reflexivity | George Soros's idea, borrowed: every drug or tracked biomarker changes the biology it targets and can undermine itself. |
| Biologically closed electric circuits | Björn Nordenström's idea of a second, electrical circulation running through blood vessels and the fluid between cells. |
| Biophysical degausser | His name for high-dose insulin, which he says strips cell membranes of their charge. |
| Blepharospasm | Eyelid spasm; to him an early warning that deuterium and heavy metals have reached critical density in the brainstem. |
| Blood–brain barrier | The lining that controls what passes from blood into the brain; to him an isotope filter. |
| BMI paradox | Heavier people surviving acute stress that kills the very lean, because their fat buffers the surge. |
| Bohr effect | CO₂ making blood slightly more acidic so hemoglobin releases oxygen to tissues. |
| Bone conduction | Sound traveling through the skull bones rather than through the air. |
| Braak hypothesis | The idea that Parkinson's begins in the gut and travels to the brain; he supplies a deuterium mechanism. |
| Bristol type 4 | A smooth, well-formed stool; his sign that the morning bicarbonate flush is working. |
| Bruxism | Clenching or grinding the teeth; he treats it and tinnitus as one disease. |
| Buoyancy Check | His home test: if a child's sensory overload eases in a bath or pool, water pressure is compensating for deuterium. |
| Burr cells / rouleaux | Spiky red cells that have lost membrane charge / red cells stacked like coins, which he reads as magnetic collapse. |
| Bus bar | His electrician's term for the junctions linking mitochondria into one shared power network, so failure spreads body-wide. |
| Buteyko breathing | Deliberately breathing less to raise CO₂ in the blood. |
| Butyrylcholinesterase | A blood enzyme reported low in babies who died of SIDS; he reads it as a failing arousal system. |
| C3 / C4 plants | Plants that favor carbon-12 (wheat, rice, temperate vegetables) / plants that load carbon-13 and deuterium (maize, cane). |
| Café-au-lait spots | Flat, light-brown birthmarks common in neurofibromatosis; he calls them overcharged melanin "sinks.". |
| Capacitor | Something that stores and releases electrical charge; his model for membranes, structured water and body fat. |
| Capsaicin | The compound that makes chili peppers hot; to him a "thermal reset" for the body's exhaust. |
| CAR-T | Engineered immune cells used against cancer; he reads their fast effect on glioblastoma as a short-lived deuterium purge. |
| Carbonic anhydrase | An enzyme that makes bicarbonate, which he says carries deuterium out of the pancreas. |
| Cardiac operator / input operator | His roles for the right vagus (steadying the heart) and the left vagus (monitoring gut and liver deuterium load). |
| Cardiolipin | A cone-shaped fat found almost only in the inner mitochondrial membrane, which he calls its insulating sleeve. |
| Carrington Event | The huge solar storm of 1859; his benchmark extreme storm and the start of the current field-decline era. |
| Catecholamines | Dopamine, noradrenaline and adrenaline; he says melanin is broken back down into them when it can't be maintained. |
| CD₃ (deuterated methyl) | A methyl tag in which all three hydrogens are deuterium, the heaviest form he says the clock must swap out at night. |
| Cell danger response | Robert Naviaux's term for a cell's defensive shutdown mode. |
| Cell voltage (−70 vs −15 to −30 mV) | The charge across a cell membrane: about −70 millivolts when healthy, falling to −15 to −30 millivolts as cells fail or ferment. |
| Central American Volcanic Arc | The chain of volcanoes from Guatemala to Costa Rica, which he rates the ideal stretch to live, with El Salvador its densest part. |
| Central sleep apnea | Pauses in breathing during sleep because the brain stops sending the signal, rather than a blocked airway. |
| Centrifugal fractionation | Body movement spinning heavy water away from light water in the brain and spinal fluid. |
| Cerebral aqueduct | The narrow channel carrying spinal fluid from the third to the fourth ventricle, which he calls a fractionation column. |
| Cerebral salt wasting | Kidneys dumping sodium after brain injury; he reads it as a survival gear that drags deuterium out of the spinal fluid. |
| Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) | The clear fluid bathing the brain and spinal cord. |
| CFTR | The faulty gene in cystic fibrosis; in his model the master valve for the bicarbonate flush. |
| cGAS-STING | A cell alarm triggered by stray DNA that drives inflammation. |
| Chimera / mitochondrial monolith | A person carrying two genetically distinct cell lines / his term for such a person whose cells still share one mitochondrial lineage. |
| Chiral / chirality | Having a "handedness," like left and right hands; chiral molecules, he says, filter electron spin. |
| Chiral chambers | His idea of future spin-shielded pods that control light, water pressure and cold together. |
| Chiral handshake | His name for the merger of an ancient iron–sulfur motor with a fluid cell body that formed complex cells. |
| Choroid plexus | The brain tissue that makes spinal fluid; he says humans use it, with the retina's pigment layer, as birds use the pecten. |
| Chromosome 2 fuse | The leftover telomere loop where two ape chromosomes fused in humans, which he says "blows" under extreme stress or field loss. |
| Chronos / Kairos | Greek words he uses for ticking clock time (DNA as a parts list) versus the right moment (the body clock deciding when genes run). |
| Ciliary ganglion | Small nerve relay behind the eye that he says links light on the iris to the vagus. |
| Cineole | The main aromatic compound of eucalyptus. |
| Circuit breaker (insulin resistance) | His view of insulin resistance as a cell refusing fuel it can't safely burn. |
| Circumventricular organs | Spots around the brain's fluid chambers, such as the area postrema, with no blood–brain barrier, which he says sample the deuterium load. |
| CISS (chiral-induced spin selectivity) | One-handed molecules letting through electrons of only one spin direction; his "spin filter" for electron flow. |
| Clivus (hydraulic pivot) | The sloping bone at the skull base, a notochord remnant, which he says turns the sun's charge into the vertical spiral of spinal fluid. |
| Clock dampener | His term for FOXO3 pausing cell division to slow the cell clock and allow repair. |
| CME (coronal mass ejection) | A burst of charged material thrown off by the Sun that can trigger a geomagnetic storm. |
| CO₂–bicarbonate loop | Carbonated water and retained carbon dioxide building bicarbonate, which he says draws deuterium out. |
| Cognitive haze | His term for the foggy thinking he links to statins starving the thalamus, as in a strong geomagnetic storm. |
| Cognitively lattice locked | Brain water too stiff and deuterium-loaded for clear thinking, which he says drops cognition to a survival mode. |
| Coherence pump | His description of the bird's pecten turning light directly into hormone signals and steady eye fluid. |
| Coherence time | The quiet interval between deuterium strikes; he says buying low-deuterium water buys coherence time. |
| Coherent / coherence | Electron spins moving in step, which he says lets enzymes signal instead of burning energy as heat. |
| Cold thermogenesis | Deliberate cold exposure on bare skin or by immersion, his main override when sun or field is poor. |
| Complex I | The entry point of the mitochondrial energy chain; he calls it a UV-A-driven node. |
| Complex II | A second entry point to the energy chain that takes electrons from fat burning and needs very precise spacing. |
| Complex III / Q-cycle | The chain's middle step; he calls its recycling loop a "deli slicer" shaving light frequencies off food electrons. |
| Complex IV (cytochrome c oxidase) | The last enzyme of the energy chain, where electrons meet oxygen to make deuterium-depleted water; he says it should run on copper-65. |
| Containment breach | His view of inflammation: mitochondria leaking contents the immune system reads as infection. |
| Copper ring / copper coils | Open copper rings (after Lakhovsky) and coils (after Spurling) used as passive antennas and shields under the pillow or around the head or neck. |
| Copper-65 | The heavier natural form of copper, which he says belongs in Complex IV and bone. |
| CoQ10 | An electron carrier in the energy chain whose production statins also block. |
| Corrupt bit | A misfolded protein whose information has turned to noise. |
| Cortisol awakening response | The natural rise in cortisol after waking, which he says morning infrared triggers to start the bicarbonate flush. |
| Cosmic shields | His three protective layers: the interstellar hydrogen filter, the Sun's heliosphere and Earth's geodynamo, which he says are failing together. |
| Costly in time, not energy | His slogan that the body manages a deuterium mass far larger than its daily fuel, so time and rhythm matter more than calories. |
| Counterfeit timestamp | His phrase for phone blue light telling the body clock it is always noon. |
| CPAP | A mask machine that pushes air to keep the airway open in sleep apnea. |
| Cradle cap (infant seborrheic dermatitis) | A scaly infant scalp rash he reads, like jaundice, as an early sign of mitochondrial trouble. |
| Crater Flush | His name for a clinician-run approach built around intravenous methylene blue and vitamin C to restart a stalled stomach. |
| Cristae | The inward folds of the inner mitochondrial membrane, which he treats as transistors. |
| CRPS | Complex regional pain syndrome: severe, lasting burning pain after an injury. |
| CTE | Chronic traumatic encephalopathy, a degenerative brain disease he links to years of indoor, high-CO₂ team training. |
| Current of injury | Robert Becker's term for the small natural electric current that steers tissue repair. |
| Cyclone separator | An industrial device that spins heavy particles out of a moving stream; he says the brain's ventricles are shaped to work as one for heavy isotopes. |
| Cytokine storm | A runaway immune flare, which he sees as a containment failure. |
| Cytoplasm | The cell's fluid body around the nucleus; in his cancer model the place where the disease really lives. |
| Dawn chorus | Birdsong at first light, which he reads as birds flushing the night's deuterium as violet light arrives. |
| Dawn phenomenon (magnetic dawning phenomenon) | The early-morning rise in blood sugar, which he reads as failure to sync with the dawn magnetic shift. |
| De-frag | Shaking deuterium loose from tissue and restoring order, for example with sound, hypnosis or a fork and water jet. |
| Declination | The angle between compass north and true north; he wants it near 0° and calls it a "geographic multiplier.". |
| Degenerative disc disease | Breakdown of the spinal discs; he groups it with PAD, artery hardening and neurodegeneration as one disease. |
| Delay line (neocortex) | His picture of the six-layered cortex holding light signals for a few milliseconds; that delay, to him, is consciousness. |
| Dentin | The layer beneath tooth enamel; to him a liquid-crystal waveguide whose fine tubules sort deuterium out. |
| Dermatome | The strip of skin served by one spinal or cranial nerve branch; he maps vitiligo and melasma onto them. |
| Deuterium (deuteron) | Heavy hydrogen, with an extra neutron and about twice the mass; in his model it jams mitochondrial motors. A deuteron is its nucleus. |
| Deuterium bomb | His name for glucose, because plants pack deuterium into storage sugars. |
| Deuterium centrifuge | His name for gravity and the heart's pressure together pulling heavy water down and out of the brain. |
| Deuterium Ghost | A normal pulse-oximeter reading while tissues are actually starved of oxygen. |
| Deuterium interoception | His term for the felt sense of one's own isotope load, which he says GLP-1 users lose. |
| Deuterium trash can | Protein plaques he says the brain builds on purpose to wall deuterium away from the energy chain. |
| Deuterium-depleted water (DDW) | Water low in heavy hydrogen; healthy mitochondria make it, and he recommends drinking it at roughly 25–105 ppm. |
| Deuterons | The nuclei of deuterium; he says they increasingly carry the positive charge of inflammation. |
| DFF40/CAD | The enzyme that chops DNA into fragments during orderly cell death; its absence in glioblastoma lets tumor cells refuse to die. |
| DHA (magnetic fat) | The long omega-3 fat in seafood, retina and brain, which he calls a light-conducting "magnetic fat.". |
| Diamagnetic | Weakly pushed away by a magnet; his description of singlet oxygen. |
| Diamond / coal | His image for ordered 160 water versus disordered 78 water, drawn from entropy-based gravity papers. |
| Dielectric booster | Rising CO₂ and global greening raising Earth's charge-holding capacity as its magnetic shield weakens. |
| Dielectric constant | How much electric charge a substance can hold and how strongly it buffers electric fields. |
| Dielectric constant (160 vs 78) | How well water stores charge; he scores structured cell water at about 160 and ordinary or disordered water at 78. |
| Dielectric patch kits | His description of ApoB and Lp(a) sealing leaking water at the vessel wall. |
| Dirty burn | Burning glucose in a low-field, high-nnEMF setting, which throws off light flares and leaves deuterium behind. |
| Distal refinery | His name for the skin's layered network of small arteries, which sheds heat and deuterium through cold and sweat. |
| Dopant | An added material that changes how a semiconductor behaves; he applies it to jade in teeth. |
| Doping / dopant | Deliberate impurities that tune a semiconductor; he applies it to the cell's chemical tags and to foreign chemicals that trap electrons. |
| Double strip | His term for pregnancy on or soon after GLP-1 drugs: both draw down the mother's DHA and fat at once. |
| Drusen | Yellow deposits beneath the retina on eye scans; to him a visible sign of a failing melanin shield. |
| Dry biophysics | His label for consciousness theories that ignore the water around microtubules. |
| Dual exhaust | His pairing of the trigeminal drain through Meckel's cave and the vagal drain through the jugular foramen, which he says work as a buddy system. |
| Dual-axis transistor | His description of neuropsin reading both sunlight and Earth's magnetic field. |
| Dumping syndrome | Rapid stomach emptying after vagus surgery; one of the signs he reads as gut organs filling with deuterium. |
| Dyspraxia | Clumsiness in planning and coordinating movement, which he links to heavy inner-ear fluid. |
| Eating mass / harvesting flux | His contrast between living on food energy alone and living on light and magnetic energy captured by the body. |
| EDAR | A gene that builds sweat glands and other skin structures; he calls its cooling loop a route for dumping deuterium through the skin. |
| Elastin | The springy protein that lets arteries recoil; deuterium turns it brittle in his model. |
| Electric and magnetic containment | The two conditions he says mitochondria need to make melatonin; grounding restores only the electric one. |
| Electrochemical therapy | Treating tumors by inserting electrodes and passing a weak direct current, as Nordenström and later Chinese doctors did. |
| Electroculture | Using buried wires, magnets, antennas and stone to shape the soil's electrical and magnetic environment to boost plant growth. |
| Electromagnetic terroir | Gene activity following the local magnetic field and ground currents, as wine follows its soil. |
| Electron-volt (eV) | The unit physicists use for the energy carried by a single photon or electron. |
| Electronic winter | His phrase for modern people chilled at the cellular level after trading the sun's infrared for cold blue light. |
| Elovanoids / neuroprotectin D1 | Healing signals made from DHA that he says deuterium mistunes. |
| Endolymphatic sac | The inner ear's own drain; another exhaust in his model. |
| Endothelial sandpaper | His image of homocysteine scraping the vessel lining in thick, turbulent blood. |
| Energy resistance (Picard) | Martin Picard's idea of how much the cell resists energy flow; health sits in a "just-right" zone. |
| Entrainment | The brain falling into step with an outside rhythm, such as 40 Hz sound, light or vibration. |
| Entropy | Accumulated disorder, which he says telomeres record instead of age. |
| Entropy tax | His name for high blood sugar and insulin as the body's compensation for a deuterium-jammed engine. |
| Ephaptic coupling | Neighboring cells communicating through fields rather than synapses; he says wireless signals jam it. |
| Epigenetic drift | Slow change in the chemical marks on DNA, which he treats as a passenger of field damage. |
| Equatorial bulge | The planet's widening at the equator from its spin, which makes gravity slightly weaker there; to him, the reason heavy hydrogen is cheapest to clear in the tropics. |
| Error rate (declination) | Declination read as the mismatch between Earth's spin and its magnetic dynamo; the wider it is, the worse body rhythms and spins mesh. |
| Exclusion-zone (EZ) water / structured water | Gerald Pollack's ordered, charge-holding water layer that forms against surfaces; his "160" state. |
| Excursion / reversal (geomagnetic) | An aborted pole reversal where the field weakens and wanders for up to ~2,000 years / a full pole swap; he says excursions select survivors, reversals cause extinctions. |
| Fab plant | His image of the mother filtering deuterium and processing nutrients like vitamin K for the baby. |
| Fab plant (genome as) | The genome seen as a factory that builds to order, with light and melanin, not genes, setting the design. |
| FADH₂ | An electron carrier that enters the energy chain after Complex I; fat burning routes more electrons through it. |
| Fake battery / fake spark | His labels for Melanotan (a tan without the matching voltage) and outside nicotine (a jolt to a stalled system). |
| Faraday cage | An enclosure that blocks fields; he says buildings act as one, cutting people off from sun and field. |
| Fascia | The connective sheets wrapping muscles, which he says stiffen and stop carrying light and charge when muscle is built under LEDs. |
| FCHO1 | A gene T cells need to pull their receptors inside; he cites a girl born without it as the extreme case of a stalled immune system. |
| Femtosecond | A millionth of a billionth of a second; his measure of how fast light alters biology before nerves react. |
| Ferredoxin | A small iron–sulfur protein he treats as a relic of life's first electron motor, older than the genetic code. |
| Ferritin | The iron-storage protein; he reads high blood ferritin as a sign of a weak local field and poor light and grounding. |
| Ferrous / ferric iron (Fe²⁺ / Fe³⁺) | The working, magnetically useful form of iron / its oxidized, "rusted" form. |
| Fetal overnutrition | The usual idea that a mother's excess food overfeeds the fetus; he rejects it in favor of a deuterium-sink view. |
| Fibrillin | An elastic protein that, with elastin, lines arteries; he says melanin-carrying cells follow it to the choroid plexus. |
| FICZ / AhR | A compound UV-A makes from tryptophan in skin / the aryl hydrocarbon receptor it switches on, which he treats as a light antenna. |
| Fine-structure constant (about 1/137) | The number that sets how strongly light grips matter; he says structured water tightens that grip and deuterium loosens it. |
| Flash-wash | His term for sugar's quick burst of light protons used to clear deuterium in an emergency; fat is the long-game fuel. |
| Flavins | Vitamin B2-based molecules that respond directly to blue light. |
| Follicular fluid | The fluid surrounding a developing egg; he says gut deuterium can be sent into it. |
| Four-phase sun day | His daily sequence: sunrise red/infrared, mid-morning UV-A, brief midday UV-B, sunset red/infrared. |
| Fourth phase | Gerald Pollack's name for exclusion-zone water, which Kruse says cytochrome c oxidase forges. |
| Fourth ventricle | A fluid space in the brainstem where he says the body filters deuterium. |
| FOXO3 | A gene common in very long-lived people that can pause cell division to protect the genome. |
| Fraunhofer lines | Narrow dark gaps in the sun's spectrum, which he reads as a solar "barcode". |
| From the pharmacy to the foundry | His slogan for shaping the body's physical hardware rather than adding drugs. |
| Frosted lens | His image of heavy water under the retina scattering light before melanin can convert it. |
| GABA | The brain's main calming messenger, which he says light at night lowers. |
| Galvanic corrosion | Metal slowly dissolving when an unwanted current runs through it; boats are grounded to stop it, his picture of why people should ground. |
| Gamma-secretase | The enzyme that processes amyloid's parent protein in the brain and a growth signal in hair follicles. |
| Gas discharge imaging | Photographing the faint glow around hair or nails in an electric field, used by him to gauge how readily the body gives off electrons. |
| Gastrocolic reflex | The reflex that empties the colon after a meal; he stresses its morning timing. |
| Gastroparesis | A stalled stomach that empties too slowly. |
| GDF15 / mitokine storm | A stress signal from struggling mitochondria / a surge of it, which he links to fatigue and faster aging. |
| Genomic instability | DNA that keeps breaking and mutating; to him a sign that the heavy, deuterium-loaded cell fluid can no longer vibrate in step, not a primary gene fault. |
| Geodynamo | The churning molten iron in Earth's core that generates the planet's magnetic field. |
| Geological ark | His name for El Salvador, whose volcanic, iron-rich geology he says keeps the field flat and stable. |
| Geomagnetic jerk | A sudden lurch in how fast the field is changing, which he says can tilt a local field within a year. |
| Geometric zombies | His name for cancer cells that can no longer die on schedule and shrug off radiation and chemotherapy. |
| GLP-1 drugs | Weight-loss injections such as semaglutide, which he says force a crude deuterium purge and damage gut and stem cells. |
| Glucagon gene network | A gene cluster on chromosome 2, switched on at sunrise, that he says runs the bicarbonate flush and makes GLP-1. |
| GLUT4 | The transporter that lets insulin move sugar into muscle and fat; birds lack it, which he cites as built-in insulin resistance. |
| Glutamate | The brain's main excitatory ("go") messenger, which he says also sets melanin levels. |
| Glutathione | The cell's main internal antioxidant, which he says acetaminophen and isolated ascorbic acid drain. |
| Glycerol-3-phosphate shuttle | A back-door route carrying light hydrogen into mitochondria, which he says runs during insulin resistance and leaves deuterium behind. |
| Glycolysis | Breaking down sugar without mitochondria (fermentation). |
| Glymphatic backlog | His name for waking around 2 a.m. when the brain's overnight deuterium flush meets a blocked exhaust and cortisol forces a restart. |
| Glymphatic system | The brain's waste-clearing fluid flow, most active in deep sleep. |
| GnRH | The brain hormone whose pulses start each reproductive cycle; he says they shut down when leptin falls. |
| Gravity well (heart's) | His image for the heart's pull that spins deuterium out into the blood, away from mitochondria. |
| Great Oxidation Event | The period about 2.4 billion years ago when oxygen first built up in Earth's air. |
| Grid cells / place cells | Brain cells that map where you are, which he says depend on field-anchored structured water. |
| Ground fault (SAA) | His description of the South Atlantic Anomaly as a break in the ocean's electrical circuit that stalls the Atlantic current. |
| Grounding (earthing) | Bare skin contact with earth, sand, rock or sea, or a low-resistance indoor connection, letting the body share the earth's charge. |
| H lineage | His own northern European haplogroup, whose "leaky" mitochondria he says shed heat rather than couple tightly. |
| Hantavirus | A virus family; he reads the Andes strain's person-to-person spread as an adaptation to a degraded environment. |
| Haplogroup | A maternal lineage traced through mitochondrial DNA (e.g. L1, H, D5b1, F); he calls it your blueprint, to be matched to location. |
| Hardware-dominant / software-dominant | His labels for animals that rely on external gear such as horns and hide (the rhino) versus a large processing brain (the elephant). |
| HbA1c | The standard blood test averaging sugar over about three months; he thinks deuterium load predicts damage better. |
| Heavy octave | His name for deuterium, since an octave doubles a frequency and deuterium is double the mass of ordinary hydrogen. |
| Heavy water trap | A weak field letting light hydrogen escape to space so the planet's remaining water grows richer in deuterium. |
| Heme | The iron-holding core of hemoglobin and the cytochromes, which he calls the body's magnetic antenna. |
| Heme clock | Light-timed heme production that switches off the liver's sugar output at night. |
| Henry's law | The rule that a liquid holds more dissolved gas under higher pressure; he cites it for diving squeezing deuterium out. |
| Heteroplasmy | The share of damaged mitochondrial DNA in a cell. |
| HFpEF | Heart failure with preserved pumping; in his model the end of a fast, overloaded rhythm. |
| Hidradenitis suppurativa | Painful tunnels under the skin of the armpit and groin; he links it to Alzheimer's through a shared enzyme. |
| Home Frequency | His functional "ancestry test": the place where your body switches on under strong sun and runs freely when grounded on volcanic soil. |
| Homocysteine | An amino-acid by-product he reads as a gauge of blood "sludge," deuterium load and heart risk. |
| Hot flashes (as exhaust) | Menopausal heat surges, which he reads as an exhaust vent rather than a broken thermostat. |
| HRV (heart-rate variability) | Beat-to-beat variation in heart timing; deep-sleep HRV is his main progress gauge, rigid when lattice-locked and flexible when free. |
| Human GPS | His three-axis map of the body: the sphenoid as the clock-setting X-axis, the notochord line from skull base to sacrum as the draining Y-axis, and the heart's twist as the Z-axis. |
| Hunger mirror | Hunger driven by a deuterium-jammed energy motor that the brain misreads as famine. |
| Hyaluronic acid | The slippery gel between tissue layers, which he says deuterium stiffens so layers grind. |
| Hydraulic failure | His umbrella term for reflux, polyps and colon cancer as one backed-up bicarbonate flush. |
| Hydrocephalus | Fluid building up in the brain's chambers; to him a stalled exit for spinal fluid. |
| Hydroxyapatite | The calcium mineral of bone, teeth and ivory. |
| Hyper-local magnetic excursion | Wi-Fi, smart meters and other wireless devices recreating, around one person, the effect of a planetary field collapse. |
| Hyperacusis | Sound sensitivity; he reads it as a warning the hearing system is close to collapse. |
| Hyperbaric oxygen | Breathing extra oxygen under pressure; he prefers plain pressure from diving or immersion. |
| Hyperfine coupling | The link between an electron's spin and the spins of nearby atomic nuclei, which he says radio-frequency fields disrupt. |
| Hyperfine noise trap | Extra nuclear-spin "noise" (for example from deuterium) that makes a radical-pair magnetic sensor less sensitive to weak fields. |
| Hypertonic saline | Extra-salty saline; he says it raises the dielectric constant of the body's water. |
| Hypocretin | A brain signal that keeps us awake and alert; he says it is throttled when deuterium load is high, to cut the brain's heat cost. |
| Hypomethylation | Too few methyl tags on DNA; he says deuterium jams the tagging enzymes and erases the timing that builds a three-leaflet valve. |
| HypoxyStat | A drug that makes hemoglobin hold oxygen more tightly; he cites mouse work where it reversed high blood sugar. |
| Ice-nucleating proteins | Proteins that seed ice, and so rain, at relatively warm temperatures; he cites fungi that release them. |
| IGRF | The International Geomagnetic Reference Field, a standard global field model he says smooths away local changes. |
| Impedance matching | Tuning a circuit to its power source so little energy is lost; his view of maternal lineages as tuned to the field and sunlight of where they settled. |
| Inclination (dip) | The angle at which field lines dip into the ground; he wants 0–45°, meaning fairly flat lines. |
| Induction zone | An active fault zone (such as New Madrid) that gives off electromagnetic signals under stress and makes the local field spiky. |
| Inertial mass | Sheer heaviness that resists being moved; how he treats misplaced deuterium in cells and fluids. |
| Infrasound | Sound below the range of human hearing, which elephants use and which he says shakes their body water. |
| Ink without the book | His image for a vitamin D pill: the product without the light-run code that regulates it. |
| Inner mitochondrial membrane | The folded inner wall of the mitochondrion, where the energy chain sits and voltage is held; its charge is his key health variable. |
| Input / exhaust | Field lines gathering toward the north ("input") and spreading apart over the south ("exhaust"), which he says leaves the Southern Hemisphere more exposed. |
| Insulin resistance | Cells resisting insulin; to him a protective shield, not a defect. |
| Insulin-resistant type 1s | Type 1 diabetics whose cells are so loaded with heavy water that injected insulin no longer works well. |
| Intense world | The idea that the autistic brain turns its gain up and floods itself with raw input. |
| Interior tan | Melanin and structured water deep inside the body, which he says matter more than skin tan. |
| Internal rigor mortis | Deuterium pulling the far-from-balance ATP synthase motor toward a settled, lifeless state. |
| Intersystem crossing | A forced spin flip in the energy chain that turns safe triplet oxygen into singlet oxygen. |
| Intrinsic cardiac nervous system | The heart's own "little brain" of about 40,000 neurons, which he says senses blood thickness and deuterium. |
| Intussusception | The bowel telescoping into itself; he calls it a deuterium motor stall. |
| Ion cyclotron resonance | Liboff and Blackman's idea that an AC field riding on Earth's field can match calcium's natural frequency and push it out of place. |
| Isotope centrifuge | The many steps of glycolysis and the Krebs cycle sorting light hydrogen from deuterium. |
| Isotope jam (beta-cell fatigue) | His reading of type 2 diabetes: beta cells full of insulin but too deuterium-jammed to release it. |
| Isotope mass gate | Complex IV making water that excludes deuterium. |
| Isotopic back-pressure | Taking in more deuterium and tritium from the air just as the body's ability to clear them fails. |
| Isotopic backwash | The bicarbonate flush stalling and pushing heavy water back into the gut lining. |
| Isotopic dead zone | A food web so loaded with heavy hydrogen that birds can no longer live on it. |
| Isotopic debt | Deuterium quietly building up while pills make lab values look good, until the body "goes bankrupt.". |
